Feeds the snapshot's `tasksAtTailStart` (via `Instance.reconstructActiveTasks`) and `eventArchive.ts`'s lazy-page `task_completion` injection. `task_completion` is never a real wire event — the tail/live path still synthesizes it client-side in `wsRouter.js`; this recovers the below-tail batches those paths can't see.
- **src/instances.ts `Instance.reconstructActiveTasks(beforeSeq)`** (async) — the snapshot's `tasksAtTailStart` seed. Runs the cheap ring-only `reconstructTasks` first; only when it reports `hadOrphanUpdate` (and the ring has trimmed, and a `sessionId` exists) does it call `buildArchive` (same combine as `eventArchive.ts`'s lazy paging: `archive.events.slice(0, cut)` ++ ring events below `beforeSeq`) and re-run `reconstructTasks`, so a batch whose `TaskCreate` was evicted entirely below the ring is recovered without a deep scroll. A batch that completed inside the archive still yields `[]` (no resurrection).
- **src/sessionEdit.ts** — Atomic destructive jsonl edits: `truncateSessionAtUserMessage` (tmp → fsync → rename, appends fresh metadata at the new leaf), `forkSessionAtUserMessage` (copies prefix into a new `<sid>.jsonl` with `sessionId` rewritten on every line). Both return `droppedText` for composer prefill.
- **src/sessionPrune.ts** — **Prune**: a mechanical, zero-token context compaction. `analyzeSessionForPrune({cwd,sessionId})` returns per-turn, per-category prunable-token counts; `pruneSessionToNewId({cwd,sessionId,cutTurnIndex|keepLatestTurns,pruneThinking,inputMode,mode})` writes a stubbed copy under a fresh `sessionId` (same atomic tmp→rename + `sessionId`-rewrite + `writeSessionMetadata` convention as `sessionEdit.ts`) and returns `{newSessionId, turnCount, cutTurnIndex, saved}`. **Cut range: `0…turnCount` inclusive** — `turnCount` is a full prune, newest turn included; only `turnCount+1` and above 400, and a zero-turn session is refused outright. The two cut arguments are **XOR** (supplying both, or neither, throws — no silent precedence): `keepLatestTurns` resolves to `Math.max(0, turnCount - keepLatestTurns)` **inside** the transform, which is the only point past `Instance.pruneSession`'s subprocess kill — a caller that pre-read the count via `analyzeSessionForPrune` could have a turn land in between and silently keep one turn too many. A user prompt's own text and every `text` block survive a full cut untouched (a pure-user-prompt line's `content` is a string, so it is not `touchable`), and the unresolved-`tool_use` thinking exemption is message-keyed and cut-independent, so an interrupted newest turn keeps its signed thinking. Both passes route every block through ONE `pruneBlock()` helper, so the dialog's savings preview cannot drift from what the transform does — which is also why the exempt-tool check (`isPruneExemptTool`, invariant 6 below) lives **inside** `pruneBlock` rather than in either caller's loop; `pruneBlock` returns the original block whenever the stub would be *larger*, so pruning never inflates the context. Turn boundaries come from `isPureUserPromptLine` (transcript.ts) — the same index space rewind/fork use — which is what makes the slider's turn-snapping structural rather than cosmetic: the cut can never land between a `tool_use` and its `tool_result`. `Instance.pruneSession()` (instances.ts) wraps it in the `rewindToUserMessage` skeleton (`_mutating` guard → idle-only → `kill` → transform → `_wipeForResume` → `launch({resume:newSid})`) and then reuses `carryMarkersAcrossRenewal(oldSid)` from the renewal path to carry temp/conducted/title/backend and archive the abandoned id. **Only the BACKING id rotates — the instanceId and the public `sessionId` are both preserved**, so the idle-wake graph, overage timers, the renew controller, every `?caller=<instanceId>` MCP handle and every id a conductor holds stay valid with no migration. The rotation is recorded as a `prune` segment BEFORE `launch()` (prune is server-minted, so it is the one rotation that can be durable before first use) and the fail-safe catch calls `revertRotation`, so a throw inside `launch()` can never leave a segment the process never ran. It sets a one-shot `_skipUsageSeed` consumed in `loadHistory`, because the jsonl's newest assistant `usage` still reports the PRE-prune size. On a mid-transform throw it fails **safe**: the subprocess is already dead by then, so the catch respawns against the ORIGINAL backing id (always possible — Prune only ever writes a new file) and rethrows the real cause, rather than leaving the instance wedged with no proc and no respawn. `Instance.prompt()` refuses with 409 while `_mutating` is set, closing the sliver between a caller's idle check and the kill in which a prompt would be written to stdin and its partial tail folded into the rewritten file — rewind gets the same protection for free, and `Instance.forkAtUserMessage()` (instances.ts, sitting alongside its two rewrite siblings) sets the same flag around `forkSessionAtUserMessage` — fork never kills its source subprocess, so `!this.proc` does not cover it at all; the flag is scoped to the READ, since once the copy is on disk a prompt to the source cannot affect it. Fork also refuses (409) when `_mutating` is already set, rather than reading a jsonl a concurrent rewind/prune is rewriting. Fork takes no `_suppressTempDelete`, unlike its two siblings: they kill their source and respawn it, so the temp archive would fire on a session about to come back, whereas fork never kills anything — a source exiting mid-fork is a REAL exit and must archive. Sound because the two footprints are disjoint: the archive writes the sub-agent dir and the temp/archived marker stores, all keyed on the SOURCE id, while the fork reads the source jsonl and writes only the new id's jsonl + metadata. `_archiveTempSession` itself deletes no jsonl. `createArgs` carries `temp` explicitly, so the child is a full temp session (marked by its own `spawn()`, archived on its own exit); create()'s sidecar recovery would not supply it, since forking writes no temp marker for the new id. The fork ROUTE (`routes.ts`) only parses `userMessageIndex` and spawns the `createArgs` the method hands back — every guard, the flag claim/release and the derivation of those respawn arguments belong to the instance, which is what all three of them are read off. Not a new failure class for callers, which already tolerate the `not running` throw from the same window. Unlike `renew_session` it never calls `prompt()` — a pruned session resumes **idle**.
  - **Harness-state invariants (derived by probing the real CLI — treat as spec).** The CLI rebuilds its read-before-edit cache (`readFileState`) by REPLAYING THE TRANSCRIPT on resume, reading `input.file_path` off every Read/Write/Edit `tool_use` and pairing it with the matching `tool_result`. So: **(1)** never rewrite a `tool_use.input` key set — only string *values* are edited and path keys are skipped at any depth; replacing an input with a marker object drops `file_path` and every later Edit fails with "File has not been read yet". **(2)** never touch `toolUseResult` — it is a disk-only sidecar (pruning it saves zero *context*) and its ABSENCE is the CLI's discriminator for "this is a human user turn". **(3)** `tool_result.content` stays a string **except for pruned Read/Write results, which use a block array deliberately**: the CLI only reconstructs a `readFileState` entry when `typeof content === "string"`, so the array form is what makes the read-before-edit guard RE-ARM once the file's content has left the context. Normalizing (3) back to a string would look like a cleanup and would silently re-break that guarantee — the model would be allowed to edit a file it can only "remember", reconstructing an `old_string` that may not match or may match the wrong occurrence. Switchable via `PRUNE_STUB_AS_BLOCKS` (default `true`). An Edit-seeded cache entry is out of reach either way: the CLI re-reads that path from DISK using only `input.file_path` and a non-error result. The reconstruction pass branches on exactly three tool names — **Read, Write, Edit** — so `SEEDING_TOOLS` is `{Read, Write}` (Edit's result content is irrelevant to it). `NotebookEdit` is a defined tool-name constant that the pass never references: a notebook result cannot seed the cache, and since nothing seeds it from a transcript, a notebook edit already needs a live Read after ANY resume — pruning neither causes nor worsens that. The guard is only *enforced* for a fixed model set (haiku-4-5, sonnet-4-5/4-0, opus ≤4-6, 3-x) — which is why `tests/prune.real.test.mjs` pins `claude-haiku-4-5`; on Opus 5 / Sonnet 5 it would pass vacuously. **(3a)** Truncate mode cuts with `sliceCodePoints`, which backs off one unit when the cut would land inside a surrogate pair — `slice` counts UTF-16 code units, and a lone high surrogate has no valid UTF-8 encoding, so a naive cut writes unencodable content into a jsonl whose whole contract is that it resumes cleanly. Minimal mode replaces whole values and is immune. **(4)** No entry and no block is ever removed, which is what keeps the `parentUuid` chain and the `tool_use`/`tool_result` pairing invariant safe with zero relinking. **(5)** Sidechain entries are never pruned and never counted in the estimate; the sibling sub-agent directory (`<encoded-cwd>/<sid>/subagents/`) is COPIED to the new sessionId, without which every sidechain silently vanishes from the pruned session's transcript view (`loadSubAgentTranscript` keys off the sessionId — `forkSessionAtUserMessage` has this same gap today). **(6)** Some tools are EXEMPT by name (`isPruneExemptTool`): their `tool_use` and the `tool_result` answering it are copied verbatim in every mode, at any cut. Two classes — **`AskUserQuestion`**, because the question card is rebuilt entirely from `input.questions` on replay (`transcript.ts`) and the answer is recovered by *string-matching* the question text and option labels against the following `user_echo` (`public/userQuestionAnswers.js` → `isUserQuestionAnswerText`/`parseUserQuestionAnswers`, driven from `public/conversation.js`), so squeezing either side leaves an unreadable question and silently drops the answer off the card; and **core conductor MCP calls** (`mcp__code-conductor__<tool>`), the orchestration record of what was spawned, approved, merged, filed. The MCP half is exempt by NAMESPACE so a core tool added later is exempt by default, minus a hardcoded two-entry denylist `PRUNABLE_CONDUCTOR_MCP_TOOLS` = {`project_read`, `project_bash`} whose results are bulk file/command output — precisely what Prune exists to shed. **Not configurable**: which class a tool falls in is a property of the tool, not a user preference. Matching the prefix alone is not enough: what remains after it must ALSO contain no further `__`, since a plugin-forwarded tool carries one (`mcp__code-conductor__code-kanban__file_task`, `mcp__code-conductor__acme-tools__run`) and stays prunable. The separator tested is `__` and not `_`, which keeps a core tool whose own name contains a single underscore (`spawn_instance`, `merge_worktree`) on the exempt side; testing for `__` rather than for the first-party `code-*` plugin naming habit is deliberate, so a third-party plugin id classifies the same way. An orphaned `tool_result` (no `tool_use` in context ⇒ no name) stays prunable. Thinking blocks keep their `signature` when their text is stubbed: an *unsigned* thinking block survives the CLI's own other-model strip pass (which only removes SIGNED blocks) and would then reach the API unsigned; a stale signature was measured to provoke no rejection, and the CLI has a strip-all-and-retry recovery path regardless.
- **src/parser.ts** — stream-json line → UI event normalization. Delta merging by `(msgId, blockIdx)`, `thinking_redacted` emission, `parentToolUseId` routing, structured `user_question` / `plan_request` events (the latter carrying any `planFilePath`/`planPath` the tool input named — `planFile.ts` fills the rest). Also owns `QuiescenceScan` — the "nothing mid-stream, every tool returned" state machine — used by BOTH the snapshot/paging boundary snap (over an event array) and `Instance`'s live deferred-interrupt fire (over the event stream, see protocol.md → Two-tier interrupt).

  **Its two consumers ask different questions of it, which is why it publishes two things**. Paging asks "is state empty AT index i", evaluated *before* applying event i. The live abort asks "has a block boundary been crossed SINCE I armed" — unanswerable from an empty-state reading, because on a stream whose closes never arrive the event that retires a stale block is the same event that opens the next one. So the class carries `boundarySeq`, a monotonic count of retirements, and `Instance` snapshots it into `_interruptArmSeq` at arm time.
  A block is retired by its own close, by a turn boundary, or by **progression** — a different `${msgId}:${blockIdx}` key appearing. Progression MARKS the stale block rather than deleting it: a never-closed block is unfinished *permanently*, so a consumer that must render whole blocks is right to keep counting it — deleting instead hands paging extra cuts wherever a stale block is followed by one that closes normally, ending the preceding page on a dangling block, which is what the snap exists to prevent. With marking, quiescent cut indices are identical by construction (pinned in `tests/quiescent-paging.test.mjs`, including the stale-then-normally-closing case). Progression reads ONLY events carrying both a `msgId` and a numeric `blockIdx` — a candidate that read it from any event fired at index 35 of `tests/fixtures/trace-quiescent-boundary.jsonl` while a Bash tool ran until index 47.
  **Tool spans are deliberately NOT progressed** — only a matching `tool_result` or a turn boundary discharges one. Measured over 68,431 real outer tool spans across the identity backend and six substitution backends: a msgId change with a span open occurs 85 times and all 85 spans are dangling (0 late-resolving), and orphan `tool_result` ids number 0 — so progression there would buy an unattested class at the cost of discarding live tool work. If that class is ever seen in the wild, re-run the orphan-`tool_result` scan rather than re-deriving this.
- **src/systems/** — the **System seam**: every project-scoped operation (git, project-tree file I/O, shell commands in a project dir) goes through a `System` handle instead of `node:fs`/`spawn` directly. **Two implementations**: the in-process `local` built-in, and `ProviderSystem`, which reaches a system by speaking a documented NDJSON protocol to a provider process ([docs/systems-protocol.md](systems-protocol.md)). No TRANSPORT is shipped — cc defines the provider contract and launches a provider, it does not implement SSH or docker: the provider command is the user's, stored as argv on the registry row (`launch`). A project on a system with no such command, or on an id with no row at all, is REFUSED by name at resolution rather than resolved local.
- **src/systems/fuse/** — the **FUSE-union chroot**, MANDATORY for a remote-backed worker and refused by name where it cannot mount. The one exemption is structural, not configurable: `LauncherLike.inProcess`, the test-only launcher that runs the CLI inside cc's own process and so has no subprocess to put in a mount namespace.  A remote-backed worker's CLI runs inside a private mount namespace whose root is a union filesystem, so the FILESYSTEM decides which bytes appear at a path instead of a pull/push hook. Engaged for every session on a non-local project.
  - `union.c` + `policy.h` — the daemon. `union.c.sha256` pins **both** sources, one row each.

    `union.c.sha256` is a **deliberate-edit latch**, not a build input: the build already content-addresses the daemon binary on **both sources plus flags** (`ensureUnionBinary` in `src/systems/fuse/build.ts` hashes `union.c` AND `policy.h`), so the pin's job is **disclosure** — editing a pinned source without regenerating the pin in the SAME COMMIT is the undisclosed drift that `A16` in `tests/fuse-lifecycle.test.mjs` catches. It carries exactly **two rows**, `union.c` and `policy.h`, in standard `sha256sum` output format (`<digest><whitespace><name>`).

    `A16` resolves each row's name against `src/systems/fuse/` regardless of the test's cwd, hashes each named file, and asserts the row COUNT and both NAMES — so a source added to the build (`UNION_SOURCE` / `POLICY_SOURCE` / `build.ts`) without a pin row is a test failure, and regenerating with one file alone fails the row-count assertion.

    The rows carry the **bare** file names, so manual `sha256sum` operations resolve them against the CURRENT DIRECTORY — every pin command runs from inside `src/systems/fuse/`:

        cd src/systems/fuse
        sha256sum -c union.c.sha256              # verify; both rows must print OK

    REGENERATE, in the same commit as the edit that invalidates it, by naming BOTH files and never a path — from the repo root, `sha256sum src/systems/fuse/union.c …` embeds the path in each row's NAME, which `sha256sum -c` then cannot resolve and `A16` rejects:

        cd src/systems/fuse
        sha256sum union.c policy.h > union.c.sha256
        sha256sum -c union.c.sha256              # both rows must report OK

    A failing `-c` that prints `No such file or directory` means you are in the wrong directory — it is the checker not finding the files, not a digest mismatch. When a third source joins the daemon build, add its row in the same regeneration (and update `A16`'s row-count and name assertions with it).
    **THE POLICY, AND THERE IS ONE.** There are no selectable routing modes and no measurement knobs. `enum tier { T_FAIL = 0, T_HOST, T_PROJECT, T_HIDE, T_BIND, T_SYNTH }` — and `T_FAIL` at index 0 is the whole of it, because `tier_of` returns index 0 for a path no pin matches, so **an unmatched path is served from neither side to the marked CLI** — a remote-first index with a host fallback would give one path two answers. `route()` has no `default:` arm and no fallback. The table is read through one of **two views** (`enum view`): `VIEW_CLI` is the table as written and only the MARKED CLI resolves against it; `VIEW_HOST` is everyone else's, and in it the `project` pins are struck and the ancestor table is not consulted — so an unmarked caller is served the orchestrator's own filesystem wherever it has something, and a traverse-only overlay node at the cwd components it has not. `host` and `bind` paths are served to marked and unmarked callers alike, and `hide` is subtracted from both views.

    ### The two views: an unmarked caller resolves in `VIEW_HOST`

    **THE OPERATIVE DEFINITION.** *For an unmarked caller the union **subtracts nothing from the orchestrator's own filesystem and adds only what the chroot cannot run without**: the remote tier is struck from its view, and each component of the CLI's cwd is made traversable — by the orchestrator's own directory floored to `--x`, or by a traverse-only node where the orchestrator has none.* It names neither `systemPath` nor `mirrorRoot`, and **every unmarked answer is identical at every geometry** — with the `project` entries struck the pin list an unmarked caller resolves against does not mention `mirrorRoot` at all, and the cwd chain does not vary with it either, because `mirrorRoot` is always an ancestor-or-equal of `systemPath`, which *is* the cwd. That invariance is the property that makes a geometry-dependent unmarked answer unreachable rather than merely fixed; `b38` asserts it across three geometries. The narrower rule it subsumes — *an unmarked process always gets the host entry wherever one exists, whatever the `mirrorRoot`* — **falls out of the view** instead of being tested for: the geometry cannot be consulted because the remote tier is not in the view. The invariant marking protects is narrower than the code first assumed: an unmarked caller must never see **remote file content**, and that is now STRUCTURAL — `tier_of` skips every `project` pin in `VIEW_HOST`, so no unmarked resolution can name the remote, send a control frame or read the mirror. `policy_project_route`'s mark check is retained as defence in depth and is unreachable from an unmarked caller.

    **THE CHAIN'S TRAVERSE-ONLY NODE IS NOT UNMARKED-ONLY, and the definition above is scoped to the unmarked caller only because the FLOOR is.** Where a chain component carries no pin of its own — every component `worktreePathFor` manufactures between a host pin and a non-local worktree — a covering `host` pin wins in `VIEW_CLI` too and shadows the ancestor promotion, so the overlay node answers the marked CLI there as well. Without it the CLI meets `-ENOENT` at *every* absolute path under its cwd, not merely at the bootstrap's `cd`: `MOUNT_OPTS` sets all three timeouts to `0` and `pt_init` clears `kernel_cache`, so each absolute path is re-walked component by component, per caller, per op. `b51` drives it; `policy_tier_is_caller_sensitive` is untouched, because `synth` was already in its set and classifying the component as the overlay node is what makes `route()` read the mark there.

    **AND WHERE THE HOST HAS NOTHING — the default configuration in production — the narrower rule still holds:** at the project path an unmarked caller gets NEITHER read NOR write, and `policy_project_route`'s mark check is untouched.

    `policy_tier_is_caller_sensitive` is `{T_FAIL, T_PROJECT, T_SYNTH}` exactly, and it is an **optimisation, not the rule** — the cheap conservative gate on the per-op `/proc` mark read, holding exactly the tiers whose `VIEW_CLI` answer can differ in `VIEW_HOST`. `T_PROJECT` because the pins are struck; `T_SYNTH` because the ancestor table is not consulted, so an ancestor-of-a-pin directory falls to `fail` there and `fail` means host — without it nothing would ever ask, and an unmarked caller would keep meeting a `0555` scaffold node over a directory the orchestrator HAS — *"a violation, not a rounding"*. `hide`, `bind` and `host` are out because `VIEW_HOST` strikes only `project` pins, so all three answer identically in both views. The map is a function of `(tier, marked)` and the view alone — the host filesystem is not a routing input — and it is driven exhaustively by `b19` (twice, once per host axis, asserting the two passes AGREE), `b20`, `b37`, `b38`, `b40` and `b41`.

    **WHY `fail` IS HERE, AND WHY ITS RULE IS THE UNCONDITIONAL ONE.** `fail` means *no pin covers this*, which is a statement about **cc's pin list** and not about a caller that was never going to be served the remote. Every shell, hook, forwarder and MCP subprocess in the chroot is a fresh and permanently unmarked thread group, so before this the pin list had to cover every object every one of them loads — which is why `libtinfo.so.6` killed `bash` although marking never enters it, and why the list grew each time a new tool appeared on the host. A project path is pinned **by construction** (`add('project', systemPath, …)`), so the project tier never was a source of that growth. **The list does not grow with the host** — it has to cover the CLI's own execution closure and nothing else, and an entry whose every path is read exclusively by an unmarked caller is deletable on that ground alone, at every geometry.

    | table said | marked caller | unmarked caller |
    |---|---|---|
    | `fail` | `-ENOENT`, `deny`/`unpinned-fail-closed` | **`host`, unconditionally**, plus a `served`/`unmarked-host-served` row |
    | `project` | the remote, via the control frame — **never a host fallback** | the pin is **struck**, so the path re-resolves in `VIEW_HOST`: **`hide`** if a shorter `hide` pin covers it (hidden stays hidden — `route()`'s `T_HIDE` arm answers `-ENOENT` first) and **`bind`** likewise; else the **overlay node** if it is a cwd component the orchestrator lacks — *including where a shorter `host` pin covers it*, which a project nested under the projects root, the home dir or a plugin dir always is; else a shorter `host` pin; else `fail` → **`host`** with a `served`/`unmarked-host-served` row. Never `T_PROJECT`, structurally |
    | `synth` | the read-only scaffold node, `0555`, listing its pinned children | the ancestor table is **not consulted**, so the path falls to `fail` → **`host`**: the orchestrator's real directory, with its real mode, ownership and write surface. This is intended, not a regression |
    | `host` / `hide` / `bind` | unchanged — with the same cwd-chain exception as the unmarked column: a `host`-pinned cwd component the orchestrator **does not have** is the overlay node here too, because a component *between* a host pin and the cwd carries no pin of its own, so the covering `host` pin wins in `VIEW_CLI` as well and shadows the ancestor promotion (which is gated on `fail`) | **unchanged** — `VIEW_HOST` strikes only `project` pins. One exception, and it is the cwd chain's: a `host`-pinned path that is a cwd component the orchestrator **does not have** is the overlay node, because the chain's guarantee is unconditional and a `host` pin routinely covers a remote `systemPath` nested under it |

    **PER-TIER, WHETHER THE ANSWER IS DECIDED OR INCIDENTAL** — because an accident is not a policy, and each of these is named so nobody later "simplifies" one out:

    | tier | answer to an unmarked caller | decided or incidental |
    |---|---|---|
    | `hide` | `-ENOENT` at `route()`'s first arm, before anything else | **DECIDED**, explicit and unconditional. It is what keeps the mirror — the only place remote bytes exist on this machine — and cc's control socket unreachable |
    | `synth` | `VIEW_CLI`: `0555` root:root from the ancestor table, reading nothing — **and the overlay too**, at a cwd component the orchestrator lacks that the ancestor table cannot answer for, either because a covering `host` pin shadows the promotion or because an exact pin strips the path out of the table; such a node takes `policy_cwd_ino`'s sub-range, an ancestor-table one takes the ancestor sub-range, so the two views can report different inodes for one path. `VIEW_HOST`: the **overlay** — a `0555` node at a cwd component the orchestrator lacks, inode from `policy_cwd_ino`'s own sub-range | **DECIDED.** A fixed stat, no host access either way. The ancestor table is `VIEW_CLI`'s alone |
    | `bind` | the bind target node, so `mount --bind` has a directory to land on | **DECIDED, and load-bearing for the launch**: `/proc`, `/sys` and `/dev` are resolved by **unmarked `mount`** before the marking event ever fires. Measured at 156 `mount` and 482 `umount` ops |
    | `project` | struck from `VIEW_HOST` entirely; `-ENOENT` from `policy_project_route`'s mark check for anything that still reaches it | **DECIDED** (2026-09-11). The denial is retained as **defence in depth** — no unmarked caller can reach it, because the tier is not in its view — and is kept because deleting a liveness guard on the only function that sends a control frame is not worth the risk |
    | *(`cwd`)* | **not a tier.** There is no `T_CWD`, no `policy_cwd_exempt`, no `policy_cwd_getattr` and no `0111` node: the chain is answered by the host with the floor, or by the overlay | — |
    | `host` | passthrough `openat(policy_host_fd, policy_rel(path))` | **DECIDED** by the pin |
    | **`fail` → `host`** | passthrough; the host's own answer | **The DECISION is "an unmarked caller is served from the host"; an ENOENT there is then INCIDENTAL** — the host filesystem's truthful answer about a path the table now says is the host's, which is exactly what every other host pin already produces. It is not a fallback wearing a policy's clothes, because there is no second side to have tried |

    **AND IT COSTS ONE REAL THING, WHICH THE EVENT LOG PAYS.** The instrument the pin list is derived from is the `unpinned-fail-closed` row naming the path, and an unmarked caller's missing object writes **no** such row, because the host's ENOENT is not a policy event and `route()` could not know about it anyway. So `policy_caller_tier` emits `served`/`unmarked-host-served` **on the substitution itself**, regardless of what the host read then does. The row means *an unmarked caller was routed to the host at an unpinned path*, deduped on `(path, reason, tgid)` — so one row per distinct path **per thread group** for this reason, around twenty per shell startup.

    **THE PROJECT TIER HAS NO SUBSTITUTION RULE OF ITS OWN, AND THERE IS NO `unmarked-project-host-served` REASON.** A project-tier path re-resolves in `VIEW_HOST` and lands on the same `fail` → `host` rule as everything else, under the same `unmarked-host-served` row — one rule, not two asymmetric ones gated differently. `fail`'s rule is unconditional and for its own reason: gating it on host existence would take away the unmarked **create** at an unpinned path (`R13(f)`, "host means host"), which `b43` pins at all three geometries.

    **THE WRITE SIDE IS REAL, AND IT IS THE WIDENING TO KNOW ABOUT.** `host` is a passthrough, so an unmarked caller gets the host's write side too and **the orchestrator's own permissions at the caller's uid become the only gate**, where an unpinned path would otherwise answer `-ENOENT` to everyone. Measured at both ends on this host: as **root**, an unmarked shell created `/usr/nope` on the orchestrator for real; at **uid 1000**, a write into a world-writable unpinned directory lands, while a write into a `0555` synthetic ancestor does not (`default_permissions` refuses it before the daemon is asked). So an unprivileged worker's reach into the orchestrator's filesystem is *whatever that uid could write there anyway, at any path cc did not pin*. Every such op is logged by path. Pinned by `R13(f)`; `R3`'s own fail-closed probe had to become a **marked** caller because of it.

    **THE SAME WRITE SIDE EXISTS WHEREVER THE ORCHESTRATOR HAS THE PATH**, project spelling included: an unmarked process can modify the orchestrator's file at the project's own absolute spelling. It grants **no authority the invoking uid did not already have outside the chroot** — the chroot was never a privilege boundary — and narrowing it to reads would give one substitution two behaviours. **This reaches the default narrow root too:** an unmarked caller meets the orchestrator's real directories at `/`, `/usr`, `/bin`, `/etc`, `/home` and `/root`, with the real write surface — `mkdir` inside one succeeds rather than answering `EROFS` from a synthetic `0555` node. That is intended, not a defect: *"'it could not have written there anyway' is not an acceptable substitute for serving the real directory"* (criterion 4).

    **UNDER AN ADVERTISED `mirrorRoot: '/'` THERE IS NO `fail` TIER AT ALL.** `add('project', '/')` covers everything unpinned, so `tier_of` can never return `T_FAIL` and the whole synthetic-ancestor scaffold collapses: `/bin`, `/usr`, `/usr/lib`, `/etc` and `/root` all resolve `project`. Measured by building the real table both ways, and pinned as a table case in `tests/systems-mirror-wide.test.mjs`. **The bootstrap death this shape causes cannot be fixed by a pin**, because it is multiply determined: `bootstrap.sh`'s `exec chroot "$ROOT" /bin/sh -c …` resolves `/bin` **unmarked** (the chroot's own exec precedes any marking event), gets `-ENOENT`, and the worker dies at exit 127 — and `/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu`, the parent of the pinned `libc.so.6`, is `project` too, so `libtinfo.so.6` is downstream rather than the cause. **THE VIEW DISSOLVES IT WITHOUT A GATE**: an unmarked caller resolves in `VIEW_HOST`, where the `project` pins are struck entirely, so that unmarked `/bin` is `fail` → host at **every** advertised root and the tier the CLI would have seen never enters the question — which is also why the discriminator is not host-entry existence (see the operative definition above). The CONFIGURATION fact stands on its own and is worth knowing: for the **marked CLI** a wide root really does collapse the synthetic-ancestor scaffold, and that is what `tests/systems-mirror-wide.test.mjs` pins.

    **A WIDE `mirrorRoot` PUTS ONE REQUIREMENT ON THE MIRROR SOURCE, AND IT IS A FIXTURE TRAP.** Every unpinned directory is `project`, including the **ancestors of the host pins** (`/usr` and `/usr/lib` above `/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libc.so.6`), and a MARKED caller resolves those through the control channel — it has no host fallback, so an ancestor the source lacks stops the walk short of the pin below it. A real remote system has them by construction. A *narrow* stand-in source does not, and the failure is named rather than mysterious: the daemon answers `remote-absent` at `/usr` and the marked walk ends there. **It is the MARKED walk and nothing earlier** — the bootstrap's own shell and `setpriv` precede the marking event and are host-served at every advertised root (see the pre-mark window below), so no pin of theirs is what this scaffold keeps alive. `R14`'s fixture therefore scaffolds a directory-only mirror of the host and **checks that precondition against the daemon's own `pins.txt`**, so a future pin whose ancestors it does not reach fails by name.

    **ONE STANDING CONDITION EITHER WAY: `markPath` MUST STAY THE CLI LAUNCHER.** The whole argument rests on the marked population being the CLI's own thread group and nothing else. Point `CC_UNION_MARK_PATH` at some widely-used binary and every process that loads it becomes marked, at which point `fail → host` is the wrong answer for a caller that *can* reach the remote.

    **AND IT MUST STAY THE COMMAND SPELLING, NOT THE REALPATH.** The daemon uses libfuse's **high-level** API, so every op arrives as a reconstructed path string and symlink resolution happens in the VFS, not in the daemon. An `execve` of a symlinked launcher therefore reaches the daemon as the **link first**, then `readlink`, then the target's own ancestor chain — so the registered spelling is matched, the mark fires there, and everything after it is the marked CLI's closure (`R16`). Two consequences: `mark_maybe` must stay on **resolution** (`route()`), because under an `open`-only mark the registered spelling is never opened — only its target is — and the mark would silently never fire; and re-registering the realpath would leave the link and its ancestors unmarked without shrinking `binaryPins`, since an npm/node install goes on reading its own package directory marked.

    ### The pre-mark window: the whole bootstrap chain, deliberately

    `bootstrap.sh` fires **no** marking event. The chroot'd `/bin/sh`, `setpriv` and the backend launch command `setpriv` execs all run **unmarked**; the marking event is the kernel resolving `CC_UNION_MARK_PATH` as the first step of the CLI's own `execve`. So the pre-mark window is the entire bootstrap chain by design, and every one of those processes resolves in `VIEW_HOST` — against the orchestrator, which is the machine each of them belongs to.

    **WHY THE WINDOW IS WIDE ON PURPOSE.** A marked caller resolves in `VIEW_CLI`, where an advertised `mirrorRoot: '/'` puts every unpinned path in the remote tier with **no host fallback**. A mark fired anywhere ahead of the CLI therefore makes `setpriv`'s own `PATH` lookup for the backend command a remote-tier resolution of a path that exists only on the orchestrator — measured as `deny getattr /usr/local/bin/<launcher> remote-absent comm=setpriv`, then `setpriv: failed to execute`. A mark fired by a shell `[ -e ]` on the launcher does the same to the shell's symlink walk, putting the launcher's *target chain* (`/usr/lib/node_modules/…`) in the remote tier too.

    Measured, and re-measured by `R12` on every gate run (default narrow root, fake CLI, 16-core box):

    | | measured |
    |---|---|
    | pre-mark ops on the CLI's tgid | **392** |
    | distinct pre-mark paths | **45** |
    | post-mark ops on the same tgid | ~360 |
    | pre-mark ops resolving `project`, `fail` or `hide` | **0** |

    The window is dash's startup, `setpriv` and its `libcap-ng`/`libcap` closure, the identity lookups (`/etc/passwd`, `/etc/group`, `/etc/nsswitch.conf`, the `userdb` probes), and the project cwd itself — the bootstrap's own `cd`. `R12` asserts a shell interpreter, the `setpriv` binary and the cwd are all in it, so a mark statement added to the script fails by name rather than by a number moving.

    **THE WINDOW STAYING CLEAN IS A PROPERTY OF THE VIEW, NOT OF ITS SIZE.** An unmarked caller resolves in `VIEW_HOST`, where `route()`'s `T_HIDE` arm answers `-ENOENT` before the trace is written and the remaining tiers can only be `host` or `synth` — so no path can answer one way to the pre-mark ops and another to the post-mark ones from one caller, however wide the window grows.

    **THE `cd` IS ANSWERED BY THE CWD CHAIN, NOT BY A MARK.** Each component of the CLI's cwd is traversable to an unmarked caller — the orchestrator's own directory floored to `--x`, or the overlay node where it has none (see the cwd chain below). `b-cwd-unmarked` (`tests/fuse-union-policy.test.mjs`) drives the whole unmarked route at the cwd leaf; `R12` observes the same fact at a real mount.

    **WHAT THE WIDE WINDOW COSTS, AND WHERE IT IS PAID.** A pin exists only for a path a **marked** caller reads. The bootstrap's interpreter chain (every shell spelling and `setpriv`), everything `binaryPins` derived from each, and `libcap-ng.so.0` are read exclusively by unmarked callers, so they are served from the host **by the view rather than by a pin** — and they carry no pin. `R13w` asserts at a wide root that no marked op names a path under `/usr/bin` at all, which is the standing licence; if the CLI grows a marked read there, `R13w` fails and they come back. **`/usr/bin` is not one of the retired entries, because it was never an entry at all**: it is derived output of `installPins(dir, dir)`, which returns the directory itself whenever a pinned binary there is not a symlink — so on a host where node is `/usr/bin/node` it survives through `binaryPins(execPath)`. What the wide window does **not** make droppable is `binaryPins(claudeCommand)`: the mark fires at the launcher's own lookup, so the launcher, its realpath and the realpath's ancestors are all walked marked (`R16`).

    **AND ONE RESIDUAL, DISCLOSED RATHER THAN DESIGNED AROUND.** `mark_maybe` fires on **resolution**, so any process that merely `stat`s the launcher marks itself — a backend probing for `claude` on `PATH` before forking, say. It cannot leave the CLI unmarked (a forked child is a fresh thread group and marks itself at its own `execve`), and the event log's `tgid`/`comm` columns name it directly. Closing it fully would need an `open`-only mark, which is ruled out: the daemon matches the **reconstructed path string** from libfuse's high-level API, and a symlinked launcher's registered spelling is never *opened* — only its target is — so an `open`-only mark would silently never fire.

    **THE ORDERING IS PINNED AT TWO LAYERS**: the script text (`2c` in `tests/fuse-lifecycle.test.mjs`, ungated, which fails if any statement precedes the `cd` or if a mark path reaches the script at all) and the consequence at the mount (`R12`).

    ### From a logged row to a pin entry, in four steps

    1. The lists live in `src/systems/fuse/tierTable.ts` — `LOADER_OBJECTS`, `ETC_PINS`. **No second copy anywhere in cc.**
    2. Add the `entry` the log printed to the `list` the log named. `suggestPin(refusedPath)` is the mapping and it runs on every harvested `deny`/`unpinned-fail-closed` row: `/etc/…` → `ETC_PINS`; a `.so` by name or a `/lib`,`/lib64`,`/usr/lib`,`/usr/lib64` path by location → `LOADER_OBJECTS` **in the `/usr/`-prefixed spelling** (`LOADER_PINS` derives the `/lib` spelling *and* the realpath from whatever is in the array, so the other spelling leaves the closure open — the `libcap-ng.so.0.0.0` failure); `/bin`,`/sbin`,`/usr/bin`,`/usr/sbin` → **no guess**, with a note saying what a bin-directory denial *means*: the bootstrap's own shell and `setpriv` are unmarked and host-served with no pin, so a refusal there is the **marked CLI's**, and the first thing to check is whether it is the launcher's own closure, which `binaryPins(claudeCommand)` pins automatically. That note **rules both arrays out rather than offering them** — neither is reachable as a correct destination from that arm, since an `/etc/` path and a loader path are claimed by the two tests above it. Every other path → **no guess** as well, and a note naming both arrays and the session's `localRoots`. **The session's own repair line prints that `note` verbatim** (`describePolicyEvents`, `src/systems/fuse/session.ts`), grouped by the note rather than by the array name so the two `list: null` arms cannot share one sentence — there is no second spelling of this advice anywhere in cc.
    3. `buildTierTable` runs per spawn and `renderPinsFile` writes `<rundir>/pins.txt`, which the daemon parses at mount — so **the change takes effect on the next spawn after an orchestrator restart**, because cc holds the module in memory. Every emitted line says so.
    4. `npm test` re-runs the tier-table tests, which pin both spellings, the realpath closure, the install-prefix derivation and the realpath ancestor chain `installPins` falls back to.
    **THE DERIVATION RULE HAS THREE "NEVER"s.** Add EXACTLY the prefix the log named, with a `why` that says what needed it. **Never** reinstate a fallback tier; **never** widen past what the log named; **never** pin a parent because several children appeared. **The three govern hand-adding a path the log named to an array, and nothing else** — they do not reach the launcher-closure derivation (`installPins`), which pins a parent because the marked VFS walk traverses it, not because children appeared in a log. The log is the only evidence of what the closure needs, and each of those three turns one measured refusal into an unmeasured grant.
    **AND A CLEARANCE DOES NOT TRANSFER.** What licenses declaring a set of fail-closed paths harmless is `R4` — a full turn completing with those paths fail-closed, so nothing the worker needed was among them. That basis is **geometry- and turn-specific**: under an advertised mirror root of `/` those same paths are project-tier and served from the source, and a CLI that grows a dependency on any of them reopens the question. `R4` is the standing instrument, not a one-off clearance — re-run it rather than citing a past run of it.
    **`T_SYNTH` IS DERIVED, never written in the pins file.** With `/` itself `fail`, nothing under any pin would be reachable — so `anc_build()` takes every strict ancestor of every pin, plus `/`, minus any path carrying an exact pin, and serves each as a read-only directory with **fixed** attributes (`0555`, uid/gid 0, nlink 2, size 0, all times 0, `st_ino` from the load-time table offset into `SYNTH_INO_BASE`). It never stats the host directory of the same name — existence, mtime and size are exactly what the tier table withholds. Every mutating op on a synthetic or `bind` node is **`EROFS`, not `EACCES`**: there is nothing behind the node a permissions fix could reach.
    **THE CWD CHAIN, AND THE TWO MECHANISMS THAT MAKE IT ENTERABLE.** Its first user is `bootstrap.sh` itself: the chroot'd shell `cd`s into the CLI's cwd unmarked, before `setpriv` and before the CLI exists. Its second is every subprocess the CLI spawns — a spawn `chdir`s into the CLI's cwd **in the forked child**, before it execs, so that caller is a brand-new and therefore unmarked thread group — and without the two mechanisms below every child the CLI spawns at its own cwd dies before its image runs, in two distinct ways: at the project tier's unmarked denial, and — under a `mirrorRoot` that is a strict ancestor of `systemPath` — in `chdir()` on an intervening directory the kernel refuses search on, which makes every Bash call die `Exit code 1` with no output. Each component of the chain is made traversable by one of two things and by nothing else — the floor in `VIEW_HOST` alone, since the marked CLI is shown the host's real mode; the overlay in **both** views, since `VIEW_CLI` has no host directory to floor at a component the orchestrator lacks:

    - **the floor** — `policy_floor_traversal` (and `policy_floor_mask` for `pt_access`) sets `0111` on the orchestrator's OWN directory where it has one. It is **the only mutation of a host stat this daemon makes**, and it is scoped to the chain: everything else keeps its real mode, which satisfies constraint 1 *more* exactly than an unscoped floor would, since an unscoped floor grants traversal the host itself denies.
    - **the overlay** — `resolve_class`'s clause, below the view branch and therefore answering in **both views**, returns `T_SYNTH` where the contest produced `fail` **or `host`**, the path is a chain component, AND `policy_host_absent` says the orchestrator has nothing. The tier set is **enumerated, not negated**, so `hide`, `bind` and `project` stay out by construction: hidden stays hidden, a bind target keeps its own node, and `VIEW_HOST` cannot produce `project` at all. `host` is in it because a `host` pin routinely **covers** a chain component the orchestrator lacks — `buildTierTable` pins the projects root, the home dir, cc's own checkout and every plugin dir `host`, and a `project` pin nested under one of those is struck in `VIEW_HOST`, handing the contest to the shorter `host` pin. **At an INTERVENING component it covers in `VIEW_CLI` too**, and for a different reason: such a component carries no pin of its own at all, so nothing longer contests it in either view, and `VIEW_CLI`'s ancestor promotion — gated on `fail` — is shadowed rather than absent (the component *is* in the table). Gate on `fail` alone and the overlay is unreachable at exactly those paths: `route()`'s `T_HOST` arm hands the op the orchestrator's own fd, the chroot'd `cd` dies `-ENOENT` before `execve`, and the marked CLI dies the same way at every absolute path under its cwd. `VIEW_CLI`'s ancestor arm is asked **first**, which keeps an explicit `fail` pin on an absent chain component a refusal rather than a node. `policy_synth_getattr`'s chain arm is unscoped by view for the matching reason: an exactly-pinned absent chain component routes to the overlay while `anc_build` has stripped it from the ancestor table, and a `VIEW_HOST`-only arm would answer `-ENOENT` at a path `route()` has already called a node. The clause **cannot reach what a `host` pin protects** — `policy_host_absent` fires it only where the orchestrator has nothing, so the store and the plugin dirs, which exist, are structurally out of range; and the node it produces serves no remote bytes and no remote names (`policy_table_child_exists` drops every table name the orchestrator lacks). Its intended side-effect is that an unmarked `ls` of the host-pinned parent now **names** the project directory, which is what keeps `ls` and `cd` from disagreeing about it. `b50` drives the clause and its four exclusions at the cwd leaf, `b51` at the intervening components and in both views. It is **irreducible**, and the reason is a *must-hold-when* rather than an *always*: "serve the host, floored" dies at one path — the cwd itself — **wherever the orchestrator lacks `systemPath`**, because a floor changes a mode and there is no node to put a mode on, so `chdir` gets `-ENOENT` at *every* geometry, today's default included. Whether it lacks it is **deployment- and geometry-conditional** (`_assertRemoteMountable` lstats the *mirror root* through the **system's** provider — `src/instances.ts` — so it constrains the remote and never the orchestrator). The clause is irreducible because it must hold **when** the orchestrator lacks the path, not because it always does; a host collision at that spelling is the exception, and it is divergence rather than a leak. **That divergence reaches the bootstrap's own `cd`:** where the orchestrator happens to have a directory at the project's absolute spelling, the pre-CLI shell's cwd is the *orchestrator's* directory and the marked CLI's is the *system's*. Only traversability is consumed — `chdir` reads nothing and writes nothing, and routing is by path string in both views — so the CLI's own answers are unchanged.

    **THERE IS NO CWD EXEMPTION DOING THIS, AND NONE MAY BE REINTRODUCED**: no `T_CWD`, no `policy_cwd_exempt` and no `getattr`-only op allow-list under it, no `policy_cwd_getattr`, no `0111` node, no `cwd-traversal-served` or `unmarked-project-host-served` reason, and no "stop at the first ancestor the host has" traversal bound for `route()`'s ordering to enforce. There is no conditional grant whose extent an ordering could bound. What survives with a new role is the injected cwd (`cwd_path`), the chain predicate (`policy_cwd_component`) and the chain's inode sub-range (`policy_cwd_ino`), now reached from `policy_synth_getattr`.

    **THE FLOOR'S FOUR OP SITES, AND THE ENUMERATION IS THE POINT.** `pt_getattr`'s path arm, `pt_getattr`'s **fh arm**, `pt_readdir`'s per-child stat (all three through `policy_floor_traversal`) and `pt_access`'s mask (`policy_floor_mask`). The fh arm is a **no-op today** — libfuse passes `fi` to `getattr` only for regular files, so `S_ISDIR` is false — and is applied anyway so the two arms agree *structurally* rather than by a property of the library version; its view comes from a new `fd_view` table written by the same `fd_tier_set` call the open already makes, so the fast path stays fast. `pt_access` is not optional: without it `stat /root` reports `0711` while `access(X_OK)` answers `EACCES`, which is `test -x` and `stat` disagreeing from one caller. `b47` pins that the two entry points agree; a source-shape assertion in `tests/fuse-union-policy.test.mjs` pins that they appear at those four bodies **and nowhere else**, so a fifth site means the enumeration is wrong rather than that the site should be added.

    **THE FLOOR'S RESIDUAL, WHICH CANNOT BE CLOSED.** A floored `0700` directory advertises a traversal its *contents* then refuse — `stat /root` says traversable, `stat /root/secret` says `EACCES` — because the daemon's own op runs under `cred_enter` against the real mode. `cred_enter` is mandatory (measured: without it the CLI's `Bash` tool fails outright), and `default_permissions` means the kernel decides traversal from the mode the union reports, so permitting the walk *requires* reporting `x`. The floor grants **path resolution only, never access**: everything under such a directory stays refused by the real filesystem, and the chain itself works because the next component is either also floored or is the overlay node, which touches no host.
    **THE FLOOR'S MINIMALITY IS OPEN, AND IT CANNOT BE COMPUTED EXACTLY.** At a `0700` chain component owned by the worker's own uid the chroot runs without the floor, so read as a minimality constraint an UNCONDITIONAL floor over-serves. Narrowing it is arguable and looks cheap in isolation, but `fuse_get_context()` carries no supplementary groups and no ACLs, so a conditional floor is exact only from above and still widens in a residual set — and it adds a credentials axis where today only marked/unmarked exists. `policy_floor_mask` would then have to condition IDENTICALLY to `policy_floor_traversal`, or `stat` and `test -x` disagree from one caller. Treat it as a separate change with its own evidence, not a tidy-up.

    **A DIRENT RULE HAS TWO HALVES AND THEY ARE DIFFERENT QUESTIONS.** `policy_dirent_visible` answers *may this view SEE this name*; `policy_table_child_exists` answers *is there anything there*. Conflating them produces an `ls`/`cat` disagreement at each of the three emit sites: an `exclude` under the project named `node_modules` in an unmarked listing of the overlay cwd while every op on it answers `-ENOENT`; a `project`-pinned child that resolves to the **overlay** is host-checked against the raw pin tier, found absent and dropped, so `stat <systemPath>` answers and `cd` works while `ls` of its parent omits the name; and a `T_SYNTH` **pin tier** is emitted unchecked, which is true of a `VIEW_CLI` scaffold node and false in `VIEW_HOST`, where an off-chain host-absent ancestor is `fail` → host → `-ENOENT`. The existence rule is one sentence — **a fixed node exists by construction, everything else exists exactly where the orchestrator has it** — and it is view-shaped only because WHICH paths are fixed nodes differs: the ancestor table in `VIEW_CLI`, the cwd overlay in `VIEW_HOST`. It is asked at the merge boundary (`pinned_children_emit`) **and on a `VIEW_CLI` synthetic node's own children too**, through a `scaffold` flag on the predicate that carves out **`T_PROJECT` and nothing else**. Skipping the check wholesale on those nodes leaves the `/etc` instance standing, so the two-part reason has to be stated or the carve-out reads wider than it is. **(i)** For a `project` child the host is the *wrong axis*: a project path's existence to the CLI is the MIRROR's question, by tier, wherever the host happens to hold it, and the right axis is a control frame that does not exist. *(Do not rest this on "the orchestrator has nothing at `systemPath`" — that is deployment- and geometry-conditional, and the axis argument holds without it.)* **(ii)** For a **`T_HOST` pin child of that same node** the host IS the right axis and the probe costs one `fstatat` with no control frame, so the carve-out does not reach it — leaving it unchecked has the MARKED CLI's `ls /etc` name `ETC_PINS` entries absent on the host while `cat` answers `-ENOENT`, the third instance of the same class. `b49` drives both halves, and asserts the flag inert in `VIEW_HOST`.

    **THE ABSENCE PROBE IS PER-OP AND FALLS "NOT ABSENT" ON AN UNKNOWN ERROR.** `policy_host_absent` answers ABSENT only for `ENOENT`, `ENOTDIR` and `ENAMETOOLONG`; on `ELOOP`, `EIO`, NFS under `root_squash` or a permission-enforcing FUSE beneath us it answers **not absent**, so no node is synthesized and the path falls to `fail` → `host` where the host answers for itself. Falling *absent* would place a traverse-only node over a directory the orchestrator may really have, hiding it and its write surface **silently**; falling *not absent* breaks `chdir` at that one path **loudly**, with an event row naming it. It is not cached at mount: the set needing the decision is `depth(cwd)` paths behind a string compare, and a cache would buy that back for a stale window in which the orchestrator *gains* a directory and a cached "absent" goes on hiding it. `b48` drives every errno including a constructed `ELOOP`.

    **THE DERIVATION, AND THERE IS NO TABLE TO GO STALE.** `cwd_path` is one injected string — `CC_UNION_CWD`, fed from the `CC_FUSE_CWD` `wrap.ts` already sets from `plan.cwdInside` — compared per op, the same shape as `mark_path`. It lives in **`policy.h` and not `union.c`**, and that is a design requirement rather than a placement preference: the unit fixture compiles `policy.h` alone and assigns it directly as a seam, so a variable in `union.c` would be undrivable — which is exactly why `mark_path`'s own marking event is COVERED NOWHERE.

    **THE COMPARISON IS ANCESTOR-OR-EQUAL AT A COMPONENT BOUNDARY, NEVER A BARE PREFIX.** The TS side keeps containment on `path.posix.relative` (`withinPosix`, `src/systems/mirror.ts`) precisely so `/app-backup` is not inside `/app`; the chain test has the identical trap in the other direction — for a cwd of `/root/srv2` the candidate `/root/srv` **is** a string prefix, and a bare `strncmp` would put a directory on the chain that is not on it at all. C has no `path.posix.relative`, so the boundary check **is** the C spelling of that rule. Both directions of the trap are rejected, by different mechanics, and `b22` drives both.

    **`CC_UNION_CWD` IS REQUIRED, HAS NO DEFAULT, AND IS REFUSED WHEN NOT NORMALISED.** The chain is the whole domain of both the floor and the overlay, so a missing cwd means no floor, no overlay node, and every unmarked `chdir` dead at its destination — a regression that looks exactly like a working mount. `main()` refuses to mount without it, alongside `CC_UNION_MARK_PATH` and `CC_UNION_CONTROL` and for the same class of reason: one input enables the project tier at all, this one enables entry to it. A non-normalised value is **refused, never normalised** — normalising means a path canonicaliser in C with its own bugs, `..` cannot be resolved without touching the filesystem, and **cc owns the input**, so a non-normalised value is a cc defect and a loud refusal is the right response. `buildFusePlan` asserts the same thing at configuration time, where the failure is legible.

    **THE OVERLAY NODE IS `0555`, AND THE OLD "MUST NEVER BE UNIFIED" ARGUMENT IS DISSOLVED RATHER THAN OVERRULED.** It held because `T_CWD` sat over a *project* path, where a listing could name remote content and `0555` would have handed an unmarked caller *"the content of these remote directories"*. With the remote struck from `VIEW_HOST` the node's listing names nothing remote — but **not** because "`policy_synth_children` finds no pin or ancestor under a path the orchestrator does not have", which was the first argument here and is false as a mechanism: that function scans the **pin table**, which knows nothing about what the orchestrator holds, and an `exclude` or a deeper pin under the project puts names there readily. The emptiness comes from the **emit**: `policy_table_child_exists` drops every table name the orchestrator does not have, and it has nothing under a path it has nothing at. What survives is fixed nodes, which name nothing remote either. The correction is recorded because the false version would let a reader delete the check that makes the claim true. `policy_mutation_check`'s `-EROFS` still applies and is still right: the orchestrator has nothing at that path, so constraint 1 owes nothing there.

    **THE INODE HAS ITS OWN SUB-RANGE, AND `policy_bind_ino` CANNOT SERVE THE CHAIN.** `policy_bind_ino` scans for an **exact** pin and returns a single shared fallback for anything unpinned. The chain covers intermediate components with **no** exact pin, so every one would report the same `st_ino`. `policy_cwd_ino` keys on the component's **depth**: `SYNTH_INO_BASE + MAX_ANC + npins + depth`, disjoint from the ancestor range and the exact-pin range, which `b27` pins. What observes it: `use_ino = 1` is this daemon's own stated invariant, and `test -ef` compares `(st_dev, st_ino)` at two `stat` calls and no readdir, so under a collision `[ /root -ef /root/srv2 ]` would answer TRUE.

    **What it does NOT fix, as results rather than caveats.** (1) It makes a spawn *start* and it makes an unmarked process able to read **the orchestrator's own filesystem**; it does not make one able to read the **remote**. Every project-tier file stays unreachable to an unmarked caller — structurally now, since the tier is not in its view — so the CLI's own git spawns remain broken by design and `Glob`/`Grep` stay denied. (`rg` itself is reachable: its binary and its `libpcre2-8`/`libacl`/`libattr` closure are host-served to an unmarked caller — the tool is still denied at cc's own seam.) (2) **The chain is the whole domain, and no further** — not files, not children, not siblings, and not ancestors of a project pin that are off the cwd path. The old bound ("above by the first ancestor the host has", a property of `route()`'s ordering) is **gone with the exemption**: there is no grant to bound, because the chain's answer does not depend on what else the table says. `b22` pins the extent and `b39` pins that every link of it is enterable at all three geometries. (3) For a caller holding `CAP_DAC_READ_SEARCH` the mode bits grant nothing — but there is no op allow-list left to be the second gate either, because in `VIEW_HOST` the node is an ordinary read-only synthetic directory, and its listing is emptied by the **emit's** existence check rather than by anything about the node itself. Through a real mount the acceptance arms are R8, R9 and R13 in `tests/fuse-union-routing.real.test.mjs`.
    **`policy.h` IS THE DETERMINISTICALLY-DRIVABLE HALF**, and the split is what makes it provable: it includes no libfuse header and takes three injected seams — `policy_proc` (`/proc` field 22, TID→TGID, `comm` and `cmdline` — four readers), `policy_clock` (the resolution cache's TTL) and `ccu_xport` (the control round trip). `tests/fixtures/union-policy-driver.c` supplies fakes for all three and `tests/fuse-union-policy.test.mjs` runs it, compiling through the **same** `detectToolchain()` the product builds with so it can only skip where the product could not have built the daemon. What stays real-gate-only is listed in `docs/systems-protocol.md` and the real gate's own headers name what stays real-gate-only.
    **THE CALLER MARK** is sticky per TGID, survives `exec`, and is revalidated against `/proc/<pid>/stat` field 22 on **every** check, so a recycled pid cannot inherit it. It fires on the CLI's own `execve` of `CC_UNION_MARK_PATH` and on nothing earlier: `bootstrap.sh`'s chroot'd shell, `setpriv` and the backend launch command all run unmarked, and the `cd` into the project tree is answered by the cwd chain rather than by a mark. See the pre-mark window above.
    **THE RESOLUTION CACHE** replaces the kernel's: `(tgid, path) → {errno, expires}`, `CACHE_TTL_MS = 1000` against the injected clock, consulted **before** the mark check — which is what makes the tgid load-bearing rather than decorative, since only the key stops an unmarked caller matching a marked one's warmed entry. `FETCH` never consults it and always invalidates, so freshness at open is exact and a cached `-ENOENT` cannot outlive the fetch that found the file.
    **THE POLICY EVENT LOG** (`CC_UNION_EVENTS`, `<rundir>/events.log`) records every fail-closed path, every unmarked denial, every refused control reply **and every op the daemon served some way other than the way the tier table said**, as `<kind>\t<op>\t<path>\t<reason>\t<pid>\t<tgid>\t<comm>\t<cmdline>` after a `#` header line, deduped on `(path, reason, tgid)`. Row format, the escaping, the recorded absences, the two kinds and every reason with its kind are in `docs/protocol.md`.

    **AND IT RECORDS WHO ASKED.** `deny getattr /bin unpinned-fail-closed` alone cannot tell the bootstrap shell dying at `exec` from a hook subprocess poking around, and `CC_FUSE_TRACE` cannot substitute — the trace is off unless the operator set it *before* the failing spawn, and a spawn death has to be diagnosable from the log that was already running. **IDENTITY IS SAMPLED AT POLICY TIME**, after the decision and after the dedupe, so the two /proc reads it costs — `comm` and `cmdline` — are paid once per DISTINCT ROW rather than once per op. The TGID read is not: the dedupe key needs it, so it happens on every call, and it is the same read `mark_of` already makes. Identity can never change an answer; `exec(2)` replaces `comm`/`cmdline` while leaving pid, tgid and start time untouched, so a row may name what the process *became* and no validation can fix that — the header line says so in the file. **THE TGID IS PART OF THE DEDUPE KEY, and has to be**: at `(path, reason)` the first caller to reach a path wins the row and every later one is dropped, so the identity columns would answer *who asked* with *whoever happened to be first*. `harvestEvents`' key matches the daemon's by design, and the two must change together. It is the instrument the pin list is DERIVED from: read it, add **exactly** the prefix it named to `tierTable.ts` with a `why` saying what needed it — never a fallback, never a parent because several children appeared.

    **IT IS NOT A REFUSAL LOG, AND CALLING IT ONE WAS A FALSE CLAIM.** `self-recursion` returns 0 from `host_fd` — the op *succeeds* — and `pinned-children-truncated` drops a name from a `readdir` that also succeeds, so two non-denials already sat in a file called `refusals.log` and every reader's filter had to enumerate reason strings by hand to exclude them. **The kind is the first column so a filter derives from it**, which is what R4 does; a filter enumerating reason strings by hand instead reads `self-recursion` (project-tier only, therefore inside the project tree by construction) as "a project path was refused".

    **AND THE CWD CHAIN NO LONGER WRITES A ROW, BECAUSE THERE IS NO GRANT TO RECORD.** A `served`/`cwd-traversal-served` event would be an exemption's own event, showing *which process needed which link* — the question a traversal bound would have to be decided from. There is no bound and no exemption: a chain component is answered by the orchestrator's own directory (a `served`/`unmarked-host-served` row, like any other `fail` → `host`) or by the overlay node (no row — nothing was substituted). The reason is retired from `EVENT_KINDS`, and re-adding it means re-adding a mechanism.

    **AND IT SURVIVES THE SESSION.** `runTeardown` `rm -rf`s the run directory, and refusals are not traced (`tr()` runs only after `route()` returns 0), so the log was the only record of a fail-closed path and the reclaim destroyed it. It is now harvested **immediately before** the reclaim into `orchStoreRoot()/systems/fuse/events.log` (`fuseEventStore()`), **keyed `(path, reason, tgid)` exactly as the daemon keys it** — so ONE PATH CAN PRODUCE SEVERAL ROWS, and routinely does, because two CALLERS can reach it: `/var` carries `deny`/`unpinned-fail-closed` from the marked CLI and `served`/`unmarked-host-served` from an unmarked one, observed in gate runs. A path-keyed harvest keeps whichever row the daemon wrote first, so when the `served` row wins, **the store carries no pin suggestion for a path the CLI's own denial asked for** and nothing says so. (This is a deliberate departure from "one row per distinct path"; `TeardownReport.eventPaths` stays a distinct-PATH list, which is what its one consumer asks.) Row shape: `<iso8601>\t<instanceId>\t<kind>\t<op>\t<path>\t<pid>\t<tgid>\t<comm>\t<cmdline>\t<suggested list>\t<suggested entry>` — the session row's own columns in the daemon's own escaped spelling, and the last two for `deny`/`unpinned-fail-closed` rows only, because a pin does not fix an `unmarked-host-served` row. The denial sentence names `comm[pid]` per row; the full argv is one `grep` away in the store file the sentence already points at. One insertion point covers the clean, wedged and contained-throw paths, and because `sweepFuseSessions` reaches the same function a session lost to a crash is harvested at the next boot. **Surfaced on three existing surfaces, no new event type**: the session's stderr stream, `Instance._awaitFuseMount`'s spawn-failure message (read **before** `fuse.teardown()` deletes the log), and the boot sweep's `log.warn`. **Capped at 20 ROWS per kind inline, then `(+K more; full list at …)` — never a bare count**, because the rows under one `unpinned-fail-closed: 60` can be one missing library, sixty times. Rows and not paths, because the key carries the reason and the tgid: a path carrying two deny reasons, or one reason from two thread groups, occupies two of the twenty. The cap's job is bounding the OUTPUT, so rows is the unit that bounds it.
    **WHICH CASES MOUNT FOR REAL, because it is not only the gate:** `tests/systems-mirror-geometry-follow.test.mjs` boots `realProcess: true` and spawns real remote workers, so its arms are inside `npm test` and inside both `gate:systems` rows. Anything that changes what cc hands the daemon — the pins schema, `MOUNT_OPTS`, `CC_FUSE_*`/`CC_UNION_*`, `bootstrap.sh` — reds them, not only `RUN_FUSE_LIFECYCLE=1`.
  - `control.ts` — **the control channel, cc's half.** A unix stream socket at `<rundir>/control.sock`, created by `FuseSession.prepare()` before the spawn (the daemon refuses to mount without one) and closed by `FuseSession.teardown()` — **outside the teardown latch**, because `prepare()` may have opened a newer one. Fixed binary frames, big-endian, **no payload ever**: `request: u32 'CCU1' | u8 op | u8 flags | u16 pathlen | path`, `reply: u32 'CCU1' | u8 status | u8 pad | i32 errno | u32 reserved`. Ops are `STAT`/`LIST`/`FETCH`/`DIRTY`; statuses `READY`/`ABSENT`/`REFUSED`, and the **errno is cc's** so the daemon never invents one. **The flags byte is OP-SCOPED** — each bit is meaningful for exactly one op, and naming which is part of the definition: `FOR_CREATE` 0x01 (FETCH), `FOR_WRITE` 0x02 (FETCH: take the claim; DIRTY: keep it), `REMOVED` 0x04 (DIRTY), `RELEASE_ONLY` 0x08 (DIRTY). They exist because cc cannot tell the worker's intent from its own cache management, so the intent is DECLARED. A frame cc cannot act on kills the CONNECTION — the stream is out of frame and every later byte would be misread.

    **THE ADDRESS IS NOT THE PATH.** `sockaddr_un` is `char sun_path[108]`, so bind(2)/connect(2) take 107 usable bytes — and the socket's real path grows with the store root, which would make a deep root a spawn-time `EINVAL` with nothing naming the limit. Both ends address the socket as **`/proc/self/fd/<dirfd>/control.sock`**, formed from a directory fd each opens on the socket's parent: `sunPathAddress(dirfd, name)` here, and `control_connect()` in `union.c`. The socket **file is unmoved**; only the address string is, from one that grows with the store root to one bounded at ~34 bytes.
    - **cc HOLDS its dirfd until after `server.close()` resolves**, then closes it — and also on the `listen` failure path, WITHOUT WHICH a refused spawn would leak one per attempt. (A spawn refused *after* a successful listen leaks nothing either: the fd is held by the `ControlServer` with the same lifetime as the socket file, and `teardown()` or the next `prepare()`'s `#control?.close()` reclaims both.) libuv unlinks the pipe by the name it bound with, and a closed fd number is reused immediately — closing first would point that unlink at a `control.sock` in whatever directory took the number. `close()`'s `fsp.rm` on the **real** path is the authoritative unlink.
    - **The daemon holds none — it opens one per connect.** `control_connect` runs lazily per FUSE worker thread and again after every framing error (`control_drop`), so a held fd would need a lifetime rule spanning the whole mount; it unlinks nothing, so the hazard above does not apply. It splits the path with `strrchr`, never `libgen`'s `dirname()`, which mutates its argument — and `CC_UNION_CONTROL` points into `environ`.
    - `O_RDONLY|O_DIRECTORY` on the node side: `fs.constants.O_PATH` is `undefined`, and this needs no magic number for an address-only fd.
    - **Containment is unchanged and `SO_PEERCRED` is deliberately not taken.** `/proc/self/fd` resolves in the *calling* process's own fd table, so the address names nothing a chrooted caller can reach; `<rundir>` keeps its `hide` pin and `route()` still answers `-ENOENT` for the socket's real path. A same-uid process in the chroot can already reach the socket through the bind-mounted `/proc/<ccpid>/root/…` — pre-existing — so authenticating this route would imply a boundary the `/proc` bind mount does not provide.
    - `FUSE_CONTROL_SOCK_PATH_TOO_LONG` survives **as a diagnostic on the formed address**, not on any path on disk. It cannot fire in production: `/proc/self/fd/` (14) + the fd's digits + the basename tops out around 34 bytes and the store root is not in it. Firing means cc chose an ~80-character basename — a cc defect, and the message says so instead of the old, now-false *"move the store root somewhere shorter"*.
    - **Per-open revalidate**, `#fresh`: `p → (source size, source ms-mtime, MIRROR INODE)`, recorded at every materialisation and compared before the next copy. Without it every open transfers the whole file — `#shape` alone skips re-truncating a stub whose size and ms-mtime match, but the copy after it runs regardless — which across a wire is the whole file, per open, for the life of the session. **The mirror inode is what makes it SOUND rather than merely fast**: source size and mtime alone would let cc skip a copy for a mirror entry it is no longer the author of (one re-shaped under it, or replaced by a rename) and serve whatever now sits there as fresh; the inode changes in every such case and in none of the safe ones. `bigint` inodes, because a Number comparison answers "same" for two different files above 2^53. **The order is the policy — `source.stat` → `#shape` → cap → freshness → copy**: `#shape` already leaves a matching file alone so the two agree, but freshness first would skip a `#shape` that a kind change needs. **A CLAIMED PATH REACHES NONE OF IT** — `#fetch`'s claim short-circuit precedes `#fetchBody` and therefore precedes every source call, the freshness check included, which is what keeps revalidate out of the two-handle window. What it rests on: millisecond mtime — the wire's `find -printf '%T@'` is seconds.nanoseconds on GNU, so no precision is lost at the source, and **a source whose `stat` collapses sub-second precision must be refused before this check is trusted**, which nothing in cc enforces today. A source file rewritten within one mtime tick **at an identical size** is missed — nanoseconds wide, named rather than defended against.
    - **The protocol cap, checked before the transfer is attempted.** A source file above `MAX_FILE_BYTES` (32 MiB) is refused `REFUSED`/**`EFBIG`** — a named errno, not the catch-all `EIO` — from the size the stat already carried. The saving is real rather than cosmetic: `readFileBytes` refuses over-cap too, but LATE, because `ProviderSystem.#read`'s fence counts what cc **keeps** — the provider streams `data` frames until cc's accumulation crosses the cap and only then does cc send `close`, so up to 32 MiB of base64 crosses the wire to be discarded. Whole-file materialisation is structural: the mirror must hold the whole file to serve arbitrary offsets, so the read frame's `offset` would buy nothing.
    - **The mode ledger**, `#mode`: `p → (the mode the SOURCE had when cc last looked, the MIRROR INODE that was carrying it)` — written by `#record`, in ONE CALL with the fingerprint. The two values are not one observation and the invariant does not claim they are — the mode is the **source's** (`st.mode`) and the inode is the **mirror entry's** (`cur.ino`); what holds is that they are recorded TOGETHER, so the pair can never be half-updated and the inode a mode is compared against is always the entry that was carrying that mode. **THE DISCRIMINATOR IS THE INODE, and it has to be.** An atomic write ends in a `rename`, which REPLACES the mirror entry, so the mode cc is about to push is the tmp file's fresh 0644 and the pre-edit 0755 is gone; a deliberate `chmod` KEEPS the inode, so the mirror's mode is the one the worker asked for and restoring anything would discard it. *"Always restore" breaks chmod; "never restore" breaks Edit.* On a `push`, `#restoreMode` chmods the MIRROR back to the recorded mode when the inode differs and does nothing when it matches. **cc chmods the mirror rather than passing a mode through `push`**: `RemoteSource.push` keeps one signature so both sources behave identically, the invariant `#shape` maintains (*the mirror entry's mode is the source's*) is restored rather than bypassed, and the property becomes observable on this machine. A path with no recorded mode is a file the worker created and pushes the mirror's own mode. `tests/fuse-transport.test.mjs` T11 drives both arms; `tests/fuse-union-routing.real.test.mjs` R10 drives the rename at the real mount.
    - **The fault record**, `#faults`: `p → Fault`, **sticky for the session's life**, and per-session like the mirror it describes. **CLEARED BY EXACTLY ONE THING:** a SUCCESSFUL push of the same path, which is the moment the diverged sentence becomes false. The reason for "cleared by nothing" — cc cannot resync without destroying the bytes the worker wrote — is true of every path except that one, where the bytes are the ones that LANDED. An `over-cap` fault is cleared by nothing at all. Two kinds, differing in what stays reachable:
      - `{kind:'diverged', detail, refuses:'writes'}` — set when a `push` fails. **THE CLAIM IS KEPT**, and that is the whole of what makes the criterion hold: cc stops managing the path as a cache, so the worker's unpushed bytes survive in the mirror rather than being re-shaped away by the next `STAT` or parent `LIST`. Reads still succeed and serve those bytes — the recovery channel the refusal points at — and writes are refused. **Recorded on the `push` branch ONLY**: the wording promises the local copy is intact and readable, which is true exactly there, so a failed `remove` and a claimed path whose mirror entry vanished under cc are released as before.
      - `{kind:'over-cap', size, cap, refuses:'all'}` — set where the cap is enforced, so a FILE TOOL can be refused by a sentence naming the cap before the worker ever opens the path. Refuses reads too, because cc never materialised the file and has nothing to serve.
      **THREE SPELLING RESIDUALS, named so the next reader does not re-derive them.** `#faults` keys the daemon's raw `req.path` while `#mirrorPath` normalises, so a fault and a tool lookup can disagree about one path in three ways: a tool input naming a **symlink** (the VFS resolves the leaf before FUSE sees it, so the control frame keys the resolved target while `faultAt` is asked about the link's spelling); a non-normalised absolute input (`..` segments); and the raw-vs-normalised key itself. **All three are exactly the exposure `classifyForTool` already carries** — it is a longest-prefix rule over the caller's own spelling and never resolves a path either — so the fault surface is never *weaker* than the tier gate, only equally literal. A miss costs a refusal not made, never a refusal wrongly made.
      `#fetch`'s fault gate runs **ahead of the claim short-circuit and of every source call**. Two accessors serve the hook — `faultAt(p)` (a plain map lookup, no probe) and `settle(p)` (await the path's in-flight frame, never rejects) — wired in `src/instances.ts` as **getters through the instance**, because the redirect is constructed before the `FuseSession` and a rewind hands the same redirect a new control server.
    - **`CCU_FLAG_RELEASE_ONLY` (0x08, DIRTY):** release the claim and reconcile nothing. `pt_release` sends it when `fd_dirty[fd]` is clear — a `flush` already landed the bytes, so the releasing frame has nothing left to carry. Without it **every written file uploaded twice**, once at `flush` and once at `release`, because the releasing frame is also a reconciling one. A DEDICATED BIT and not a flag combination: one bit, one meaning, one op. **`policy_abandon_claim` carries it too**, and that is the other reason it exists: an abandoned claim — a mutating op that took one and then failed before mutating — has cc's own unmodified cache copy in the mirror and nothing to reconcile, and a BARE frame is indistinguishable from `pt_release`'s frame for a handle that wrote and never flushed. Without the bit cc pushes that copy, and a failed push records a divergence for a file the worker never wrote and freezes the cache on it for the session (T13c). **The killed-process backstop is preserved by the condition itself** — a handle whose `flush` never ran, or whose `flush` refused, still has `fd_dirty` set and therefore sends a FULL reconcile, which is the whole of what is owed when there is no `close(2)` left to answer. cc answers a release-only frame before any source call and **does not clear a fault**, which is consistent in both directions: a diverged `flush` returned non-zero, so `fd_dirty` is still set and `release` sends a FULL reconcile — and that reconcile's own outcome decides.
