It fails again and the fault stands, or it lands and the fault is cleared, which is the only way a fault is ever cleared.
    - **After a successful push cc re-stats the source and stamps the MIRROR entry with the mtime the source now carries** (`#adoptWriteMtime`). An atomic write gives the source a fresh mtime the fingerprint has never seen, so the next open would re-download the file the worker just wrote. One round trip on the write path, saving a whole download on the following read. The **mirror** is stamped and never the source: setting the source's mtime to the mirror's would misreport when the source changed, which build tools on that machine depend on. **AND IT IS GUARDED, because it is the only writer of a fingerprint that does not materialise the bytes it describes** — every other one stats the source BEFORE copying, so a source change during the copy leaves the fingerprint stale and the next FETCH conservatively re-copies; this one stats AFTER. The fingerprint is recorded only while the source's size still equals the mirror's, so a box-side writer landing in that window cannot have cc pair the mirror's bytes with another writer's `(size, mtime)` and then serve the worker its own stale bytes as the file's content. What a same-length race still costs is named above and is not defended against.
    - **WHAT THE TRANSPORT COSTS, and STATE THE CONFIGURATION, because a number whose configuration is unstated is a claim.** Re-measure the double reconcile's second upload with `node tests/fuse-transport-bench.mjs --case write-close --arm reference --sizes 64k,1m --runs 30 --release-only {on,off}` — the observed `writeFile` frame count per close sequence is **1 with the bit and 2 without**. Report the run count per size per arm alongside any figure taken from it. The bench's `docker` arm requires `--provider-argv` and `--remote` and has no defaults, so a figure from it names the remote it was taken against or it is not a figure.
    - **Two limits accepted by name, so neither is rediscovered as a bug.** (a) **Which syscalls the CLI's own `Write`/`Edit` emit at a project-tier path inside the union is UNMEASURED**, because `RUN_CLI_CONTRACT` has not been re-run against it. Mode preservation does not depend on the answer: it is inode-driven, so a rename replaces the mirror inode and the recorded mode is restored, while an in-place write keeps the inode and the mirror's mode is already the source's. The mechanism is proven by DRIVING the syscall sequence (T11), not by observing which one the CLI picks. (b) **The two-handle window stays deferred, and the premise is a STANDING CONDITION with a check** rather than an argument to re-derive — stated in full below.

      **THE TWO-HANDLE WINDOW.** `fd_claimed` is per-FD in the daemon while cc's claim is per-PATH, and `release` is the only releaser — so with two OVERLAPPING write handles on one path, the second handle's close releases the path's only claim while the first is still live. cc resumes cache management over a path with an open writable handle, and a `STAT` or a parent `LIST` can re-shape the mirror over the first handle's unpushed bytes, which its own `flush` then publishes.

      It is **unreachable while the marked population is the CLI alone**: it needs two overlapping write handles on one path, and the CLI's file tools are open→write→close sequential (Bash is proxied to the system by `bashForwarder`; an unmarked caller gets `-ENOENT` at the remote tier under `R_MARKED_STRICT`). **That premise is what expires**, so it is a standing condition, not a closed question. It holds **on three legs, none of which is about the source**: (1) the marked population is unchanged by the transport — marking is driven by `CC_UNION_MARK_PATH`, a resolution of the CLI binary's own path, not by where bytes come from, and the transport is a `System` handle *inside cc*, not a caller of the mount; (2) `atomic: true` means the write target is a fresh tmp path, so a create-write-rename is two paths and not two handles on one; (3) per-open revalidate cannot reach a claimed path, because `#fetch`'s claim short-circuit precedes `#fetchBody` and therefore precedes every source call, the freshness check included (pinned by T10b in `tests/fuse-transport.test.mjs`).

      **WHAT EXPIRES IT:** a second *marked* writer — mark inheritance, which this design forecloses because cc's plumbing IS the worker's process subtree and no ancestry cut separates them, or a second marked binary — or a CLI file tool that opens a project path for write twice concurrently.

      **THE CHECK IS NARROWER THAN "no overlapping interval".** A `CC_FUSE_TRACE=1` capture of a real turn, counted offline by `tests/fuse-trace-count.mjs`, reports distinct marked TGIDs (leg 1) and write-opens per project-tier path — the latter only because `tr()` carries the frame intent, so `open` at `cflags & 0x02` is a write open. It **cannot measure an interval**: `tr()` records no timestamp and `release` is not traced, so a write-open's WINDOW is not in the capture. The overlap question is therefore answered structurally and only while the count allows it — if no path has more than one write-open in the capture, then no two write-opens of one path can overlap, whatever their windows were. A path with two or more is reported **by name and without a verdict**; that is the case a timing instrument has to settle, and inventing an ordering from this capture is the failure the intent field exists to prevent.

      **The fix, when the premise expires, needs no protocol delta**: the daemon knows `fd_claimed` per fd and can keep `FOR_WRITE` set on a `release` while another claimed fd for the path survives, sending the releasing bit-clear `DIRTY` only from the last one. cc cannot do it alone — the frame carries no handle id.

      **The consequence accepted deliberately:** two handles on one path share the mirror inode, so a second reader sees a writer's in-progress bytes. That is ordinary POSIX — a filesystem giving each handle a private snapshot would be the surprising one — and it is recorded, not fixed. It is also the boundary of what freshness buys: `#fetch` copies **unconditionally** — `#shape` may skip a file whose size and ms-floored mtime already match, but the copy after it always runs — so freshness *against the source* holds at every open, while isolation *within one session* does not, and a second `FETCH` would copy over unpushed bytes.

    **THE HANDLER TOUCHES THE MIRROR AND THE SOURCE ROOT, NEVER THE MOUNT**, which is what makes a daemon↔cc deadlock structurally impossible: a FUSE thread is blocked on the reply to the frame being served. `STAT` shapes a **sparse stub** (`truncate`d to the source's size, mode and mtime carried) so metadata is faithful and no bytes move; `LIST` shapes every child and removes the ones the source lost, so `readdir` shows no ghosts; `FETCH` always copies, with no revalidation shortcut.

    **`DIRTY` IS A RECONCILE, not a write-back**, and that generality is what lets the whole set of mutating ops land through ONE frame instead of a frame per op: it means *make the source entry at P match the MIRROR entry at P, whatever the mirror now holds there — including nothing*. The daemon mutates the mirror and then says the mirror is authoritative, and `mkdir`, `unlink`, `rmdir`, `symlink`, `chmod`, `truncate`, `utimens`, each end of a `rename`, and the ordinary write-then-`release` are all that one sentence. Mode and mtime cross because they are part of the entry being matched. **A rename is therefore NOT source-side atomic**: it is a mirror rename followed by TWO INDEPENDENT reconciles, one per end, and a failure of the second leaves the source holding **neither** end — the old name already unlinked, the new one never created. A single `renameat2` on the source would be one syscall; this is not that, and the failure is reported rather than hidden. **ITS DOMAIN IS EXACTLY WHAT `RemoteStat` EXPRESSES** — file, dir, symlink, absent — and the three ops outside it (`mknod`, `link`, `chown`) are REFUSED by the daemon rather than applied to the mirror alone — `mknod` with `-EPERM`, a container refusing a device node being the permissions reading a caller already expects; `link` and `chown` with the shared `-EOPNOTSUPP` (`policy_unreconcilable`) — because a device node has no faithful mirror representation, a hard link is an alias where a reconcile copies, and the system's uid space is not the orchestrator's. **No project-tier mutation reports success having landed nowhere.** A failed reconcile fails the op; making that divergence *sticky* is the write path's own rule (below).

    Serialised per path — the PATH alone, with the op deliberately out of the key, so two frames naming one path never run concurrently. **That is all it does, and it is NOT protection for an open writable handle**: the worker writes through the mirror inode directly, cc sees no frame at all between the open's `FETCH` and the release's `DIRTY`, and there is therefore nothing at that path for the queue to order. Guarding that window is the claim record's job, not the queue's.
    **A FILE CREATED THROUGH THE UNION IS TRACKED UNTIL ITS PUSH.** It exists in the mirror from `create` and on the source only at `release`, and libfuse issues a `getattr` immediately after every create — so without that record the post-create stat answers `ABSENT`, the create fails, and `STAT`'s stale-entry removal deletes the file the worker just wrote. Found at the real gate (R3), pinned deterministically in `tests/fuse-control-channel.test.mjs`.
  - `remoteSource.ts` — the one interface behind the handler (`stat`/`list`/`fetch`/`remove`/`push`), and `localDirSource`, **the deterministic source**. It proves the channel, the mirror discipline and the tier policy, and **no transport, no latency and no `System` call**. It is no longer production — `systemSource.ts` is — but it is KEPT, with three live jobs: the unit suite's source (`tests/fuse-control-channel.test.mjs`, no provider, no latency), the real lifecycle gate's (criteria 3 and 4 are only checkable when the remote's bytes DIFFER from the host's at the same path, and that gate must not need a container), and **the measurement control** the transport's cost is reported against. `CC_FUSE_SOURCE_OVERRIDE_ROOT` selects it and moves its root; `src/instances.ts` is the ONE reader of that variable and reports it on the session's own stream at every launch, because a session using it is not talking to its system at all.
  - `systemSource.ts` — **the production source: `RemoteSource` over a real `System` handle**, and the only source that crosses a wire. **No root argument, and that is the whole point**: a path `P` from the daemon IS the path on the system, so there is no `root + P` arithmetic and no place for one to be wrong. `stat` is one `lstat`; `list` is ONE `readDir`, which is what the widened `SystemDirent` bought (a name-and-kind listing costs a round trip PER CHILD); `push` is `writeFileBytes(atomic, mode)` for a file — the `mode` is what makes it preserving — `mkdir` + `chmod` for a directory, `symlink` for a link, and it RETRIES ONCE via `removeEntry` on `EISDIR`, the one kind change a rename cannot land over. **Only `ENOENT` and `ENOTDIR` become `null`**: `remoteSource.ts`'s header records what conflating absence with unreachability costs — an EMFILE read as "absent" removed a live mirror entry and then, at the reconcile, a live SOURCE file. `ETRANSPORT`, `EACCES`, `ETIMEDOUT`, `EUNKNOWN` are each `{error}`, which the handler turns into REFUSED and no mutation.
  - `preflight.ts` — `assertFuseAvailable`, criterion 9. The probes run in root-cause order (`/dev/fuse` → `sudo -n` → the bootstrap binaries → `fusectl` in `/proc/filesystems` → `gcc` + `pkg-config fuse3` → the binary compiles), each throwing `httpError(501, …, {code:'FUSE_UNAVAILABLE'})` naming the FIRST failure and the package that supplies it. **Assert, never install.** Probes are injected, so the refusal path is testable with each dependency stubbed absent.
  - `build.ts` — `ensureUnionBinary()`: lazy `gcc` compile to `<store>/systems/fuse/bin/union-<sha256(union.c + policy.h + flags)>`, content-addressed (an edit to EITHER source rebuilds; a stale binary is impossible), single-flight under `withLock`, compiled to a temp name and renamed. A compile failure is the same `FUSE_UNAVAILABLE` refusal carrying gcc's stderr. **`policy.h` is in the address because it is a header** — nothing else observes an edit to it, so hashing `union.c` alone would leave a stale binary at a live address. `detectToolchain()` is the ONE detection: `ensureUnionBinary` builds through it and the C policy test skips through it, so that test cannot skip where the product would have compiled.
  - `tierTable.ts` — **THE** tier/deny artifact, one table with **two consumers holding the same array**: `renderPinsFile` (the daemon's pins file) and `classifyForTool` (the hook's deny surface). They must be one artifact or they drift, and the mechanism is **identity, not equality** — `src/instances.ts` calls `buildTierTable` once and hands the same `TierEntry[]` to `SessionRedirect` and to `buildFusePlan`; neither builds its own.
    **Five kinds**, longest prefix wins at a component boundary (the same rule as `union.c`'s `tier_of`): `host` / `project` / `hide` / `fail` / `bind`. Each entry also carries **`toolAccess`** (`allow` for `project`, `deny` for everything else, except a `localRoots`-derived entry, which carries the bit its declaration gave it) and a **`why`** that renders into both the pins file's comments and the refusal a worker reads.
    Seeded from cc-owned facts: `localRoots` (read, not restated), the claude binary, its realpath and the realpath's install chain (`installPins` — the common ancestor where it clears the 3-component floor, otherwise the realpath's ancestor chain floored the same way; the **command** side is never chained, because it is walked before the mark fires), `process.execPath`, `selfProjectDir()`, `projectsRoot()` (one prefix covering the cc repo, the store and the plugin dirs), the whole `os.homedir()`, the `/etc` + TLS + loader/NSS/gconv set (`ETC_PINS` and `LOADER_OBJECTS`, `src/systems/fuse/tierTable.ts`), `hide` on the run dir and `project` on the project's real remote path.
    **`localRoots` is a DECLARATION, not a list**: `LocalRoot = {prefix, access, why}`, one construction site (`src/instances.ts`), and **the file-tool ALLOW set is that array and nowhere else** — there is no second list of allowed prefixes in cc. Every entry is host-pinned for the daemon whatever `access` says.

    **The per-remote CLI config farm needs NO tier-table entry — do not add one.** `<store>/claude-config/<dirName>/.claude/` is already covered, at its exact spelling, by `add('host', input.projectsRoot, …)`: `orchStoreRoot()` is `projectsRoot()/.code-conductor` by construction, so the store sits inside that one prefix. The farm's *entries* are symlinks into `$HOME`, which `add('host', input.homeDir, …)` pins whole and separately — the union follows a pinned symlink and the target has its own tier, the same property `realpathsOf(ETC_PINS)` relies on. What `localRoots` DOES contribute there is `access`, not reachability: the session's own `<cfg>/plans` is `allow` (the CLI resolves plan mode's `Write` to `<configDir>/plans`, so denying it breaks plan mode outright for every remote-backed worker), its own `<cfg>` is `deny`, and `claudeConfigFarmRoot()` is `deny` beneath both so longest-prefix lets a worker reach its own config dir while refusing every other remote's. Pinned by `A9b` (the cross-remote deny) and `A9c` (that both sides of every link carry a tier) in `tests/systems-file-tool-refusals.test.mjs`.
    **TWO MECHANISMS, NEVER MERGED, and both now reach the daemon.** `BIND_MOUNTS` (`/proc`, `/sys`, `/dev`) is a **separate constant** and renders as kind `bind`, because `bootstrap.sh` binds the orchestrator's own over those three paths and a `mount --bind` onto a target the daemon answers `-ENOENT` for kills the launch. A provider's `exclude` renders as kind `fail`, derived only from the advertisement, and **only for entries inside the mirror root** — one outside it is *inert* (criterion 4) and renders nothing, having already been reported on the session's stream. Where the two coincide (`/proc` excluded), first-occurrence-wins keeps `bind`; the tool is denied either way.
  - `tierTable.ts` → `classifyForTool(entries, {exclude, mirrorRoot, systemId, systemPath}, p)` — the hook's half. Returns `allow`, or `deny` with one of **four classes** and its wording: `excluded` (`excludedRefusal`, `src/systems/mirror.ts`), `outside-mirror-root`, `bind-mount`, `host-pinned`. **It never touches the filesystem** — deciding by probing the mirror would deny the first read of every file in the project, since the union materialises a path when the CLI opens it. **All four name Bash**, and what differs is what each has to say about the answer Bash gives. `excluded` and `outside-mirror-root`: Bash reaches the path on the system under no such restriction. `bind-mount`: additionally whose KERNEL, since a file tool there would answer about the orchestrator's. `host-pinned`: additionally which MACHINE, because the same path exists on both — see the wording contract below for why that qualifier is load-bearing. All four state that this is cc refusing to carry the file and **not** the file being absent.
  - `plan.ts` — `FusePlan`, the immutable per-session geometry. **It takes the tier table, it does not build one** (`FusePlanInput.tiers`) — see `tierTable.ts` above for why that is identity rather than style. Plus `fuseRunDir()`/`fuseRunDirName()` — the run directory is named with the **whole instance id**, and `fuseRunDirName` is the identity. It stays a function because it is the one place the id→name mapping is spelled: `sweepFuseSessions` **derives** its readdir filter through it while the orphan backstop keys on the whole id (`CC_FUSE_INSTANCE_ID` off `/proc/<pid>/environ`), so the sweep carries two sets — equal today, derived rather than assumed so the pair survives the name shape being revisited. The whole uuid is what licenses the sweep's *"a fresh uuid per process, so anything left under `run/` at boot is dead by construction"*. `runTeardown`'s `report.instanceId` falls back to `path.basename(rundir)` — now exact — only when neither `intent.json` nor `mount.json` is readable. **No length constraint reaches this file:** the control socket is addressed through a directory fd, not by its path (`control.ts` above). Plus `fuseEventStore()`, `EVENT_LOG_NAME`, `resolveTraceEnabled()`, the **configuration-time** `FUSE_CWD_NOT_NORMALISED` refusal (see the cwd chain above — the daemon refuses the same value at mount, but *this* is the layer where the failure is legible) and the **configuration-time** `FUSE_REMOTE_ROOT_CONTAINS_MIRROR` refusal — a source-override root that contains `<rundir>/mirror` makes cc read its own staging mirror back as remote content and serve it to the worker. **Two exemptions, both exercised**: no override at all (production — a path `P` from the daemon IS the path on the system, so there is no arithmetic for a containment to break), and an override of `/` (there `<root>/P` is `P`, so cc reads the mirror only for a `P` at or inside it, and `runDir` is `hide`, so `route()` answers `-ENOENT` before any control frame is sent). `tracePath` is `<rundir>/trace.log` when `CC_FUSE_TRACE=1` and `''` otherwise — the daemon's `CC_UNION_TRACE`, opt-in because `tr()` resolves ids per op and reads `/proc`. **The bootstrap must not set `CC_UNION_TRACE` EMPTY**: the daemon tests the pointer, and an empty string is non-NULL in C, so it would `fopen("")` and refuse to mount on every ordinary spawn. **`CC_UNION_MARK_PATH` carries the same hazard and closes it at the daemon instead**: an empty value passes the `if (!mark_path)` NULL test, and `mark_maybe`'s `strcmp` then matches no routed path, so the mount comes up and marks nobody — the silent host-only filesystem the presence refusal exists to prevent. `union.c` therefore also runs `policy_cwd_normalised(mark_path)` (the path the kernel hands the daemon is always absolute and normalised, so no other spelling could match anyway), and `InstanceManager._doCreateResolved` refuses `FUSE_LAUNCHER_UNRESOLVED` before that, where `CLAUDE_BIN` can be named. **The switch and the path have DIFFERENT NAMES, and that is what makes the off path safe.** `CC_FUSE_TRACE` is cc's own flag, keyed exactly on `'1'`, and cc is its only reader; `CC_FUSE_TRACE_LOG` is the path `wrapLaunch` writes into the PLAN file, named only when the plan has one — an emitted `export CC_FUSE_TRACE_LOG=''` is inert under the bootstrap's non-emptiness test, but it is a value where the plan means an absence. **Two guards keep an operator's value out of that slot, and neither is the names being different**: `planVars` is composed from `plan` and `ctx` alone, so nothing in `spec.env` is an input to it, and the name is in `PLAN_KEYS`, so it is stripped out of the worker file, which bootstrap.sh sources LAST and which is `{...process.env}` as `instances.ts` built it. The precondition that would have to come back is a producer feeding that object from `spec.env`: an operator's `CC_FUSE_TRACE=0` — the natural way to turn a thing off — is in `spec.env` at every launch, and under one name it lands in the path slot, where the bootstrap's non-emptiness test reads it as ON and the daemon gets `CC_UNION_TRACE="0"`. The distinct names are cc's own reader contract, not that guard. The off path is byte-identical to before the trace existed **for an unset switch**; for any other value it is byte-identical only because both ends are keyed apart. `MOUNT_OPTS` fixes `attr_timeout=0,entry_timeout=0,negative_timeout=0` — zero kernel caching, because FUSE's attribute cache is per-inode and not per-caller.
  - `wrap.ts` — `wrapLaunch`, **pure**: it returns a `{command,args,cwd,env}` spec AND the bytes of two files, and writes neither. `sudo -n unshare --mount --propagation private -- bootstrap.sh <plan-env-path> <cc-uid> <the CLI's argv>`, cwd rewritten to `/`. **Nothing rides through sudo** — `-E`, like a `VAR=value` argv prefix, needs the sudoers `SETENV:` tag — so `spec.env` is `{PATH}` alone (what `spawn` needs to resolve the bare `sudo`; `env_reset` discards the rest) and the two loads travel in files under `<rundir>`: `env.plan.sh` (the `CC_FUSE_*` plan, sourced at bootstrap.sh step 0) and `env.worker.sh` (cc's own environment for the CLI, sourced at step 10, immediately before the `exec`). The worker set is `spec.env` minus `PLAN_KEYS`, because the late source would otherwise win every collision — and `procScan.ts`/`sweep.ts` read `CC_FUSE_INSTANCE_ID`/`CC_FUSE_RUNDIR` out of `/proc/<pid>/environ`. `renderEnvFile`/`shellQuote` emit one `export` line per name, single-quote-wrapped so every byte round-trips, sorted, skipping (and naming) a name that is not a shell identifier. `FuseSession.wrap` is the impure adapter: it writes both at `0600` into the `0700` run directory `#prepare` created, before it returns the spec `spawn` gets. bootstrap.sh's step 0 refuses either file if it is missing, mis-owned or group/other-writable, and step 10 unlinks `env.worker.sh` the moment its one reader — the `.` — is done, because it is cc's whole environment and nothing else ever reads it. **It does not survive a bootstrap run at all**: step 0's `EXIT` trap covers every `die` from step 1 to step 9, so a union that never mounts strands nothing either. What neither covers is an external SIGKILL, which runs no trap — `runTeardown`'s reclaim of `<rundir>` is what covers that. `#prepare` puts **every level cc creates on the way to the run directory** at `0700` (`fuseStoreChain` + the run directory), because `ensureUnionBinary` makes two of those levels on its own way to `bin/` and write access at ANY of them is write access to the environment files' directory NAME. Applied at the launcher seam (`RealClaudeLauncher.launch`'s `wrap` option); the in-process test launcher ignores it.
  - `bootstrap.sh` — runs as root in the new namespace and **execs at every link**, so cc's three pipes pass through and the final pid is the CLI's. Ordered: **step 0** — verify the two environment files cc wrote AND the directory holding them (each a regular file / a directory, cc-owned, not group- or other-writable), capture `rm` absolutely, source the plan file (whose path arrives positionally with cc's uid), and arm an `EXIT` trap that unlinks the worker file →  re-assert preflight (including that cc's control socket exists) → scaffolding → the **namespace anchor** → start the daemon → wait on `/proc/self/mounts` (never `mountpoint -q`) → **capture the connection minor from `/proc/self/mountinfo` while the mount exists** → write `mount.json` atomically → `fusectl` at a private path (not `/sys/fs/fuse/connections`, which would hand the worker an abort surface against its own filesystem) → bind `/proc`, `/sys`, `/dev` OVER the union, onto the three `bind`-tier synthetic nodes → source the worker environment file and unlink it, HOST-SIDE and before the `exec chroot`, which is why it needs no spelling inside the root → `chroot`, `cd`, `setpriv`, exec. **No name the worker file can set is READ BY that exec line** — every name in the file does reach the exec, as the CLI's own environment, which is the channel's whole point; what must not happen is the shell reading one. Every value step 10 still reads is stashed in the POSITIONAL PARAMETERS across the `.`, which an `export NAME=value` line cannot reach — otherwise an `export CHROOT_BIN=…` in cc's own environment would replace the path step 1 resolved and this shell would hand it to `exec` as root. For the same reason **no command name is resolved against `PATH` after the source**: `rm` is captured absolutely at step 0 and stashed, so a caller-supplied `PATH` (cc's Backends settings put user pairs in that environment) cannot make the unlink an exit 127 with the mount and the handshake already made. Its chroot'd script **fires no marking event** — the mark is the CLI's own `execve`, and the `cd` is answered by the cwd chain (see the pre-mark window above). It owns **no** teardown.
  - `driver.ts` — `MountDriver`, the narrow seam every process/mount observation and signal goes through, and the reason the state machine is testable with no sudo and no FUSE. **Pid discipline**: only numeric pids the caller captured, re-verified against `/proc/<pid>/stat` field 22 immediately before signalling. No kill by name or pattern anywhere behind it.
  - `session.ts` — `FuseSession` and `runTeardown`, the **single** teardown implementation (the boot sweep runs the same function). See "FUSE teardown" below.
  - `sweep.ts` — `sweepFuseSessions()`, called from `server.ts` beside `sweepSessionTmpDirs`: the record pass, then the record-independent backstop.
  - `procScan.ts` — the `/proc` enumeration the clean verdict and the backstop both rest on: membership by `/proc/<pid>/ns/mnt` or by `CC_FUSE_*` in `/proc/<pid>/environ`, two uid passes unioned, fail-closed, `ok: false` when it could not run.
  - `orphans.ts` — reclaiming a process no record covers: unmount through it, re-verify, then signal. Used by the intent-only teardown path and by the boot sweep.
  - `system.ts` — the interface. Three MUST primitives (`exec`, `readFile`, `writeFile`) plus the operations a remote provider will DERIVE from `exec` (`readFileBytes`, `stat`, `readDir`, `realpath`, `mkdir`, `removeTree`, `unlink`, `chmod`) — cc-side helpers, not provider surface. Semantics worth knowing before calling: **`stat` returns `null` for an absent path** and throws for anything else (absence is a value, a broken installation is not); **`unlink` removes one directory entry and never follows it**, `removeTree` is `rm -rf` — which one a call site uses is cc's decision, and a path cc does not own must never reach `removeTree`; `writeFile` takes `{atomic}` (temp+rename) or `{exclusive}` (EEXIST rather than overwrite), never both.
  - `localSystem.ts` — `LocalSystem`, the in-process `local` built-in: each method is the `node:fs`/`runGroupedCommand` call a call site would otherwise make, behind the interface. Also the home of `writeFileAtomic` (re-exported by `src/projects.ts`, where most callers import it) — cc's store is always local, so its atomic write and this system's are one operation.
  - `protocol.ts` — the **executable half of the wire spec**, shared by cc and any provider written in JS: frame types, `PROTOCOL_VERSION`, the `CHUNK_BYTES`/`MAX_FILE_BYTES`/`MAX_LINE_BYTES` constants, `NdjsonDecoder`, capability reading, and the error taxonomy (`SystemError`, `PROTOCOL_ERROR_CODES` + `FS_ERROR_CODES`, `classifyStderr`, `execFailure`). **A malformed line is FATAL, not skipped** — `push` throws `EPROTO` and drops its buffer, because a stream that has proved it cannot be framed cannot be trusted for the frames after it. **A payload is part of its frame**: `dataB64` on a `stdout`/`stderr`/`data` frame must be present and canonical base64, checked here for both ends and both directions, because `Buffer.from(s,'base64')` stops at the first unreadable character and returns the prefix — decoding leniently turns a corrupted chunk into a `writeFile` that reports success having dropped its tail, or a command whose stdout is quietly truncated with exit 0. Unknown frame types and unknown capability keys are IGNORED: that is the extension point that lets the contract grow without a version bump.
  - `execCollector.ts` — `ExecOutputCollector`, the output accounting for one `exec` (per-stream `StringDecoder`, the `cap` tail that also clips the interleaved `output`, the `headCapBytes` head cap that keeps DRAINING after it stops retaining, the `maxBufferBytes` fence that is a FAILURE rather than a truncation). **Shared by both implementations of the primitive** — `runGroupedCommand` and `ProviderSystem.exec` — so a caller cannot tell them apart and a fix in one cannot miss the other.
  - `providerConnection.ts` — `ProviderConnection`: spawn, handshake, multiplexing by id, teardown, restart. Supervision is **restart-on-demand with a fail-fast backoff**: a dead provider fails every in-flight operation with `ETRANSPORT` at once, the NEXT operation relaunches, and an operation inside the backoff window (100 ms doubling to 5 s) is **refused rather than queued**. A connection cc **disposed** is the exception and is TERMINAL: `dispose()` sets a flag that `#teardown` deliberately does not — a crashed provider must still reconnect, which is this class's whole contract — and every later `ensureUp` on it throws `ETRANSPORT` instead of respawning the old argv. `ensureUp` is single-flight, so one failed launch is one failure however many callers were waiting on it — the backoff is derived from that count. The handshake MUSTs are ENFORCED, not assumed: an id-carrying frame before the hello, a second hello and a protocol-version mismatch are each `EPROTO` at the handshake. **There is no `system` descriptor to validate** — the hello's required, refusal-enforced `system.shell` is gone with the long-lived shell, which was its only reader and `os`/`pathSep`/`home` had none even before that. A handshake that is torn down BETWEEN resolving and being recorded (a second hello or an immediate exit arriving in the same chunk) fails the connect with the real reason — recording it would mark the connection up with no child behind it, and every later operation would be written into a closed pipe and wait out its deadline. The child and its stdio are **unref'd**, ref'd only while an operation is in flight, so an idle connection can never hold the event loop open — which is what lets a test process exit while a provider is still connected.
  - `providerSystem.ts` — `ProviderSystem`, the `System` implementation over the wire. Three primitives on the protocol; `stat`/`lstat`/`readDir`/`readlink`/`symlink`/`removeEntry`/`realpath`/`mkdir`/`removeTree`/`unlink`/`chmod` **derived from `exec`** (`stat -L -c '%f %s %.3Y'`, `find <p> -maxdepth 0 -printf '%y\t%m\t%s\t%T@\t%l\n'`, the same fields plus `\t%f` for a listing, `readlink -v`, `ln -sfnT`, `rm -d`, `realpath -e`, …), and `writeFileBytes` beside `writeFile` on the write primitive. **`lstat` and the widened `readDir` are one round trip each**, which is the whole reason they carry mode, size, ms mtime and the symlink target rather than name-and-kind: with name-and-kind a listing of N children costs 1 + N round trips to answer the same question. `%m` is permission bits alone, so both implementations reconstruct the type bits from the kind (`typeBitsFor`, `src/systems/system.ts`) and a kind cc cannot name reports none on either side; `%T@` is `seconds.nanoseconds`, parsed as TWO INTEGERS and never as a float, and **both implementations derive whole milliseconds through the one `msFromNanos`** — the local side from a `bigint` stat's exact nanoseconds — so the two are comparable exactly rather than within a tolerance. Rounding each side in its own arithmetic converged by luck and missed about once in 5000, on a half-millisecond boundary. **`writeFileBytes` is the whole of the binary fix and needed nothing from a provider** — the wire already carried base64 of raw bytes, and the only lossy step was cc's own `Buffer.from(data, 'utf8')` on the way in; `writeFile` now delegates through it. **No `exec` cc issues carries an `env` frame field** — cc's own plumbing and a caller's command alike run in the provider's own environment — the far side's PATH and toolchain, which across a boundary is the only environment whose paths exist; sending cc's made the target's own toolchain unreachable and put cc's whole environment, a live session token among it, in front of every far-side process. Derived commands run under `env LC_ALL=C …` because `env(1)` ADDS to that environment where a frame `env` would replace it, keeping the `strerror()` text `classifyStderr` matches on; a caller needing a variable there ships it in argv the same way, as the post-worktree hook's `CC_*` vars do. `opts.env`, when a caller names one, still REPLACES. `exec` **never rejects**, matching `runGroupedCommand`: a dead channel becomes a `spawnError`. **No operation is unbounded, with no exceptions**: every `exec` arms a cc-side deadline (`timeoutMs + 5 s` when the caller named one, a generous `defaultOpTimeoutMs` ceiling when it did not), and `readFile`/`writeFile` carry the same ceiling and fail `ETIMEDOUT` — without it a provider that completes the handshake and then goes mute leaves project listing and git status pending for ever, with no `ETRANSPORT` and no refusal. A redirected shell command is an ordinary bounded `exec` like any other, carrying `ProviderShell`'s per-command ceiling (next bullet) as its `timeoutMs`; the long-lived `openStream` that armed nothing went with the long-lived shell. The ceiling is a liveness fence, not a performance budget; it is a constructor option so a test can assert it without waiting for it. The `find <p>/.` trailing dot is load-bearing — `find <file> -mindepth 1` exits 0 with no output, which would read as an empty directory instead of `ENOTDIR`.
  - `shellFraming.ts` + `providerShell.ts` — the **redirected shell**, framed by cc rather than by the provider: `{ <cmd>\n} < /dev/null` plus a sentinel carrying `$?` and base64 `$PWD`, with a **random 128-bit nonce per command** and **first-match-wins** (a CONSTANT nonce was measured desynchronising the parser when a command echoed the sentinel). **The nonce's per-command FRESHNESS is not load-bearing, and only its randomness is** — stated because the obvious rationale rests on freshness, and the world that rationale needs is not this one: freshness matters where a single byte stream carries every command and a forged sentinel can move the NEXT command's boundary, whereas each command here has its own `exec`, stream and parser and a forgery is confined by construction. A nonce shared across a session's commands would break nothing. What survives as load-bearing is the PAIRING — a command must be parsed with the nonce it was framed with — which is lexical today (one `const` per call in `#runOneShot`) and is what the concurrency tests would catch if it became a per-run field two overlapping commands could overwrite. **ONE `exec` PER COMMAND** — the shape a local `Bash` call already has, and the only shape there is now that the long-lived shell is gone. Each framed command **opens with a sentinel of its own on both streams** and everything before it is discarded — that is what keeps a profile-file banner out of a command's output, and it is load-bearing on EVERY command, because every command gets its own shell and its own copy of that banner. It is also what makes the framing **interpreter-agnostic**: measured on Linux 6.17 / bash 5.2.37 / dash 0.5.12 as `/bin/sh`, under `bash -lc`, `bash -c`, `/bin/sh -c` and `/bin/sh`: identical `$?` propagation, identical `$PWD` capture, and the preamble discarded in every case — so the interpreter is the provider's choice and a login shell is not required. **The sentinel's `$?` is the only exit code there is**, and is NOT redundant with the `exit` frame: measured, the framed script's own `exec` exits 0 while the user command exits 2, because the script's last statement is a `printf`. **EVERY sentinel — opening and closing, both streams — is emitted with an injected leading newline**, because a sentinel only counts when it STARTS a line and what precedes it may have none: a command ending in `printf err >&2`, or a profile printing an unterminated banner. Without it the marker glues to that text, never matches, and the command runs to its deadline instead of returning — on every command, because every command gets its own shell and its own copy of that banner. The parser strips the newline back off the CLOSING sentinels only; before an opening one everything is discarded anyway. **CAPTURE, NOT CARRY**: cc reads `$PWD` back out of the sentinel so it can TELL the worker where its command ended, and feeds it to nothing — every command starts at the project root. Carrying it forward on the one-shot path (`#cwd` → the next `exec`'s `cwd`) is a second deletion parity required, not a code path already in place — which is why "the code path already exists" was the wrong reading. **THE PER-COMMAND CEILING NOW DOES ONE JOB** — the longest a command may **run** — and the value is therefore simply correct for it, where a ceiling doing three jobs could only say no value was right: `DEFAULT_COMMAND_TIMEOUT_MS` (605 000 ms = `BASH_TOOL_MAX_TIMEOUT_MS` 600 000 + `SHELL_TIMEOUT_SLACK_MS` 5 000, `ORCH_SHELL_COMMAND_TIMEOUT_MS` overrides it, `commandTimeoutMs` is the test seam). Its other two jobs — the longest a **wedged** shell stayed wedged, and the longest a queued command **waited** for its turn — were the counter-pressure that wanted it small, and both went with the long-lived shell and the queue. It is enforced by the PROVIDER, as the `exec` frame's own `timeoutMs`, where every other operation's ceiling already lives — **but it is NOT the only bound, and the earlier claim that it was is measured false.** When the provider reports nothing at all, cc's own abandon timer (`providerSystem.ts`, `timeoutMs + EXEC_TIMEOUT_SLACK_MS`) is what fires; it reports its own computed value as `ExecResult.abandonedAfterMs`, and `providerShell.ts` names THAT number, because it is longer than the deadline the provider was given and naming the ceiling told a worker it had waited a time it had not. **The caller cannot move it, and no longer offers one**: the tool `timeout` a redirected `Bash` carries does not reach cc at all, and cc needs it for nothing. At the tool timeout the CLI **DETACHES** the forwarder and hands the agent a background task — measured at CLI 2.1.258, for a rewritten forwarder command and for the same command left un-rewritten alike, and whether its output was flowing or silent — so the command keeps running and THIS ceiling is what bounds it, which is why it sits above the documented max rather than at it. **NO ABORT REACHES cc AT A DETACH** — measured at CLI 2.1.258, the forwarder's socket stays open and still accepts frame writes long past that timeout, so cc has nothing to act on. A **kill** of the forwarder (an interrupt, or a stopped background task) closes the socket and is cc's cancellation channel, carrying no number either. The mirroring defect it avoids is this number becoming the deadline, which kills the command at the instant the agent was handed a pointer to it. **WHAT SETTLES A COMMAND IS cc's OWN CLOSING SENTINEL, not the `exit` frame**. The reference provider emits `exit` from the child's `'close'`, which fires when the STREAMS close — so a `cmd &` job that inherits the command's stdout pipe holds it open and the exit is never reported, and a command that exited 0 was handed to the worker as a FAILURE with an EMPTY stdout at the abandon timer above, while the complete parsed answer had been on the wire since ~154 ms. Reporting `exit` at PROCESS exit instead is not the fix and was measured losing up to 64 KB of a command's output to an undrained stream, with no zero-heuristic way for a provider to know a drain is done — so the fix is at cc's seam, where the sentinel is an exact boundary needing no grace timer. `ProviderShell` passes `completeMarker` (the closing sentinel) on `ShellExecOptions`; `ProviderSystem.#exec` scans each stream for it with the parser's own rules — line start, complete line — and settles when BOTH have it (stdout carries `$?` and the cwd, stderr carries a bare marker, and `#runOneShot` throws `ESHELLGONE` unless both parse). **`completeMarker` lives on `ShellHost`, never on the shared `ExecOptions`**: `LocalSystem.exec` takes the same options type and would accept-and-ignore it, so a caller could not tell which semantics it got; `ShellHost` has exactly one implementor. Settling then sends the **`detach` frame** — the operation is over, kill nothing — rather than `close`, which is abandon-and-kill-hard; without it the provider keeps the exec open with its timer armed and reaps the survivor when that fires. **That also closes a divergence between the two shipped capability configurations**: `close` reaps the background job only where `processGroupSignal` gives it group reach, so without the settle the same redirected command leaves a survivor on one system kind and not the other. Failure modes: `ETIMEDOUT` (the ceiling expired and the provider killed the command, OR the provider never reported the exit and cc abandoned it — the message says which), `ESHELLGONE` (the command destroyed its own framing — an `exit`, a syntax error — so no sentinel arrived; immediate, measured 17 ms against the 1504 ms a long-lived shell took on the same input, which is the whole of why the WEDGE CLASS IS GONE), `ECANCELLED`, `EFBIG`.
  - `referenceProvider.ts` — **the local machine over the protocol**, with every optional capability settable by flag: `--no-process-group-signal` and `--remote <id>=<absolute root>` (repeatable — given at least one it advertises `remotes`, requires every `exec`/`readFile`/`writeFile` to name a known target (unknown or absent → an **id-addressed** `ENOREMOTE`), scopes each target to its own root, and injects `CC_REMOTE=<id>` into every child), plus `--mirror <[id=]absolute root>` and `--exclude <[id=]absolute path>` (repeatable) — the **mirror advertisement** (`docs/systems-protocol.md` §2.1), advertised as `remoteDescriptors` iff at least one is given, so with neither the wire is byte-identical. Those two are deliberately **independent of `--remote`'s root**: that root is a *fence* (outside it → `EACCES`) while an advertised mirror root is a *claim about geometry* cc consumes, and keeping them apart is what lets a test advertise a mirror wider than the fence and prove cc reads the advertisement rather than deriving it. The root scoping and `CC_REMOTE` exist because the provider runs on cc's OWN machine, where a wrong-target operation would otherwise still find the file and still go green: they make a misroute refusable and a correct route positively assertable. **The `exec` cwd fence exempts the exact path `/`** — cc's placeholder for a derived command, which carries its real target in `argv` (see `docs/systems-protocol.md` §7); what binds those instead is the `remoteId` on every frame. With no `--remote` flags nothing is injected and behaviour is byte-identical. **It reports `exit` from the child's `'close'`, i.e. when the STREAMS close, not when the process exits** — measured as the ONLY choice that does not drop output (reporting at process exit lost up to 64 KB of an undrained stream), and the reason cc settles a redirected command on its own sentinel instead of waiting for the frame. Its `detach` handler is `close` minus the kill: it marks the exec closed, clears the deadline and drops the entry, and **leaves the `'data'` listeners attached with the streams flowing**, so a survivor holding the pipe is drained-and-discarded rather than blocked (`pause()`) or SIGPIPEd (`destroy()`) — both measured, both divergences from a local background job. Dropping the entry also matters for teardown: `shutdown()` group-kills every exec still in the map. **fd accounting, measured: 2 fds per stream-held exec, and this is unchanged by `detach`** — the pipes never EOF while a survivor holds them, so they are released when the provider exits (stdin EOF → `shutdown()`), exactly as they already were after a `close`. Not a transport: it exists so the protocol's sufficiency is TESTABLE (see Testing → the systems gate), and as the worked example a third-party provider is written from. **The root fence is this provider's property, not a protocol obligation** — the conformance suite never exercises it (its only `EACCES` is a `chmod 000` fixture), so `docs/systems-protocol.md` §10's launch surface documents the flags without it.
  - `registry.ts` — `resolveSystem(projectName)`: the one place a project name becomes a System. It returns the `local` handle when the placement is local and otherwise defers to `systemById` below, which either hands back a live handle or refuses BY NAME. Refusing rather than falling back to `local` is the point: a fallback would run every operation for that project against a path on the wrong machine and report success.
    - `placementOf(projectName, location)` — **the pin, and the one reader of a record's `location`**, pure and taking an already-read location (`readProjectRecord`) so a caller holding one does not read it twice. `projectPlacement(name)` is the async wrapper and answers `null` for an unregistered name. It returns `{system, remoteId, path}` with **`path` ALWAYS set** — a local project's location carries a path exactly as a remote one's does, which is what makes the two indistinguishable everywhere downstream. A `local` location forces `system: 'local'` and `remoteId: null`: cc's own machine is one machine, so a target named on it names nothing. `remoteId` — **which TARGET of that system**, when one endpoint serves many (ten containers behind one `docker exec` provider) — absence is the provider's own default target. The `.conduct` pin is on the MACHINE: a record naming a system for it is ignored, its path is still the record's. The tuple that identifies a project is `(system, remoteId, path)`.
    - **`.conduct` is pinned local unconditionally**, ahead of any record lookup, INSIDE `placementOf` — so the pin holds for every reader of a project's system at once: resolution, the listing, and the registry's still-referenced check. It IS the orchestrator, its dir is cc-owned under `projectsRoot()` and it drives every other project over `127.0.0.1` MCP. A record claiming `.conduct` is elsewhere is ignored, not honoured; `tests/systems-conduct-pin.test.mjs` constructs exactly that record and fails if the pin line is removed.
    - **The LISTING form lives in `src/projects.ts` as `tryResolveProject(projectName)` → `{system, unreachable}`, not here**, because a listing needs the project resolved and not merely its system — a record naming a REACHABLE system with no `systemPath` resolves its system fine and still has no tree to measure. See `src/projects.ts` below for the contract and its four consumers.
    - `MANAGED_SYSTEMS` + `SystemRecord` (`{id,label,managed,launch?}`) — the code-authoritative registry rows (just `local`), consumed by `appSettings.getSystems()`. Also the home of `CONDUCT_PROJECT_NAME` (re-exported by `src/conduct.ts`), so the pin needs no import of the conductor bootstrap.
    - `systemById(id, subject)` — a registered id onto a LIVE handle, and the one place a non-local system is reached. One `ProviderSystem` per id, cached on (id, launch argv): a handle is a connection, so two would be two provider processes claiming to be one machine, and an edited command must replace the handle rather than leave the old process serving the new configuration (`disposeSystemHandle` / `disposeSystemHandles`). Dropping the entry cannot reach a handle a live worker session already RETAINED — `SessionRedirect`'s `#system` and `Instance._redirectPlacement.system`, the same object, fixed when the session was created. What contains that is `dispose()` being terminal on the CONNECTION (above): the retained handle is refused rather than serving the pre-swap machine, so an edited command breaks every live session on that system at its next operation — and **respawn, rewind and prune do not recover one**: they relaunch through `Instance.launch`, which reuses the retained placement. Only a NEW instance re-resolves, because `attachRedirect` has one call site, in `_doCreateResolved`; that is the recovery the refusal message names. It CONNECTS before returning, which is what keeps the degraded listing honest — `tryResolveProject` turns the refusal into the row's `systemUnreachable` reason, whereas a lazily-connecting handle would fail every fact separately with nothing to say why. Three refusals, each its own code because each is a different repair: `SYSTEM_NOT_REGISTERED` (501), `SYSTEM_NO_PROVIDER` (501), `SYSTEM_UNREACHABLE` (502). All three are marked as a family, readable via `isSystemRefusal(e)`, and **`runGit` raises two more into it**: `GIT_DID_NOT_RUN` (502) for a command that never ran, and `GIT_TIMED_OUT` (504) for one whose answer never arrived — distinct codes because the repair differs (fix the path or the binary vs. fix the provider). **A spawn error is not a git result, and neither is a command that never answered.** The timeout half is: an expired `exec` reports `{code:124, stdout:''}`, so `isGitRepo` read it as git ANSWERING and the project listing published `isGitRepo: false` with `systemUnreachable: null` — the positive claim "not a git repo" about a box cc never heard from. Both throw, for the same reason: a `code: 1` return with the transport diagnostic in `stderr` is byte-identical to git having run and said no, so "the command never ran" becomes a fact about a repository: an invented worktree absence, a git-conflict code carrying a transport cause, and safety checks whose FAILURE reads as PASS. `runGit` throws instead, because 57 call sites already treat a non-zero code as git's answer and an unaudited one has to be LOUD. Which failure it is comes from **`ExecResult.transportFailure`**, set by the wire layer that knows its own code paths (`ProviderSystem`'s `ensureUp` catch and `down`), NEVER from the message text: a transport failure's message embeds the dying provider's stderr TAIL, so classifying by substring reads the corpse as the diagnosis — a provider that dies of, or merely logs, an errno was taken for the far side answering about a command, and the throw was skipped. That decays further, because the tail is captured into the backoff refusal every later call gets. Only the **command-level** branch (an id-addressed `error` frame, plus a local spawn error `classifySpawnError` recognises) keeps FS classification, and there a missing cwd or a non-executable git is a real, local, actionable answer that must not be reported as unreachability. `LocalSystem` does not set `transportFailure`, but the branch turns on the classification too, so a local spawn error `classifySpawnError` does not recognise is `EUNKNOWN` and **throws on `local` as well** — that is the mechanism; the measured triggers are `spawn git EMFILE` under fd pressure, `spawn ENAMETOOLONG` from an over-long cwd and `spawn E2BIG` from an over-long argv, each reaching 502 `GIT_DID_NOT_RUN` on system `local`. What is unchanged locally is the CLASSIFIED case — a missing cwd or a non-executable git still returns, diagnostic in `stderr`. **The same rule binds every READER of `spawnError`, not just `runGit`.** The complete set, enumerated because this is the kind of fix that misses a layer:
    - **Producers:** `src/groupedCommand.ts` (local only, never flagged) and `ProviderSystem`'s two transport paths (the `ensureUp` catch, `down`).
    - **Readers that CLASSIFY, all flag-first:** `runGit`, `ProviderSystem.#derive` (under `realpath`/`stat`/`mkdir`/`removeTree`/`readDir`), `ProviderShell`'s start path. These are also the only three call sites of `classifySpawnError`. Before the flag, an ENOENT-shaped corpse made `adoptProject` assert `TARGET_NOT_FOUND` about a tree that was there, and an EEXIST-shaped one made `createProject` report a path as taken on a dead system.
    - **Readers that REPORT ONLY — correct as-is, do not "fix":** `project_bash` (prints the message, claims nothing), `execCollector` (copies it into `stderr`/`output`), and `runPostWorktreeHook`, whose `{ran: true, exitCode: null, error: true}` is load-bearing — `spawnDialog` gates the hook result's visibility on `hook?.ran`, so flattening it would delete a user-visible fallback (D10). The structured vocabularies convert: `mergeWorktreeIntoParent` (whole body, with `mayHaveCompleted` past the point `git merge` was issued), `syncWorktree`, `adoptProject`, MCP `mergeWorktree`, and both listing faces' per-row `computeGitFacts`. Everywhere else the throw IS the answer. `probeSystemLaunch(argv)` is the registration-time check: a throwaway handle, one handshake, disposed.
    - `src/systems/transcriptKey.ts` — replaced `sessionRoot.ts`. Its surviving job is the CLI **transcript-directory** guard. It compares the WHOLE directory — `path.join(transcriptRoot(place), encodeCwd(normalizeSystemPath(cwd)))` — on both sides, derived through `transcriptRoot` rather than re-spelled, so the guard cannot disagree with the thing it guards. Because each remote has a config directory of its own, what still collides is what genuinely IS one directory: two places on ONE target at one path, and two LOCAL places whose cwds encode alike. A remote and a local place at one path, two systems at one path, and two targets of one system are all registrable. `TranscriptPlace` therefore carries `remoteId` as well as `system`. `registeredPlaces()` derives each worktree's cwd with `worktreePathFor` (`src/worktrees.ts`), the same call `createWorktree` uses, and skips a row whose record could not be parsed, which names no directory anything could collide with. Refusal: **409 `TRANSCRIPT_DIR_COLLISION`**, naming the target rather than just the system when those differ, and raised at `registerProject` so every creation surface inherits it — reachable in a new shape now that projects may nest, since `<root>/A/b` and `<root>/A-b` are two directories the CLI encodes alike.
    - `src/systems/toolRedirect.ts` — `SessionRedirect`, the per-session POLICY, composing **one `ProviderShell` for the session** (see the shell lifecycle below). **The invariant it exists for: the boundary is consistent.** Every tool that can observe or mutate the system's tree is fully redirected or refused by name; a half-redirected boundary is measurably the worst outcome available, and is reproducible on demand by leaking. `Bash` → `updatedInput.command` naming the forwarder (POSIX single-quoting, so a command containing quotes, `$` or a newline arrives byte-identical; the tool's own `timeout` rides out **not at all** — mirroring it onto the per-command ceiling is deleted, as is the queue-wait bound it became, leaving cc with no use for the number). File tools (`FILE_TOOLS` — `Read`/`Write`/`Edit`/`NotebookEdit`) → **HOOKED TO REFUSE, AND FOR NOTHING ELSE**: the CLI runs inside the chroot, so at an allowed path it opens the system's own bytes at the system's own path and there is nothing to translate or pull. What the hook adds is the REFUSAL — `classifyForTool` decides from the session's tier table, in memory, and a path the union does not serve to this session is denied with a sentence rather than left to answer `-ENOENT`. Hooked to REFUSE for all four; hooked-but-not-GATED for `Read` alone — `REDIRECT_UNGATED_TOOLS` (`src/hookBroker.ts`) is `{Read}`, so in ask mode a redirected `Read` is auto-allowed while a redirected `Write`/`Edit`/`NotebookEdit` still raises a permission card, exactly as a local one does. The exemption is scoped to redirected sessions and is that one-element set, not a class of tools. `Glob`/`Grep` → refused BY NAME, and that one is not a leftover: a marked CLI's `Grep` spawns an UNMARKED `rg`, which the union routes as a stranger, so it would search the wrong side and return silently wrong results rather than failing. 
      - **Shell lifecycle.** **ONE `exec` PER COMMAND** — nothing is long-lived, nothing is keyed per agent, and nothing is serialised. The `ProviderShell` `SessionRedirect` builds on the first redirected `Bash` holds CONFIGURATION only: the project root every command starts from, the output fence, the ceiling. It replaced a shell per AGENT, keyed off the `agent_id` a subagent's `PreToolUse` payload carries, which existed so a subagent's `cd` could not re-base its parent's next command — one `exec` per command SUBSUMES that by construction and strictly strengthens it: no command's state reaches ANY later command, its own agent's included, exactly as a local session behaves. `agent_id` is therefore no longer read anywhere in `src/`; the CLI still sends it, and `tests/systems-cli-contract.real.test.mjs` still pins the payload shape as a CLI fact rather than as something cc depends on.
        - **What a worker is told when its `cd` is discarded.** `#cwdNotice` fires only when the command's captured `$PWD` differs from the project root, matching local's silence — a notice on every command is noise, and noise is itself a divergence. It arrives LAST on the sink, after the command's own output, because where a command ended cannot be known before it ends. There is NO double emission: the CLI prints its own `Shell cwd was reset` line only when ITS OWN shell's cwd moved, and both a subshell `cd` and a child process's `chdir` were measured leaving it silent (CLI 2.1.258, this host) — the forwarder is exactly that shape, a child the CLI's shell spawns. cc's wording is its own; no byte-identity with the CLI's is claimed anywhere.
        - **Concurrency is now unbounded by cc, deliberately.** Every command of a session overlaps every other. That holds because `ProviderConnection.#ops` is a `Map<id, handlers>` with monotonic never-reused ids and `#onData` routes every frame **by id before any decoder sees the bytes**, and each command owns its own parser — so two commands' bytes never enter one parser. Measured: 8 concurrent framed commands in 535 ms, 8 distinct far-side pids, every frame matched to its own command, zero cross-talk; and frames genuinely interleave on the wire (one read was observed carrying frames for two ids), which is what makes routing-before-parsing the load-bearing part. The per-command nonce defends forgery *within* one stream and is not what makes this safe.
        - **What bounds cc's heap with N commands in flight**, since the fence is per command and nothing serialises: the exposure is `N × maxOutputBytes`. **Unbounded in principle, small in practice** — N is whatever the CLI's own Bash concurrency is, realistically 1-10 (the model issues Bash calls a turn at a time, and a wide fan-out is a handful of subagents each issuing one), i.e. 8-80 MiB. It is strictly better than what it replaced: the pre-strip ceiling was `17 × fence` (the main agent plus the 16-subagent cap), ~136 MiB, and cc had no say in that either. Parity says the bound is the CLI's, and no cap is added here.
        - **There is no per-session file state left to share.** The pull/push bridge, its session-root cache and its dirty-path tracking are gone with the geometry: the union serves one tree at one path, so two agents editing one file is the same exposure as two agents editing one local file, with nothing cached in between.
      - **Cancellation is PER CALL** (`ShellRunOptions.signal`, threaded from the forwarder's socket closing). A command that is cancelled before it crosses never runs on the system at all; one already running is stopped through `ExecOptions.signal` — `ProviderSystem` sends `close` on THAT command's own `exec` id, which the provider turns into a hard kill of its group — and every other command is untouched. A cancelled command records nothing for the next one to be told about: nothing was reset, because nothing was shared.
        - **`close()` REAPS, and it RE-ARMS.** There is no shell process to close any more, so `SessionRedirect` holds its own `AbortController` and relays it, per call, into a per-call controller alongside the caller's signal — **explicitly attached and explicitly removed in a `finally`, and deliberately NOT `AbortSignal.any`**, which retains per-call state on the longest-lived input and was measured growing cc's heap linearly with the number of commands a session had ever run. Without it a command survived its session and ran to completion on someone else's machine with nobody left to read it — a defect measured as reachable in production on any provider that does not advertise `persistentShell`, which the strip would have promoted to the only behaviour. The controller is REPLACED on every `close()` rather than left aborted, and that is load-bearing: `close()` is not terminal — instance exit and DELETE never touch the redirect again, but a **rewind/respawn** calls it too and the same redirect serves the next turn, so a controller left pulled makes every later command fail `ECANCELLED` the instant it is issued. Found by six end-to-end reds in `tests/systems-remote-worker.test.mjs`, not by reasoning.
        - **THE R5 BACKGROUND-JOB DEVIATION DOES NOT APPLY, but the measurement behind that is narrower than a general claim.** A command is its own `exec` with its own id, so stopping one does not close a shell it shares a process group with — which is the mechanism that killed a `nohup … &` job and forced the R5 deviation. **What was actually measured is one form, on one side:** a `nohup … &` job, observed on the FAR SIDE only, through the reference provider on this host — it reparented to `ppid=1` and outlived its command. **Not measured, and not claimed:** a bare `&` job, a `disown`ed one, a job holding the `exec`'s stdout open (which could plausibly keep the `exec` from reporting exit, leaving a command that never settles and only the 605 s ceiling to end it), and the LOCAL comparison — no local run was made, so "as it does locally" is an inference from the fresh-shell-per-call shape, not an observation. Carded separately; this codebase's own rule that a negative assertion needs a positive control applies to the unmeasured forms, and they have none.
      - **Output fence.** `ProviderShell`'s `maxOutputBytes` (`DEFAULT_MAX_OUTPUT_BYTES` = 8 MiB in `toolRedirect.ts`) is a **failure**, not a truncation: past it the command is killed and the call throws `EFBIG`. cc accumulates a framed command's bytes in its own heap while the command runs — the parser's first-match-wins rule is about the whole stream — so `head -c 40M /dev/zero | base64` was measured taking the server heap from 29 MB to 822 MB, which kills every session on the host. It is passed as `maxBufferBytes` and read back as `ExecResult.outputOverflowed`. Same class, second site: `ProviderSystem.#read` fences on what cc **keeps**, not on the `length` it asked for — a provider is not trusted to honour a bound it was merely told, and a file can grow between the `stat` and the read.
    - `src/systems/bashForwarder.ts` — the local stand-in a redirected `Bash` actually runs. Node builtins only, spawned by whatever shell the CLI uses with `process.execPath` as argv[0]. A SEPARATE PROCESS rather than a shell one-liner for two reasons: the original command travels as one argv element, so no quoting of it survives into a second shell; and the CLI's kill of it closes the HTTP socket, which is cc's only signal to kill the command on the far side. **It writes as it reads**: the response is NDJSON, parsed a line at a time (a frame can be split across two TCP reads, and half a JSON object is not a frame) and replayed immediately onto stdout/stderr, so a long command reaches the worker while it is still running. A response that ends with no `exit` frame is a FAILURE, not a success — the connection was cut mid-answer and nobody knows how the command ended.
    - **Streaming, end to end.** `exec`'s `onChunk` is the streaming primitive and it always existed; what P5 first shipped was two one-shot hops between it and the model. The chain now is: the provider's `stdout`/`stderr` frames → `ProviderShell` (per-stream `FramedStreamFilter`) → `ShellStreamSink` → `SessionRedirect`'s `ForwardSink` → NDJSON frames on the open response → the forwarder's descriptors. There is one path and it feeds `FramedStreamFilter` from `ExecOptions.onChunk`, so the live view and the buffered result are parsed by the same rules and cannot disagree about what a command printed.
      - **`CLAUDE_CODE_TMPDIR`** is pinned per redirected session to `<store>/session-tmp/<instanceId>` (0700, created at spawn; reclaimed by `remove`, `removeAllForProject` and `shutdown`, and by a boot-time `sweepSessionTmpDirs([])` in `server.ts` — the last of those is what covers a KILLED orchestrator, which runs none of the teardown paths, and it is safe because an instance id is a fresh uuid per process so nothing under `session-tmp` can belong to a live session at boot). WHAT KEEPS THE PIN: `sessionTmpDir(id)` is one of the `localRoots` the create path builds (`src/instances.ts`), and that array seeds the union's HOST tier (`tierTable.ts`), so this exact directory is served from the orchestrator's own filesystem inside the chroot and the worker's task output means the same thing to the CLI and to cc. The per-uid default the CLI would otherwise use is not in that array. Its FORMER justification — the redirect refused any file path outside the session root, so a task file under the per-uid root was unreadable — died with the geometry, and no file tool is hooked to refuse anything now. Per session and 0700 because the CLI validates the override's ownership and mode, and one session must not read another's task output.
      - **`includeGitInstructions: false`** for a redirected session (`src/settings.ts`). The CLI shells out to run that git itself — unmarked, and outside cc's remote-forwarded `Bash` tool — and an unmarked caller never reaches the REMOTE working tree at all: it resolves in `VIEW_HOST`, where the `project` pins are struck, so it gets the ORCHESTRATOR's own file where the orchestrator has one and `-ENOENT` where it has none. Neither is the tree the session is working in, so the answer is wrong either way and in the shadowed case it is wrong about a *different repository*. **The chain changes only WHERE that spawn dies**: it starts, `chdir`s into the project root — the orchestrator's directory with the floor, or the overlay node — and then gets the orchestrator's answer at every path below, which for a remote-only `.git` is `-ENOENT`. It no longer writes a `deny` row: that spawn once produced exactly two `deny`/`unmarked-project-denied` rows (`<proj>/.git`, `<proj>/HEAD`) and that reason is unreachable for an unmarked caller — the rows are `served`/`unmarked-host-served` and the ENOENT is the host's own. Guidance derived from any of it is worse than none. (The ORIGINAL reason — the cwd was a cc-owned session root describing the wrong repository — died with the geometry.) Chosen over `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_GIT_INSTRUCTIONS`, whose test is `e !== undefined ? !e : …`: `"0"` disables while an **empty string re-enables**.
      - `onChunk` gained a second argument, the stream name (`ExecStream = 'out' | 'err'`), because the forwarder writes each stream to a different descriptor and cannot recover the split from an interleaved callback. Every other caller ignores it. It still fires **after** the caps have had their say (`ExecOutputCollector.push`), so a streaming consumer sees exactly what is retained — nothing past `headCapBytes`, nothing past the `maxBufferBytes` fence, and the fence is still a reported failure rather than a clipped success (`tests/grouped-command.test.mjs`).
      - **The framing is unchanged.** `FramedStreamFilter` (`src/systems/shellFraming.ts`) derives the safe-emit boundary from rules the parser already has, and its contract is byte-equality with the parser under *every* chunk split — pinned exhaustively (one byte at a time, and every single cut point, across bodies including one that echoes a live sentinel). Three things are held back rather than emitted: a tail that is a PREFIX of the sentinel at a line start; a COMPLETE sentinel at a line start whose line has not ended (its tail decides boundary-or-forgery); and a trailing newline at the very end of the buffer, which may be the one cc injected before the sentinel and the parser strips. The third is why a paused command's last line arrives without its newline until the next output — emitting it eagerly would put a byte in the stream that the buffered result does not contain, and byte-equality is the contract. `flush()` releases the held bytes when no sentinel can arrive any more (the command took the shell with it, or its deadline passed), still withholding any fragment of the framing: that output has no frame to be parsed out of, so the stream is the only channel it has.
    - `src/systems/bashRules.ts` — `BASH_RULES_NOT_ENFORCEABLE`. Measured: a `permissions.deny` entry like `Bash(touch:*)` IS enforced under `bypassPermissions --allow-dangerously-skip-permissions`, and rules match the POST-hook input — so once every command is rewritten into one forwarder invocation, no `Bash(…)` rule can tell two commands apart and the rule applies to nothing. cc does NOT reimplement the matcher (the CLI splits on `&&`/`|` and matches each part; a cc-side prefix matcher would let `npm test && rm -rf /` past a `Bash(rm:*)` deny while claiming the rule was honoured — false assurance about a safety rule is worse than the collapse). It refuses the spawn instead, naming each rule and its file. A BARE `Bash` deny is unaffected: it removes the tool rather than discriminating between commands.
      - **Sources** (`bashRuleSources`), shared by both scans below: the project's own `.claude/settings{,.local}.json` READ THROUGH ITS `System` HANDLE (`scope: 'project'` — they live on the system; there is no local copy), `~/.claude/settings.json`, and `/etc/claude-code/managed-settings.json` — 2.1.250's layer list is `["userSettings","projectSettings","localSettings","flagSettings","policySettings"]` and the last of those is that file. The managed layer is read because it is the operator's least-revocable safety decision and the one that fails worst: a `Bash(touch:*)` deny there IS enforced today and would go silently dead. `flagSettings` is deliberately absent — that one is cc's own `--settings`.
      - **`REDIRECT_HOOKS_DISABLED`**, same scan, same moment, worse failure. Measured with cc's exact settings shape: `"disableAllHooks": true` makes the PreToolUse hook fire **zero** times and the worker's own command run on the orchestrator's machine while every result claims the system. It does **not** disable `permissions.*`, so cc's injected denies still fire and nothing fails loudly — the session silently diverges. No malice needed: `.claude/settings.json` is the project's own, read off the system on every spawn, so a user who turned hooks off locally and committed the file is enough. Only `true` counts. (The other suppression shapes were tested and do **not** work: `{"hooks":{}}`, `{"hooks":null}`, `{"hooks":{"PreToolUse":[]}}` all leave the injected hooks firing.)


  **Where the seam sits.** `resolveProjectDir` resolves the system alongside the path and returns it (`{path, system}`), so `getProject` hands every caller a handle; path-taking git helpers (`runGit`, `isGitRepo`, `hasUnbornHead`, `worktreeDirtyLines`, `getHeadBranchAndSha`, `getProjectUpstreamStatus`, `getWorktreeMergeStatus`) take a `System` as their first argument; name-taking entry points (`createWorktree`, `syncWorktree`, `mergeWorktreeIntoParent`) resolve it internally — and the ones that take a project-or-worktree pair (`getProjectCommits`, every entry point in `src/gitDiff.ts`, and the `project_status` / `project_read` / `project_bash` MCP tools — `project_diff` REQUIRES a worktree and resolves its own via `getWorktree` + `resolveSystem`) go through the one exported `resolveProjectCwd` (`src/worktrees.ts`), which answers `{cwd, worktreeMeta, projectPath, system}` and throws a 404 for a worktree unregistered under that project. A worktree is on the same system as its parent by construction, so naming the pair is the ONLY addressing form that reaches one: a worktree has no record of its own in `<store>/projects/`, so nothing resolves its name as a project. `ProjectInfo` deliberately does NOT gain a handle — both REST and MCP spread it into a response body. **`runGit` is derived from `exec`, not a system operation of its own**, which is why one signature change converted the whole git surface. cc's own paths — the store, `~/.claude/**`, `<projectsRoot>/.worktrees/`, `<projectsRoot>/.plugins/`, self-update — stay direct local calls: they are not on any system.

- **src/projects.ts** — FS ops on `<projectsRoot>/`, transcript-path resolution (`claudeConfigDir`, `claudeProjectsRoot`, `remoteConfigDir`, `transcriptRoot`, `encodeCwd`), CLAUDE.md seeding. **`resolveProjectDir(name)` is THE resolution chokepoint** every project path in the app comes from, and it is **ONE record read with no filesystem probing**. `readProjectRecord(name)` answers `null` for an unregistered name and the record's `location` otherwise; `placementOf` turns that into `{system, remoteId, path}`, and the path IS the answer. **THE RECORD IS THE REGISTRATION for every kind of project**, so resolution does not depend on the tree still existing — which is what lets a vanished, unmounted or unreachable tree still list and still be deregistered instead of reading as a project that never existed, and what stops a same-named directory in the projects root shadowing a tree that lives elsewhere. A record that exists and cannot be PARSED **throws 500 naming the file, never 404**: with the record as the sole registration, degrading it to an empty one would silently unregister a live project. It also resolves the project's **System** and returns the handle beside the path (`{path, system}`), so `getProject` hands every caller the seam every project-scoped operation goes through — see `src/systems/` above. `getProject` is `validateName` + that, plus the 404; `tryResolveProject(name)` → `{system, unreachable}` is `resolveProjectDir` for the LISTINGS, **where a refusal is a value rather than a throw.** Every listing fans its per-project work out through one `Promise.all`, so an unguarded resolve inside it lets one project's refusal reject the whole batch and break the list for every OTHER project — contradicting the guarantee `listProjects` already makes for a record it cannot parse ("the rest of the project list must still render"). It resolves the whole PROJECT rather than just its system, because a record naming a REACHABLE system with no path resolves its system fine and still has no tree to measure. **Four consumers:** `computeGitFacts` (`src/routes.ts`, behind `GET /api/projects`), MCP `list_projects` and `list_sessions`' group headers (`src/mcp/handlers.ts`), and plugin discovery (`src/plugins/registry.ts`, where an unresolvable project contributes no plugin rather than falling back to reading cc's own disk at the same path). An **addressed-by-name** caller keeps `resolveProjectDir`/`resolveSystem` and their throw — there the refusal IS the answer. It catches everything, not just the refusals: a listing owes the rest of the list the same duty for an unexpected fault. The degraded row is **kept, not dropped** (a project that vanishes reads as deleted) with `systemUnreachable` carrying the reason, `isGitRepo` **absent rather than `false`** (`undefined`, which `deviations` skips and `JSON.stringify` omits — "could not look" is not the claim "not a git repo"), an all-null `mergeStatus`, and its worktree registrations still listed (store-derived, no System needed) with undiverged merge status. Pinned per site by `tests/systems-listing-degrade.test.mjs`. A fifth consumer, `findSelfProject`, skips such a row outright: it has no path to compare. **`listProjects({includeConduct?})` is STORE-DERIVED and reads the projects root not at all** — one row per `<store>/projects/<name>/project.json` that parses, `validateName`-filtered, with `.conduct` skipped unless a caller opts in (`findSessionLocation` and `listArchivedGroupedByProject` do, rather than synthesising a row). That is what makes a container directory in the root possible: nothing in the root is a candidate. A record it CANNOT parse is still listed — with an empty `path` and a `degraded` reason — because invisible would also mean undeletable, which is the disappearing row the degraded-listing contract exists to prevent. `ProjectInfo` is `{name, path, workspace, system, remoteId, degraded?}`: `path` is the ONE path field (the tree's path on whatever machine it is on), there is no kind flag, and there is deliberately no system HANDLE, because both REST and MCP spread it into a response body. `readProjectRecord` returns `{workspace, location}` and THROWS on a malformed file; `writeProjectRecord` merges over its result, so a location the reader forgot would be deleted by the next unrelated write — and with the location gone the project is unregistered. **`registerProject(name, location)` is the ONE guarded writer** every creation path funnels through (`createProject`, `adoptProject`, the Library install, the `.conduct` bootstrap): `validateName` → dot-leading refusal (`.conduct` excepted — `.worktrees`, `.plugins` and the store are cc's own, and MCP `create_project` reaches creation through `validateName` alone) → held-name → transcript-key collision → write. A guard the one chokepoint does not enforce is not an invariant. **`setProjectRemote(name, remoteId, {liveInstanceIds})` is the one mutation path for the target**, so every surface (`PUT /api/projects/:name/remote`, MCP `set_project_remote`) inherits its guard rather than each carrying a copy. It refuses **409 `PROJECT_PLACEMENT_IN_USE`** while the project has live sessions or registered worktrees, NAMING both.
