A backgrounded `Agent` tool call's tool_result resolves immediately (`isAsync:true`), so `turn_end` (→ `idle`) can fire while the CLI's own `system/task_started`/`task_updated` task-tracking is still open for that call. `Instance._activeAgentTasks` (a `Map<task_id, tool_use_id>`) tracks this: incremented on `task_started` **unless** `task_type === 'local_bash'` (backgrounded/promoted Bash — a promoted long-lived command never emits a terminal event and would pin the count forever; unknown `task_type`s still count), decremented on a terminal `task_updated` (`patch.status` one of `completed|failed|cancelled|error` — an unrecognized status is treated as non-terminal on purpose, to avoid flipping back to idle early) or on `task_notification` (belt-and-suspenders). `summary().displayStatus` reads `'running'` instead of `'idle'` while the map is non-empty; every other `status` value passes through unchanged. Reset on (re)spawn. `Instance.activeAgentTaskCount` exposes the live map size. `IdleSubscriptionHub._onTurnEnd` **gates on TWO conditions**: a `turn_end` **defers** the idle wake (the armed wake and its heartbeat both stay armed) while **either** `activeAgentTaskCount > 0` (a subagent is still running) **or** `taskNotificationPending` is set — a `task_notification` fired *during* the turn now ending **and no top-level tool_result followed it**, which per the CLI's queue semantics means the notification is still queued and an unprompted **re-invocation turn** will deliver it. Both cleared, the wake fires — so a wake means "the worker AND all its subagents finished, and no re-invocation turn is still owed." `Instance._taskNotificationPending` (getter `taskNotificationPending`) mirrors the CLI's stdout-invisible message queue by event order: SET in the `task_notification` handler only when `status === 'turn'` (a completion while *idle* re-invokes immediately — that turn is its processing turn); CLEARED by any top-level (`parentToolUseId == null`) `tool_result` event — a sync-delivered task's own held-open result (fast Agent calls, long Bash promoted to a task, e.g. a full test run) or the outer tool round-trip the CLI attaches queued notifications to (batched flush) — by every idle→turn transition in `_setStatus` (the dequeued notification IS the new turn's input), and on (re)spawn. Nested (subagent-forwarded) tool_results never clear it. Every status-gated frontend control still keys off the real `status`; the cosmetic surfaces (sidebar dot, sub-agents strip, header chip) read `displayStatus`.

**Idle task-drain settle (the second wake path).** A background task can finish while its worker is ALREADY idle with **no re-invocation turn ever following** (verified trace: a subagent's nested Monitor completed after the subagent — the stream's last lines were `task_updated` + `task_notification`, no `turn_end` ever re-sampled the gate, and the wake orphaned into an endless heartbeat). `IdleSubscriptionHub._onTaskEvent` runs on every `task_updated`/`task_notification`: when a watched target is fully drained (`status === 'idle'`, `activeAgentTaskCount === 0`, `!taskNotificationPending`, `!idleWindowDirty`) it arms a **settle timer** (`ORCH_IDLE_DRAIN_SETTLE_MS`) recording the target's proc identity and `ring.nextSeq`; `_fireSettle` delivers the wake only if the subscription still exists, the SAME proc is live and still fully drained, and `ring.nextSeq` is **unchanged** — literally zero events since the arming task event. The trap this must not reintroduce is waking ONE TURN EARLY: an idle completion that DOES get a re-invocation turn announces it with CLI-local `system/init` + `system/status` writes within ms of the notification, while the `message_start` that flips `status` to `'turn'` waits on the API (seconds — no settle length can safely outwait it). Re-invocation evidence is therefore caught structurally on both sides of the drain event: **after the arm** by the frozen-seq check (init/status advance the ring before the timer fires → drop, the re-invocation's `turn_end` owns the wake), and **before the arm** by `Instance._idleWindowDirty` (getter `idleWindowDirty`) — SET when any non-task-lifecycle event (`TASK_LIFECYCLE_SUBTYPES` = task_started/updated/notification) is processed while `status === 'idle'`, CLEARED at `turn_end` and on (re)spawn — covering a task that drains *inside* another task's already-opening re-invocation window. Every failed re-check drops the settle silently (the wake and its heartbeat stay armed): the fail direction is always "wake later via turn_end", never "wake early" or "wake twice". The proc pin exists because respawn/rewind reuse the Instance AND its id but mint a new proc and reset the ring (`_wipeForResume`), making cross-run seq comparisons meaningless; history replay can't arm (it runs while `status === 'spawning'` and always trails a seq-advancing `history_replayed` event) and can't corrupt the dirty flag (replay bypasses `_handleStdoutLine`). `_justConsumed` stays turn_end-only — no worker `turn_notification` exists at settle-fire time. Accepted residuals: a CLI stalling longer than the settle between the notification and init writes (consecutive process-local writes, normally sub-ms — the window never scales with API latency) would wake one turn early; non-task noise in an idle window with no turn following leaves only the heartbeat, same as before this path existed. A **UI model switch** is now such noise: `system`/`model_changed` is retained, so a `setModel` landing inside the settle window advances `ring.nextSeq` and freezes the settle out. The pre-arm `idleWindowDirty` guard cannot see it — that flag is set only in `_handleStdoutLine`'s loop, i.e. by subprocess stdout, and `setModel` emits through `_emitUi` directly. Cost is bounded to **delay**: the subscription and its heartbeat stay armed, so the wake falls back to the heartbeat (`ORCH_SUBSCRIBE_TIMEOUT_MS`) or to the next `turn_end`, and `_fireSettle`'s contract — never early, never twice — is untouched. Pinned by the characterization test named `characterization: a UI model switch inside the settle window delays the wake, never loses it` in `tests/idle-drain-settle.test.mjs`. Rejected alternatives: declining the kind from the ring (kills the transcript notice on re-subscribe), emitting it seq-less on the `setModel` path only (asymmetric retention for one kind, and a re-subscribe loses the notice), and narrowing the freeze check by kind (it is deliberately event-shape agnostic, and weakening it to buy a human-timed race reopens the wake-one-turn-early bug).

## On-disk state
All orchestrator-owned state in a single workspace-wide dotfolder — the central store `<store>` (= `<projectsRoot>/.code-conductor/`):
```
<projectsRoot>/                             # projectsRoot()
├── .code-conductor/                        # central store
│   ├── workspaces.json                     # registry of known workspace names
│   ├── session-titles.json                 # {sessionId: customLabel} sidecar
│   ├── session-summaries.json              # {sessionId: {short?,medium?,long?}} AI-summary sidecar
│   ├── conducted-sessions.json             # {sessions:[sid,…]} — durable conducted-session markers
│   ├── session-modes.json                  # {sessions:{sid:mode}} — the mode a resume inherits; NO backfill, so an absent entry means DEFAULT_RESUME_MODE
│   ├── pending-resume.json                  # transient — "Resume after restart" manifest; written on drain, read+unlinked on boot
│   ├── pending-temp-cleanup.json            # transient — temp-cleanup manifest (normal restart); swept+unlinked on boot
│   ├── systems/<systemId>/
│   │   └── retired-session-roots-<ms>/     # ONLY on an install upgraded past the FUSE-union geometry:
│   │                                       # the pre-union local session roots, MOVED ASIDE by
│   │                                       # migrations/0034 rather than deleted (a failed write-back
│   │                                       # could have left edits in one that exist nowhere else).
│   │                                       # Review and remove by hand; cc never writes here again.
│   │                                       # SEPARATELY, and NOT under the store: an install upgraded
│   │                                       # past the per-remote config dir leaves every pre-upgrade
│   │                                       # remote transcript at `~/.claude/projects/<encodeCwd(
│   │                                       # systemPath)>/`. cc no longer reads those — they stop
│   │                                       # listing, locating and resuming. Nothing is deleted;
│   │                                       # removing them is the CLI's (it prunes after ~30 days) or
│   │                                       # the user's. There is deliberately no migration.
│   ├── session-tmp/<instanceId>/            # a redirected session's CLAUDE_CODE_TMPDIR — where its
│   │                                       # backgrounded commands' task output lands. Swept at boot
│   │                                       # (sweepSessionTmpDirs), per instance id.
│   ├── claude-config/<dirName>/.claude/      # one CLI config dir per (system, remoteId) — remote-backed
│   │                                       # workers only. `dirName` is a sanitised slug plus a 12-hex
│   │                                       # digest of the coordinate (a raw remoteId may contain `/`
│   │                                       # and `..`). The trailing `.claude` is load-bearing: the CLI
│   │                                       # resolves plans at `<configDir>/plans`, and planFile.ts
│   │                                       # recognises a plan file by the `/.claude/plans/` fragment.
│   │                                       # Every top-level entry is a SYMLINK into the host's real
│   │                                       # config dir except these: `projects/` (a real, private
│   │                                       # transcript root), `.claude.json` (left for the CLI to
│   │                                       # create — its lock is not realpath-resolved, so sharing it
│   │                                       # loses writes, and its cwd-keyed `projects` map would leak
│   │                                       # allowed tools and trust acceptance between remotes),
│   │                                       # `.credentials.json` (CLAUDE_SECURESTORAGE_CONFIG_DIR
│   │                                       # instead) and `backups/` (nothing but `.claude.json`
│   │                                       # backups, which the CLI's missing-config recovery would
│   │                                       # otherwise offer for restore across configs). Rebuilt
│   │                                       # before every spawn by `ensureRemoteConfigDir`
│   │                                       # (src/claudeConfigFarm.ts); never deleted on unregister —
│   │                                       # it holds transcripts.
│   ├── systems/fuse/                         # the FUSE-union chroot (remote-backed workers only)
│   │   ├── bin/union-<sha256>                # the compiled daemon, content-addressed on union.c + policy.h + compiler flags
│   │   ├── events.log                        # THE STORE-WIDE POLICY EVENT LOG (`fuseEventStore()`), a SIBLING of run/
│   │   │                                     # because run/<id> dies with its session and this is what outlives it:
│   │   │                                     # <iso8601>\t<instanceId>\t<kind>\t<op>\t<path>\t<pid>\t<tgid>\t<comm>\t<cmdline>
│   │   │                                     #   \t<suggested list>\t<suggested entry>,
│   │   │                                     # the last two for `deny`/`unpinned-fail-closed` rows only (a pin does
│   │   │                                     # not fix an `unmarked-host-served` row). Append-only, never rotated.
│   │   └── run/<instanceId>/                 # ONE directory per session, named with the WHOLE instance id
│   │       │                                 #   (`fuseRunDirName`) — an instance id is a fresh uuid per process, so
│   │       │                                 #   anything here at boot is dead by construction.
│   │       ├── root/                         #   The chroot / union
│   │       ├── mirror/                       #   mountpoint; the daemon's remote tier, materialised by cc over the
│       │                                 #   control socket below (a local fake remote, no transport)
│   │       ├── fusectl/                      #   fusectl, namespace-local — NOT /sys/fs/fuse/connections
│   │       ├── pins.txt                      #   the tier table, generated by tierTable.ts
│   │       ├── intent.json                   #   written by cc BEFORE spawn: a crash before the handshake is recoverable
│   │       ├── mount.json                    #   {stage:'starting'|'mounted'} — written THREE times, as soon as the
│   │       │                                 #   bootstrap has any pid to name, so nothing it starts precedes the
│   │       │                                 #   record naming it. Pids + their /proc/<pid>/stat field 22
│   │       │                                 #   starttimes, the connection minor captured AT MOUNT TIME, the mount ns id.
│   │       │                                 #   Rewritten with {wedged,terminalState,residualMounts,at} and KEPT when
│   │       │                                 #   teardown could not finish; deleted with the directory when it could.
│   │       ├── control.sock                  #   cc's control socket. A SIBLING of root/ and tiered `hide`, so nothing
│       │                                 #   inside the chroot can name it THROUGH THE UNION — `route()` answers
│       │                                 #   -ENOENT before any frame is sent. NOT structural containment: the
│       │                                 #   bind-mounted /proc reaches it at /proc/<ccpid>/root/<rundir>/ today
│       │                                 #   (measured).
│       │                                 #   Its PATH is unconstrained: both ends address it as
│       │                                 #   /proc/self/fd/<dirfd>/control.sock through an fd on this directory
│       │                                 #   (`sunPathAddress`, control.ts), so no store-root depth can overflow
│       │                                 #   sun_path.
│       ├── events.log                     #   the POLICY EVENT LOG, a `#` header then
│       │                                 #   <kind>\t<op>\t<path>\t<reason>\t<pid>\t<tgid>\t<comm>\t<cmdline>, deduped on
│       │                                 #   (path, reason, tgid). `deny` = the op was refused; `served` = it succeeded, but
│       │                                 #   not the way the tier table said. The instrument the pin list is derived
│       │                                 #   from — HARVESTED into ../../events.log immediately before this directory
│       │                                 #   is reclaimed, because the reclaim destroys the only record.
│       └── daemon.log                    #   the daemon's stderr
│   ├── settings.json                        # app settings, e.g. {transcribe:{model}, models:{backends:[{id,label,template,env}],tierBackend:{[tier]:{backend,model}},roleBackend,customRoles:[name],customModels:[{label,model,backend,contextWindow}],enabledTiers,defaultTier,onOverage,overageThresholdEnabled,overageThresholdPct,conductorCompactWindow}, systems:{registry:[{id,label,launch?}]}, tts:{enabled,voice,rate}, spawn:{debugByDefault}}  (only USER backend rows are stored — managed rows are fully code-authoritative: id/label/template/env all come from code, nothing on a managed row is stored; customRoles is a name-only string list; a model's context window is never stored on a binding — it is catalog data for `claude` and registry data for any other backend, resolved at spawn; plugin roles are live-derived, never stored; `systems.registry` likewise holds USER rows only — the built-in `local` row comes from `MANAGED_SYSTEMS` in `src/systems/registry.ts`, is in-process and carries no `launch`)
│   ├── plugins/                              # plugin system state
│   │   ├── registry.json                     # {plugins: {<id>: {project, enabled, activeVersion}}}
│   │   ├── runtime.json                      # {<id>: {pid, pgid, port, startedAt, gitHead}} — adopt-don't-drain record
│   │   └── library/                          # Plugin Library drop-in manifests, one JSON file per entry
│   │       └── <anything>.json               # {id, name, description?, repo} — external entries override a built-in with the same id
│   ├── playbook-ledger.jsonl                 # APPEND-ONLY event log — the source of truth for worker stage state; the in-memory projection is folded from it (see Playbooks below)
│   ├── playbooks/                            # user-authored playbook definitions, one JSON file per id; overrides a built-in of the same id
│   │   └── <id>.json
│   ├── conventions/                          # convention stores, one per scope (migration 0020 relocated these here)
│   │   ├── conductor.json                    # {disabled:[…], rules:[…]} — the user's off-switches (seed/custom/plugin alike; everything else is on) + custom bodies; was conduct-modules.json
│   │   ├── workspace.json                    # {disabled:[…], rules:[…]} — the user's workspace-convention off-switches + custom (was workspace-modules.json)
│   │   └── project.json                      # {rules:[…]} — custom project conventions (was project-conventions.json → optional-guidelines.json)
│   ├── whisper.cpp/                          # voice-dictation build + models (optional, INSTALL_ROOT)
│   ├── piper/                                # TTS venv + voices (optional, INSTALL_ROOT)
│   └── projects/<project>/
│       ├── project.json                    # {workspace?: "<name>", location: {kind:"local", path} | {kind:"remote", system, remoteId, path}}  — PRESENCE OF THIS FILE IS THE REGISTRATION, and `location.path` is where the tree is on whatever machine it is on
│       ├── attachments/<timestamp>-<name>
│       ├── debug/<instance-id>/            # raw CLI capture
│       └── worktrees/<key>/                # <key> is the worktree's ONE name: also its worktreeName and its directory basename
│           ├── worktree.json               # {baseBranch, baseSha, branch, parentPath, baseWorktree?, …}
│           ├── attachments/
│           └── debug/<instance-id>/
├── .worktrees/<project>/<key>/             # EVERY local worktree checkout, wherever its project's tree lives
├── .plugins/<name>/                        # Plugin Library installs — ordinary projects, kept out of the area a user browses
├── <project>/                              # a project cc created here — nothing of ours inside
└── <container>/…/<project>/                # a grouping directory: only the registered leaves are projects
```
A project is a RECORD. Nothing under the projects root is listed because it is there — which is what lets the root hold container directories, cc's own dotfolders and a worktree area without any of them surfacing as a project, and what lets a project's tree live anywhere else instead. A record cc cannot parse still lists, with an empty path and a `degraded` reason.

Orchestrator state stays in the central store; the only cc-written file inside a project tree is every project's tracked, app-regenerated `CONVENTIONS.md` (`src/projectClaudeMd.ts`) — imported by its `CLAUDE.md` and committed with the project (its line-1 marker is the self-describing source of truth). That holds for an adopted target too, git repo or not: it is written at adopt time and on every sweep, landing in the user's own tree — as an uncommitted change where that tree is a repo. Creation also makes the tree a git repo (`git init`) and commits what it scaffolded — that `.git/` is git's own, not cc-written state. Adoption commits nothing. No per-project `.gitignore` plumbing.

## What `fileBridge` carried, and where it has to land again

`src/systems/fileBridge.ts` is **deleted** in the FUSE-union geometry: the filesystem decides which bytes appear at a path, so there is no pull in `PreToolUse` and no push in `PostToolUse`. Two of its semantics were not incidental and are not reproduced by a passthrough mount: they are the specification the daemon's write path has to meet, recorded here because the file that held them is gone.

**Mode preservation.** The bridge remembered the system-side `st.mode` at pull time and passed it back on push (`fileBridge.ts` `#modes`). The reason is `atomic: true`: an atomic write ends in a `rename`, and a rename hands the replacement file *fresh* permissions — so an edited shell script silently loses its executable bit. Any write-back that renames must carry the original mode across, or re-`chmod` after it.

**A failed push is loud and sticky.** On a push failure the bridge marked the local path diverged (`#diverged`) and the layer above **refused every later write to that path** until a pull resynced it. The reason is that the local file then holds content the system does not, and the worker has no way to see that: without the sticky refusal the divergence surfaces as a wrong belief rather than as an error. A write-back that can fail needs the same property — a failed `release` must poison the path, not just log.

**The provider `exclude` list's per-path refusal lives in `mirror.ts`** — `isExcluded` and `excludedRefusal`, consumed by `classifyForTool` (`src/systems/fuse/tierTable.ts`) rather than by a local path map. The wording contract is recorded here IN FULL, because a subset of it is not a contract — every clause earns its place against one failure mode, *a model that mistakes a refusal for file-not-found concludes the file is absent instead of using the channel that works*:
- **`cc will not bridge`** — names cc as the actor and the act as a refusal. Never "cannot", which reads as inability.
- **the PREFIX, not just the path** — so the model generalises instead of retrying sibling by sibling.
- **`NOT the file being absent — cc has not looked`** — the anti-ENOENT clause, twice: a denial AND a positive statement of ignorance.
- **`Bash runs on … under no such restriction`, with `cat` / `sed -i` / `>`** — the channel that works, with concrete verbs for both directions, delivered at the point of use.
- **the negative rule:** the refusal never claims the file was absent. It has its own assertion rather than a comment, because no positive assertion can catch a rewording that keeps every clause above and inverts the meaning — and the assertion forbids the CLAIM'S WHOLE VOCABULARY (`found`/`find`/`finds`/`finding`, `does not exist` in both spellings, `no such file`, `missing`, `not there`), not one inflection: a round-1 review found the single-word form surviving its own documented killer, because `cc could not find` contains no `found`. `absent` and `exists` are deliberately NOT forbidden — the contract's own clauses use both.
- **`Bash runs on <system>`, and every one of the four wordings carries it.** The host pin is not an exception. A **dead end** there that never mentions Bash, on the reasoning that Bash cannot see the orchestrator's files either, is **withdrawn reasoning, not softened**: the dead end buys no concealment, because a worker reaches the system's `~/` through `ls ~/` whether the sentence mentions it or not, and it costs the agent its next move. The four classes and the failure mode each clause earns its place against:
  - **`excluded`** — the system advertises the prefix as excluded. Names the prefix and points at Bash. *Failure mode: the model reads the refusal as file-not-found and concludes the file is absent instead of using the channel that works.*
  - **`outside-mirror-root`** — outside the boundary the system advertises. Names the root, points at Bash, and names **the project's own tree**, because a refusal that says only "not that one" costs a call to find out where the files are.
  - **`bind-mount`** — `/proc`, `/sys`, `/dev` are the orchestrator's own, bind-mounted so the CLI works. Points at Bash **and states whose kernel**. *Failure mode: a file tool there answers about the wrong machine, and the worker cannot tell.*
  - **`host-pinned`** — pinned to the orchestrator's machine so the local CLI can run. Points at Bash **and names the machine Bash answers from**. *Failure mode, and it is why the qualifier is load-bearing rather than decoration: the same path exists on both, so `cat ~/.claude/…` through Bash returns a real file that is the SYSTEM's, not cc's — and an unqualified "use Bash instead" would have the agent read it as the file cc just refused.* The wording therefore says which machine answers and that what it answers with is **not** the orchestrator's copy. Asserted in both directions by `tests/systems-file-tool-refusals.test.mjs` (A2b, A4) with the rule's own discrimination self-test, because a rule satisfied by the sentence it was added to forbid is worse than none.
- **the enumeration `Read, Write and Edit` inside the sentence is HISTORICAL, and must not be "completed".** It predates `NotebookEdit` being in the set, and the sentence is a **byte-for-byte** restoration whose verbatim-ness is itself a verified acceptance property — adding a word voids that claim for no gain. Tool coverage is guaranteed structurally instead, by `FILE_TOOLS` enumeration: `classifyForTool` is reached for every key of that map, and `tests/systems-file-tool-refusals.test.mjs` (A3) loops the exported map rather than transcribing it, so a fifth file tool is covered the moment it is declared.

`MirrorScope.exclude` is still live besides — `resolveMirrorScope` refuses `MIRROR_EXCLUDE_COVERS_PROJECT`, and the list is pinned per session and compared on relaunch. Containment is decided with `path.relative`, never a string prefix: a prefix test claims a merely prefix-*sharing* sibling (`/app-backup` under `/app`) is inside.

Two more of its rules existed because every file op carried a whole file over a control channel: the 1 MiB whole-file cap (`SESSION_FILE_CAP_BYTES`) and the refusal of binary files. **The MOUNT reads and writes ranges; the TRANSPORT does not** — claiming the distinction away is false of the transport. `readFile`/`writeFile` are whole-file frames, cc never sends the frame's `offset`, and the mirror must hold the whole file to serve arbitrary offsets, so the frame's `offset` would buy nothing. What each rule became:

- **the binary refusal is genuinely retired.** `writeFileBytes` (`src/systems/system.ts`) carries bytes end to end; `writeFile(path, data: string)` mangles binary and is no longer on the reconcile path. `tests/fuse-transport.test.mjs` T5/T6 round-trip a NUL and a 0xFF in both directions.
- **a size limit survives, at `MAX_FILE_BYTES`** (`src/systems/protocol.ts`, 32 MiB). `ControlServer.#fetchBody` refuses **before a byte moves** — the size is already in hand from the stat — with `EFBIG`, and records an `over-cap` fault so the FILE TOOL is refused by a sentence that names the cap and points at a range read through Bash (`overCapRefusal`, `src/systems/fuse/faultRefusals.ts`). Checked-before, not checked-after: `readFileBytes` also refuses `EFBIG`, but only after up to 32 MiB of base64 has crossed to be discarded, so `tests/fuse-transport.test.mjs` T7b asserts the **frame count** rather than the failure.

**Divergence and over-cap are a separate STATEFUL surface, consulted AFTER the tier gate** — so a reader counts **four** tier wordings above and not six. They live in `src/systems/fuse/faultRefusals.ts`, carry no `ToolDenyClass`, and `SessionRedirect.#classifyFile` asks them only once `classifyForTool` has already returned `allow`. The ordering is a ruling, not a style choice: the tier gate is the subsystem's most important property, and a surface consulted ahead of it is a bypass route by construction whatever it returns today. It costs nothing, because a fault can only exist for a path written through the union, which is project tier, which the gate allows. Pinned two-directionally by `tests/systems-file-tool-refusals.test.mjs` T23/T24.

## FUSE teardown

One implementation — `runTeardown` in `src/systems/fuse/session.ts` — reached from four places: `Instance.kill()` (the commanded path, which **awaits** it), `Instance._handleExit()` (the crash path, fire-and-forget beside `_redirect.close()`), and `sweepFuseSessions()` at boot. `bootstrap.sh` owns none of it: it `exec`s into the CLI and ceases to exist as a supervisor, and a shell trap cannot survive `SIGKILL`.

Ordered, every step bounded, every failure branch continuing rather than stopping:

| # | step | on failure |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | Read `mount.json`; fall back to `intent.json`. **Re-verify every recorded pid** against `/proc/<pid>/stat` field 22. | mismatch ⇒ that pid is treated as **gone** and never signalled (pid reuse); no record at all ⇒ `NO-RECORD`, nothing signalled |
| 1 | Stop the worker: `stdin.end()`, `SIGTERM`, `SIGKILL`, polling to a deadline. Unprivileged — `setpriv` dropped the CLI back to cc's uid. | still alive ⇒ **continue anyway**. A worker wedged in `D` on the mount cannot be signalled until the connection is aborted; the abort is the universal solvent |
| 2 | Unmount **deepest-first**, from `/proc/<nsPid>/mounts` (never `mountpoint -q`), via `nsenter` as root, `umount -l` on failure. The **`fusectl` mount is held back** — step 3 writes through it. | no live recorded pid ⇒ the namespace is gone and its mounts with it |
| 3 | Abort the FUSE connection **by the minor captured at mount time**, never one re-resolved from mountinfo (that lookup is a silent no-op after the unmount). Only ever a minor this session recorded. | not listed ⇒ `ABORT-UNAVAILABLE`, recorded, continue |
| 4 | `SIGTERM` then `SIGKILL` the daemon, **privileged** (it is root-owned and is not cc's child), each re-verified by starttime. | — |
| 5 | Bounded poll of `/proc/<daemonPid>/task`. Terminal states: `GONE` \| `ZOMBIE-ORPHAN` \| `NO-PID` \| `WEDGED(threads=n states=…)`. Then unmount the held-back `fusectl`. | `WEDGED` ⇒ **reported, never blocked on** |
| 6 | `SIGKILL` the **namespace anchor**, last. | survives ⇒ noted; the namespace stays open |
| 7 | **Confirm death positively**, and take the verdict from **namespace membership**: enumerate `/proc` (`procScan.ts`), re-verify the worker, the daemon **and** the anchor against their recorded starttimes, and read mounts from every vantage point — cc's own, `/proc/1/mounts`, any live recorded pid, and any member the record never named. | any survivor, any member, any residue, **or a scan that could not run** ⇒ the wedge branch |
| 8 | Re-read `mount.json` **immediately before** the irreversible step, then `rm -rf` the run directory. | on a wedge, **keep** it and rewrite `mount.json` with `{wedged, terminalState, residualMounts, survivingPids, at}` |

**THE CLEAN VERDICT COMES FROM NAMESPACE MEMBERSHIP, NOT FROM THE RECORDED PID SET.** The recorded set is what the bootstrap wrote down; the namespace is what is actually there, and the two differ in the ordinary case — Bash is a real subprocess family and there is deliberately no process-group kill on this path, so a live worker child holds mounts open while naming nothing cc recorded. **Anything cc cannot enumerate is a wedge, not a pass**: a scan that could not run yields `ok: false`, and an empty result read as "nothing is there" is exactly how a leak stays invisible. Three separate defects on this ticket were this one flaw in different clothes — a leaked anchor whose record had been deleted, an intent-only teardown that signalled nobody, and an unrecorded namespace member holding mounts.

**THE RECORD IS THE LAST THING DESTROYED, AND ONLY AFTER A POSITIVE DEATH CHECK — never after merely having issued a signal.** Both teardown and the boot sweep iterate *records*, so a record deleted while a process it names is alive makes that process permanently unreclaimable by name. This was a measured leak, not a hypothetical: a root `sleep infinity` anchor holding a live private mount namespace, orphaned to pid 1, with its run directory already removed. **Neither half of the obvious leak check could see it** — its namespace is private, so it can never appear in `/proc/1/mounts`, and its record was gone, so it was in no recorded set to re-verify. The two repairs are paired: `bootstrap.sh` writes the record *before* anything it starts (so an intent-only run directory provably has nothing behind it, which is what makes deleting one safe), and step 7 refuses to destroy a record whose pids are not confirmed dead.

**An intent-only run directory (no `mount.json`) is NOT presumed empty.** It means the bootstrap died after starting something, or is still mid-handshake — reachable in production every time `_awaitFuseMount` times out and tears the launch down. That path runs the marker scan scoped to its own run directory, unmounts through and kills what it finds (**including the bootstrap itself**, which is what stops a mid-handshake write rather than racing it), and re-reads `mount.json` afterwards: a record that appeared during teardown is never deleted over.

**A schema-violating record is a reported wedge, never a mid-machine throw.** `fusectl` must equal `path.join(rundir, 'fusectl')` by exact match and `minor` must be `/^\d+$/`, because both reach a root-executed command line. This is about robustness rather than a threat model — the chroot is not a security boundary here. What is closed is the no-adversary variant: a malformed field makes `execFile` throw mid-machine, `runTeardown` rejects, both callers swallow the rejection (`kill()` catches, `_handleExit` uses `void`), and the daemon, the mounts and the record all survive with **no wedge report at all**. The whole machine is additionally wrapped so any unexpected throw becomes the loudest verdict it has.

**`prepare()` releases the teardown latch and refuses a wedged directory.** Rewind and prune both kill the subprocess and call `launch()` again on the same `Instance`, so a session's lifecycle repeats in the same run directory. A latch that never reset made the *second* `kill()` a silent no-op — no unmounts, no abort, no signals, a root daemon and a private mount surviving until restart, and a `null` return the caller could not distinguish from idempotent-done. `teardown()` now returns `{ alreadyTornDown: true }` for that case, and `prepare()` throws `FUSE_PREVIOUS_TEARDOWN_WEDGED` rather than mounting a second session over the handle to a wedged first one.

**`prepare()` also refuses a run directory another instance owns: `FUSE_RUN_DIR_FOREIGN`.** `mkdir(root, {recursive:true})` adopts a pre-existing directory silently, so two instances mapped to one name would share a control socket, a mirror and a record set — and the `rm(recordPath)` two lines later would destroy the *live* session's mount record. The predicate is **ownership, not existence**: it compares `intent.json`'s `instanceId` to the plan's, because the relaunch above is ordinary and lands in the same directory with the same id, so a bare `EEXIST` refusal would break it. The whole uuid in the directory name already makes a collision impossible; this turns any future change to that shape from a silently shared socket into a named refusal.

### The `/proc` enumeration and the record-independent backstop (`procScan.ts`, `orphans.ts`)

Two identities, both **carried by the process**, neither a name or a pattern: `/proc/<pid>/ns/mnt` compared against the recorded `nsMntId` (membership by definition — it sees processes no record names), and `CC_FUSE_INSTANCE_ID` / `CC_FUSE_RUNDIR` in `/proc/<pid>/environ`, put there by `execve` before the bootstrap forked anything (the only handle left when a record is gone or was never written). **Never** comm, cmdline, or any pattern: `sleep infinity` is about as generic a needle as exists on a Linux box, and a pattern match here is the mechanism that once killed this devcontainer.

**Two uid passes, unioned by pid.** Both files are `ptrace_may_access`-governed, which without `CAP_SYS_PTRACE` grants access only on a uid match — so cc as itself sees the worker and its children (uid 1000), cc through `sudo` sees the bootstrap and the anchor (uid 0), and neither sees the other's. `ok` is true only if **both** passes ran: a missing half is a blind spot, and a blind spot is not an empty result.

**`ok` means "this pass enumerated", not "the command exited 0"** — the canary. On a host missing `awk`, `sed` or `tr` every row is skipped by the scanner's own `[ -n "$st" ] || continue` guard, the shell still exits 0, and zero rows would read as an empty namespace with everything in it alive. The scanning shell is itself a process in `/proc`, so it prints its own pid and a pass that cannot find that pid among its rows is not `ok`. `passEnumerated` owns this.

**The intent-only path keeps its own scan rows.** It re-verifies each by a fresh liveness check and carries the survivors into the verdict as members, rather than trusting `reclaimProcess`'s return value — a marker process that survived its `SIGKILL` (`killed: false`, the shape of a bootstrap wedged in `D` inside a hung mount syscall, which `SIGKILL` cannot touch) is a live namespace member and therefore a wedge. `markerReclaimed.some(r => !r.killed)` is a second disjunct behind it.

It fails closed at every step: an unreadable field, a missing `starttime`, `pid <= 1`, a `rundir` outside this store's run root, or a starttime that moved since the scan all mean *not ours, not signalled*. A run directory that still holds a `mount.json` is left to the record pass rather than raced with it. Ordering within a reclaim: unmount through the process first (it is the handle), then re-verify, then signal — privileged, because an orphan may be the root-owned anchor or bootstrap and an unprivileged signal at one is `EPERM`, which reads as "already gone".

**What it cannot see, stated so the guarantee is not overstated.** A process that has changed credentials *and has not exec'd since* is invisible to both passes — which is exactly the union daemon (`setfsuid` per request). The worker is visible: `execve` resets dumpability, so after `setpriv` it is an ordinary uid-1000 process. The **anchor** is visible because it is credential-stable, and that is what matters: it is the last process holding the namespace open, and freeing its mounts closes the `/dev/fuse` connection, which is what makes the daemon exit. The record remains the only handle on the daemon's pid — which is why the ordering guarantee, not this module, is the repair.

**`wait(2)` is never called**, and that is not a preference: it blocks on the whole thread group, a thread stuck in FUSE I/O never leaves the kernel, and an orphan reparented to pid 1 cannot be waited on at all (`ECHILD`). cc is never the daemon's parent in any case, so `REAPED` is not a state it can produce — a single-threaded zombie is `ZOMBIE-ORPHAN`.

**The wedge branch is a reportable state, not a silent one:** a `system`/`stderr` event on the session's stream (what an operator watching the session is already looking at), a `console.warn` (which survives the session's death and lands in the orchestrator log), and the kept record (so the next boot sweep **re-reports** it rather than silently rediscovering it).

**`fusectl` entries with no record of ours are counted and never aborted.** Stale minors free nothing, survive abort and are inert: a count of fusectl entries is not a count of live daemons.

### The namespace anchor

cc reaches a session's mount namespace with `nsenter --mount=/proc/<pid>/ns/mnt`, and that open is governed by `ptrace_may_access`. Where `/proc/sys/fs/suid_dumpable` is `2` and `CAP_SYS_PTRACE` is **not in the container's bounding set** — the shape this runs in, and one in which not even uid 0 has it — a process that has changed credentials is non-dumpable and needs that capability; a process that has not is reachable by a caller of the same uid. The daemon calls `setfsuid` per request and the worker is `setpriv`'d, so **both become unreachable within milliseconds of the mount coming up** — measured: the first `nsenter` after the handshake succeeds and every later one fails `cannot open /proc/<pid>/ns/mnt: Permission denied`.

The anchor (`bootstrap.sh` step 2b) is a credential-stable root process (`setsid sleep infinity`) whose `ns/mnt` therefore stays openable by root for the life of the namespace. It is a **handle, not a supervisor**: it holds no state, watches nothing and cannot restart anything. `pickNsPid` prefers it, falling back to the daemon and then the worker for hosts where `CAP_SYS_PTRACE` **is** available. Teardown kills it last, which is a second and independent guarantee behind step 2 — a namespace whose last process has gone takes every mount in it with it. Its cost: after a hard orchestrator crash the namespace and its (private, `/proc/1/mounts`-invisible) mounts persist until the boot sweep, instead of evaporating.

### The boot sweep

`sweepFuseSessions()` runs from `server.ts` immediately after `sweepSessionTmpDirs`, before `createServer()`, and is licensed by the same argument: an instance id is a fresh uuid per process, so any record under `systems/fuse/run/` at boot is dead by construction. It is **load-bearing, not defence in depth** — nothing about a running process is persisted anywhere else in cc, `scheduleRestart` calls `process.exit(0)` ~50 ms after `instances.shutdown()`, and neither `shutdownTempSync` nor `shutdownForResumeSync` can run the (async) mount teardown at all. Both of those signal the **recorded inner pid** (`killablePid`) rather than `inst.pid`, which under the wrap is `sudo`'s and cannot forward `SIGKILL`. Best-effort throughout: a directory cc cannot reclaim must never stop a boot.

## Playbooks

Four modules, layered so the policy is pure and the wiring is thin. The wire contract, the tool arguments and the known limitations are in [protocol.md → Playbooks](protocol.md#playbooks); this is the internal shape.

- **`src/playbooks.ts`** — definition catalog (built-in seeds + user overlay through the shared `fragmentCatalog.ts` factory), the load-time validator, and the **pure** policy decision `decide({toolName, args, projection, playbooks, isLive})` → `{ok, patchedArgs, move}` or `{ok:false, code, reason, legalMoves}`. `isLive: (sessionId) => boolean` is a **required** parameter, never defaulted — a default reading the projection would reintroduce the second liveness authority this shape exists to rule out — so every call site is a compile error away from being found. A bad definition is rejected **at load time**, not at spawn time. The governable-tool index resolves through a **memoized dynamic import** of `mcp/tools.ts` specifically so `handlers.ts → playbooks.ts` (which the read tools need) cannot close a cycle; `validatePlaybook` is pure and synchronous, taking that index as a parameter.
- **`src/playbookLedger.ts`** — the append-only JSONL log (`fs.appendFile`, **never** `writeFileAtomic` or a rewritten document — the `archive-store-corruption` failure was a rewritten JSON store wiping itself) and the in-memory **projection** folded from it. One `applyEvent` serves both the boot fold and each live append, so an incrementally-updated projection cannot diverge from one folded from the file. Event kinds: `spawn`, `transition`, `retire`, `refusal`, `enforcement`, `resume`. The projection carries **no liveness bit** — `retire` and `resume` are audit-only, folding to no state beyond the event itself — so `WorkerState` has nothing for a second reader to consult by mistake. `resume` still un-retires a worker **in place** in the sense that matters (nothing else about it changes): it is a distinct kind rather than a second `spawn` precisely because the `spawn` arm *replaces* the worker's state, which would reset `stageHistory` and empty `provenance` (see [protocol.md → Resuming a playbook-tracked worker](protocol.md#resuming-a-playbook-tracked-worker)). Run membership is **derived** — the connected component over `provenance` edges ∪ same-worker transitions, via union-find — so there are no run ids and no external entity to keep in sync. `stageHistory` is what makes a `needs` entry's provenance half answerable; its `liveness` half is answered from `InstanceManager.isSessionLive` (`src/instances.ts`), THE single liveness authority — `liveSessionsInStage(p, anchor, stage, isLive)` takes that oracle as a required parameter rather than reading a fold. The store path resolves through a **lazy** function so a `PROJECTS_ROOT` override is honoured per call.
- **`src/mcp/playbookGate.ts`** — the single enforcement point, composed by `buildMcpRouter` so `mcp/server.ts` stays transport. Owns the conductor predicate lookup, the enforcement-level read, `decide()`, the refusal payload, and **every** ledger write — exactly TWO writers: the `tools/call` checkpoint's commits, and the manager's `status` stream (below). A `refusal` row records every worker the call named — `sessionId`, plus `forwardSessionId` when the call carried a forward — since the `code` alone says which side was refused (one `subjects(args)` helper, shared with the `playbook_warn` payload). Under `warn` it also pushes a UI-only `system`/`playbook_warn` event onto the **calling conductor** (`_emitUi`, strictly after the append) — the ledger has no reader, so without it the mode that exists to warn a human warns nobody; the conductor *agent* is unaffected, since `_emitUi` never reaches the CLI. Appends are serialized through one chain (`append` reads the seq then awaits the write) and the ledger path is **pinned on first resolution**, because some appends are fire-and-forget and a deferred write must not land in a different store than the one it was decided against. The stream writer carries the two things no `tools/call` can report: `retire` when a subprocess exits (an audit record — capacity no longer reads it) and `enforcement` when the level changes — watching the stream rather than each toggle's call site means a spawn-time value, the WebSocket flip and a restart restore are all recorded without any of them knowing the ledger exists. The gate also exposes `isLive(sessionId) => instances.isSessionLive(sessionId)`, the SAME oracle every `decide()` call here uses, so a read surface (`playbook_state`, `describe_playbook`'s dry-run) answers from the identical source rather than re-deriving its own. A host reboot (or an orchestrator crash) needs no boot-time repair any more: capacity and `needs.liveness` both read `isSessionLive` directly, so a session this process's registry never heard of simply holds no slot — see [protocol.md → Playbooks](protocol.md#playbooks).
- **`src/playbookConvention.ts`** — the pure renderer `renderPlaybookConvention(pb)` behind the preferred-playbook convention (contract in [protocol.md → Playbooks](protocol.md#playbooks)). Pure and store-free so it is unit-testable against a definition object; composed by `conductorConventions.ts` (`getDefaultPlaybookSelection`/`setDefaultPlaybook`/`resolveDefaultPlaybookId`/`defaultPlaybookConvention`), which resolves the selected id through `loadPlaybooks()` rather than a second discovery layer. Authored `description` text is passed through **verbatim** — the definition is its one home, and a renderer that reflowed or truncated it would become a second.
- **`src/mcp/handlers.ts`** — the three read tools, reading the gate's one projection through `readProjection()`/`readHistory()` rather than folding a second. Both load on demand, and that load is read-only, which is what lets a read tool answer on an install with no ledger yet without materialising the file.

**Definitions are the authority on what the graph is; the projection is the authority on where workers are; the instance field is the authority on the enforcement level.** The persisted `defaultPlaybookEnforcement` (`conductorConventions.ts`) supplies only the level a conductor is **born** at, applied in `Manager._doCreate` before `launch()`; nothing writes back to it, so the runtime authority is never split. `projection.enforcement` is written for the audit trail and deliberately has **no reader** — `playbook_state` reports the calling conductor's live mode off its instance instead, which also means nothing can surface a non-conductor as a governed actor.

## Migrations
`migrations/` holds idempotent migration scripts run automatically on boot (entrypoint `migrations/index.mjs`). The most recent, `0037-project-location-records`, gives every project a stored `location` and moves what the old layout implied — local worktree checkouts under `<root>/.worktrees/<project>/<key>`, Library plugin installs under `<root>/.plugins/<name>` — with a two-class error taxonomy, a ledger for what it could not move, a fixed-path snapshot and a completion marker; its own header is authoritative. Before it, `0036-temp-cleanup-manifest-place` clears the placement of any pending-temp-cleanup entry written while the manifest persisted a bare `cwd`: the sweep now needs `{ system, remoteId, cwd }` to name a subagent directory, and a placement cannot be invented — guessing one could delete a different remote's. The sweep's existing placeless branch degrades to bookkeeping only, so the loss is at most one orphaned subagent directory per temp session in flight across the upgrade. Each self-checks "already applied?" and is a fast no-op in steady state; a script that throws **aborts the boot**. See [`migrations/migrations.md`](../migrations/migrations.md) for the listing and conventions for adding new ones.

## Testing
All tests via `node tests/run.mjs` (programmatic node:test runner — Termux's glibc-runner wrapper hoists leading `--flags` into `NODE_OPTIONS` and refuses `--test`).

**TypeScript toolchain.** Server-side `src/*.ts` runs directly under Node 24's native type stripping — no emit, no `dist/`, no bundler — so converted files are limited to **erasable syntax** (`tsconfig.json`: `strict`, `erasableSyntaxOnly`, `verbatimModuleSyntax`, `allowImportingTsExtensions`, NodeNext ESM). Because stripping never type-checks, `npm run typecheck` (`tsc --noEmit`) is an **enforced gate**, not an optional script: it runs as `pretest`, before every `npm test` (including the mutation harness's `npm test` baseline/test commands). Converting a module renames it `.js` → `.ts` and every importer's specifier with it (Node resolves import specifiers literally — no extension fallback); a still-`.js` sibling is imported via a minimal hand-written `.d.ts` that declares only what the `.ts` consumer uses (`src/projects.d.ts` today). Round 1 converted `modelVersions`, `usageWindowDomains`, `storeLock`, `sessionBackends`.

**The systems gate (`npm run gate:systems`).** `CC_LOCAL_SYSTEM_PROVIDER` swaps the in-process `local` System for a `ProviderSystem` over a provider process, so **every project-scoped operation in the app runs over the wire protocol** and nothing in the suite knows it. `tests/systems-gate.mjs` typechecks once and then runs the WHOLE suite once per row against `src/systems/referenceProvider.ts` — `processGroupSignal+remotes` and `processGroupSignal:false`, **concurrently** (each row's live lines are tagged `[1] `/`[2] ` by `tests/rowPrefix.mjs`, mapped to names by the banners printed before either row starts, and the closing block is still printed last and untagged) — which is one gate making two claims: the three primitives plus the derivations are SUFFICIENT for the application, and **the optional-capability fallback actually executes** (a fallback that has never run is not a fallback). There is no third row: `persistentShell` does not exist, and a row for it would have been byte-identical to a default no-flag row — vacuous, not cheap. It is a separate command because it IS `npm test`, once per row; the per-configuration protocol suites below run inside the ordinary suite, so a plain `npm test` still exercises the fallback at the unit level. The five files: `tests/systems-protocol-codec.test.mjs` (framing + taxonomy, unit), `tests/systems-protocol-conformance.test.mjs` (**the definition of a valid provider** — the three primitives, the exec lifecycle, process-group signalling, negotiation, multiplexed ids, every error code and every derivation, in every configuration, with a closing test that fails if a taxonomy code has no producer — which fires in BOTH directions, so a code with no producer AND a cross-reference naming a code the taxonomy no longer has each red it; that is what caught `EBUSY`'s removal), `tests/systems-shell-framing.test.mjs` (the measured shell failures), `tests/systems-provider-supervision.test.mjs` (a deliberately misbehaving provider, `tests/fake-provider.mjs` — bad version, silent handshake, garbage line, mid-operation death, restart storm, wedge), and `tests/systems-provider-parity.test.mjs` (LocalSystem vs ProviderSystem answering identically). A test that WRAPS a System method must take the live handle's prototype (`tests/systemHandle.mjs`), never `LocalSystem.prototype`: under the gate the handle is a `ProviderSystem`, and a NEGATIVE assertion against the wrong class passes for the wrong reason. Row 1 carries `--remote gate=/` plus `CC_LOCAL_SYSTEM_REMOTE_ID=gate` (`LOCAL_REMOTE_ENV`, `src/systems/registry.ts`), so every frame that pass emits is target-bound — `remotes` folded into an existing pass rather than given a fourth, because a fourth costs a whole suite and puts the same field on byte-for-byte the same request frames (the counts, and how to re-take them without double-counting the suite's own providers, are in `tests/systems-gate.mjs`'s header). The seam's guard is structural: `buildLocalSystem` returns the in-process `LocalSystem` before it reads the remote-id variable, so that variable alone can never bind cc's own machine (`tests/systems-local.test.mjs`). The cost of the fold: no gate row now runs `processGroupSignal:true`+`remotes:false` together — that cell is covered by `CAPABILITY_CONFIGS[0]` of the conformance and parity suites, which run inside every row. `remoteDescriptors` is not in the matrix at all: `mirror()` is unreachable for the id `local` whatever class backs it, and a `--mirror` row was measured receiving zero `describeRemote` frames. **A red row states its own diagnosis in the closing `=== gate:systems ===` block** — the hang-guard verdict plus the failing test names (capped at `NAMED_FAILURE_LIMIT`, then counted) — because the gate is read through a `tail` of a captured log and its first-ever red lost its failing test name to exactly that. A row that reds with **zero `✖` lines** says so explicitly and points at the verdict: that is a killed/wedged file or a leaked process, not a test failure, and the block distinguishes the two at a glance instead of by re-running. `tests/gateSummary.mjs` scans and renders (ANSI- and chunk-boundary-independent, so a TTY run and a redirected one say the same thing); `tests/systems-gate-summary.test.mjs` drives both against canned rows including the zero-`✖` one. The row's output is **teed with `.pipe()`, never a bare `write()`** — `process.stdout.write` is asynchronous once stdout is a pipe, so a fire-and-forget tee would lose output under backpressure and reintroduce the very failure the tee exists to prevent; for the same reason the gate sets `process.exitCode` and never calls `process.exit()`, which would discard the summary still buffered in its own stdout.

**A `gate:systems` red is not automatically the diff under test — and two shapes should not be the first guess.** Attribute a red with `tests/gateSummary.mjs`'s closing block. Two mechanisms produce a gate red while being **no Systems-provider behaviour at all**: a test oracle resting on a port the kernel can re-issue (`reservedPort()`), and a detached `git maintenance` landing inside a tree-snapshot window (`pinGitConfig`). **Their row-independence rests on different evidence, and the difference is worth keeping straight:** the port defect is shown independent **by forcing** — the injected steal fails the test in all three arms and passes in all three without it — while the `git maintenance` defect's independence is **structural**, since no gate row's flags touch that actor, and its forcing proof never varied the provider row. Two shapes are **not to be re-assumed**, each stated with the support it actually has: **the orphan shape**, at n=52 (every run, *including both reds*, printed the identical `hang-guard: 346/346 files reported, 0 killed, 0 leaked process(es) swept, stream ended cleanly, run cap not reached` — the real line, `tests/run.mjs`, grep it verbatim), and **the wedge shape**, at n=2 (both reds were ordinary named `✖` failures at normal wall clock, not hangs). n=2 is a weak bound; it is enough to stop presuming the wedge, not enough to call it impossible.

**A test that asserts a port REFUSES connects must not get that port from `allocatePort()`.** `allocatePort` binds `listen(0)`, reads the port and hands it straight back, so the number it returns is one the kernel can re-issue to any other `listen(0)` on the box — after which the connect succeeds and the oracle reports the opposite of the truth. That is what redded `bare-TCP readiness does not fire before the port is actually bound`. `reservedPort()` in `tests/plugins-supervisor.test.mjs` is the provenance to copy: it picks a **verified-free port strictly below `ip_local_port_range`'s low bound**, which no automatic allocator can hand out (measured: **60,000/60,000** `allocatePort()` results land inside the range; the tests at the foot of that file re-check both halves at a 200-draw sample on any host that exposes `/proc/sys/net/ipv4/ip_local_port_range`, and announce-and-skip where it does not, so the re-check is host-conditional rather than universal). `src/plugins/ports.ts` is unchanged — `listen(0)` plus the supervisor's EADDRINUSE retry is correct for real plugins; only the test's port provenance moved.

**Worker sessions on a system.** `tests/systems-concurrent-commands.test.mjs` (two commands of ONE session genuinely overlapping via a filesystem rendezvous, each result holding exactly its own 300 lines in order, and an interrupt reaching one while a sibling that is provably still running completes untouched). `tests/systems-tool-redirect.test.mjs` (the forwarder rewrite, the output fence, cancellation and `close()`, and — since the geometry retirement — that a file tool passes through UNTOUCHED at an absolute system path). `tests/systems-session-root.test.mjs` is now CRITERION 8'S EVIDENCE FILE rather than the allow-list walk's: no session root and no manifest anywhere under the store after a real remote spawn (a full recursive walk, not a probe at a predicted path), and ZERO `find` frames on a recording provider's wire with the recorder's own traffic as the control. `tests/systems-transcript-collision.test.mjs` owns the collision guard, `tests/systems-mirror-wide.test.mjs` a mirror root wider than the project, and `tests/systems-mirror-geometry-follow.test.mjs` the session-fatal re-advertisement refusal. `tests/systems-bash-rules-refusal.test.mjs`, `tests/systems-redirect-hooks.test.mjs`, `tests/systems-mirror-advertisement.test.mjs`, `tests/systems-mirror-fallback.test.mjs` and `tests/systems-remote-worker.test.mjs` keep their subjects. The bridge's own suites (`systems-file-bridge`, `systems-mirror-bridge`, `systems-mirror-refusal`, `systems-session-root-skips`, `systems-mirror-geometry-move`) are DELETED with the bridge — the semantics they specified are under “What `fileBridge` carried” above, not in a test file.

**Proving streaming, not just output.** Every streaming assertion is ORDER-based rather than wall-clock-based, so none of them can flake on a slow machine. At the shell layer, the first chunk must have arrived while `run()` was still pending. At the process layer, `part1` must arrive in a write that does **not** also carry `part2` — a forwarder that buffered to completion necessarily coalesces the whole output into one write, so the split itself is the evidence. The docker suite adds the one wall-clock check that is worth having, and only as a second witness: the first write must lead the process's end by most of the command's own pause.

**Every CLI behaviour the redirect depends on has a gated pin, and each pin has a CONTROL.** `disableAllHooks`, `includeGitInstructions` and `CLAUDE_CODE_TMPDIR` are each asserted twice — once with the lever and once without — because a RENAMED key would otherwise leave the with-lever assertion passing for the wrong reason while cc's scan silently stopped finding the live one. And each asserts over a **mechanical** channel, never the model's prose: `CLAUDE_CODE_TMPDIR` from the filesystem (the model was measured reporting "the background task completed" instead of relaying the path it was asked for); `includeGitInstructions` from a `git` shim earlier on `PATH` — measured, `status --short`, `log --oneline` and `config user.name` run only when the block is built, while five other git calls happen either way, so the assertion names the three rather than counting them.

**The FUSE-union chroot's two suites, and why they are two.** One is ungated and deterministic; the other is the gated real-FUSE family, four files over one harness. `tests/fuse-lifecycle.test.mjs` runs in `npm test`: it drives `runTeardown` against an **injected `MountDriver`** whose fixtures are in-memory tables and whose clock is virtual, so every deadline in the state machine is exercised in microseconds and nothing signals a real pid. It pins what is otherwise unobservable — that the unmounts are issued deepest-first AND before the abort, that the abort names the minor captured at mount time, that a recycled pid is reported gone and never signalled, that `state == Z` with a second live thread is `WEDGED` and never reaped, that a wedge keeps the record and reports on both surfaces, and that a `fusectl` minor with no record of ours is counted and not aborted. The `tests/fuse-*.real.test.mjs` FAMILY (`RUN_FUSE_LIFECYCLE=1`) is the gate: real `sudo`, real `unshare`, real FUSE, a fake `claude`. Four files — `fuse-lifecycle.real` (the spawn, the three teardown paths, the boot sweep, the busy-mount wedge, the cost control, the criterion-9 refusal, **arm 8** — the only place on a real host where the sudo claim is observable: a sentinel provably does NOT cross `sudo -n` there, and the worker's own environment reaches the CLI anyway — and the record-independent leak arm), `fuse-union-mount.real` (R1–R7, what the mount itself answers), `fuse-union-routing.real` (R8, R15, R9, R10, R11, R13, R14 — where a path resolves for an UNMARKED caller) and `fuse-union-marking.real` (R12, R13w, R14L, R16 — who is marked and when) — over one shared harness, `tests/fuseGateCase.mjs`, which owns the dependency preflight, the server, the three systems, the mirror scaffold and the observation helpers, and which is not named `*.test.mjs`, so `discover()` ignores it. Four arms, all in `fuse-union-marking.real`, own the marking event between them — `R12` (the mark is the CLI's own and the whole bootstrap chain precedes it), `R13w` (at `mirrorRoot: '/'` the chain is unmarked and host-served, and **no marked op names a path under `/usr/bin`** — the standing licence for the chain carrying no pin; its contrast half measures the other side, that a **marked** caller is not served those paths at all), `R14L` (a backend launcher runs unmarked: its host-pinned handoff reaches the CLI, an unpinned one diverges) and `R16` (a symlinked launcher marks on the **link**, and its realpath is then walked marked — the geometry the gate's default `CLAUDE_BIN` leaves untested). **Every arm in the family captures a `before` snapshot and asserts a delta** — a host can carry inherited FUSE residue, and an absolute assertion would either fail on it or hide a leak under it. Every signal anywhere in the family targets a pid read from the session's own `mount.json`, starttime-revalidated through `killPids` (`tests/procTree.mjs`); there is no `pkill`, `pgrep` or pattern of any kind. **IT SAT AT THE PER-FILE WATCHDOG AS ONE FILE, AND THAT WATCHDOG KILLED IT.** Its cost is ~2.4s of real mount teardown per arm — `runTeardown` plus the daemon and anchor dying — so a single file was charged the SUM of its arms and the family is charged the MAX. **THE KILL IS THE REASON THE SPLIT EXISTS, AND IT IS NOT REPRODUCIBLE ON DEMAND** — both halves of that have to stay recorded, because a margin record holding only the healthy figure is what invites raising `FILE_KILL_MS` after a false kill (`tests/hangGuardConfig.mjs` states the rule). **Crossing runs, as one file on a quiet 16-core box against `FILE_KILL_MS` = 90 000 ms:** 87.0 / 89.7 / 90.2 s over three consecutive runs, then **94 341 ms** after a prose-only commit. A crossed deadline is a TRUNCATING SIGKILL: the run reports `0/1 files reported`, the file emits no summary, and node's own pass/fail tally and every per-arm name go with it — **with every arm green**. **Re-measured at the split on the same box class, the same three-run protocol, same deadline: 82 213 / 82 193 / 82 460 ms (1.09x)** — so the file was never reliably over, it was within ~10% of the deadline and crossed under conditions neither protocol controls. Treat the ~9% headroom as the *good* case and the kill as the one that decides the design. **After the split**, at the documented `TEST_CONCURRENCY=1`, over **ten** consecutive runs on a quiet 16-core box (`nproc` 16), same deadline — ten, because three clean runs is exactly what hid the contention below: **28 610–29 341 ms (>=3.06x)** for `fuse-lifecycle.real`, **26 119–26 933 ms (>=3.34x)** for `fuse-union-routing.real`, **25 902–26 466 ms (>=3.40x)** for `fuse-union-mount.real`, **13 940–14 534 ms (>=6.19x)** for `fuse-union-marking.real` — **0 kills in 10**, 4/4 files reported every run, total family wall **95.4–96.9 s**. No `CC_TEST_FILE_KILL_MS` override is needed. Read each file's wall off the run's own `hang-guard: slowest files` line, whose ranking is capped at five entries, so a four-file family appears on it whole. **BOTH PER-FILE FIGURES ARE RECORDED, because the capped ones look 30% high against any uncapped re-measurement.** The same four files, same box, over the clean runs of the 18-run uncapped rate sample above (`node tests/run.mjs tests/fuse-*.real.test.mjs`, NO cap — the configuration that also produces the kills, so these figures are not a recommendation): `fuse-lifecycle.real` **22 311–23 424 ms**, `fuse-union-routing.real` **20 813–21 247 ms**, `fuse-union-mount.real` **20 523–21 277 ms**, `fuse-union-marking.real` **10 876–11 592 ms**. So serialising costs each file ~25–30%. **WHERE THAT GOES IS LOCALISED, NOT EXPLAINED:** the two quantities the harness itself prints (`fuse gate timing [chroot]`) are flat between the modes — first spawn→idle ~0.85–1.01 s, later spawns 149–326 ms, turn 100–345 ms, in BOTH — so it is not in spawn and not in the turn. What is left in an arm is its `inNs`/`sh` probe round trips and `instances.remove` teardown, which are not separately instrumented. That these are fixed-latency chains which four concurrent file streams overlap, and a serial run cannot, is the standing HYPOTHESIS for the delta; it is not measured. **THE CAP COSTS WALL AND BUYS MARGIN, and the trade is not free:** serialised, the family's total (~96 s) is ABOVE what the single pre-split file cost (84 695–85 772 ms over six runs of its own invocation against the real guard, 0 failures, same quiet 16-core box, load ~1.0–2.2) — but that file sat at **1.04x** of the deadline with no room for a stall — the 1.09x quoted earlier in this paragraph is the SAME file in a different session and under the `CC_TEST_FILE_KILL_MS` override, so the two multiples are not a drift, they are two sessions — while each file here has 3x or better, and a stall now costs one file's arm names instead of all 26. **THOSE MARGINS DESCRIBE HEALTHY RUNS, NOT A HANG:** `TURN_TIMEOUT_MS` in the harness's `spawnWorker` is 120 000 ms, above `FILE_KILL_MS`, so a genuinely hung spawn still blows the file deadline before its own timeout fires. The remedy this config prescribes for a file at its deadline is **more files**, not a later deadline — so an arm added here is an arm that has to be paid for, and the file it is added to is what the margin is charged against. One observation is genuinely shared across the four concurrent processes: `snapshot()`'s `pid1Total`, a whole-machine `/proc/1/mounts` line count each arm asserts unchanged across itself. Under a green product nothing reaches pid 1's table, so it is stable; if it ever flakes under concurrency the answer is a concurrency cap, not a widened assertion.

**WHY THE CAP IS IN THE INVOCATION: UNCAPPED, THE FOUR FILES STARVE EACH OTHER.** The split traded one long file for four that run CONCURRENTLY, and each of them spawns real workers into real FUSE mounts. The runner's `peak concurrent fake-claude subprocesses` line is a **100 ms sampler's maximum, not a census**, so read it as a range over runs rather than a value: over 11 uncapped runs it read **3 ten times and 4 once**; over 10 capped runs it read **1 every time**. Measured on the bare glob at the default concurrency, settled box, no override: **3 kills in 18 runs (~17%)**. Every one had the same shape — a single arm starves, is cancelled at the runner's own 60 000 ms per-test `timeout` (`run()` in `tests/run.mjs`), and its whole file then rides to `FILE_KILL_MS` and is SIGKILLed at ~90 100–90 200 ms, so that file emits no summary and its arm names are lost while every other file passes. **The stalled arm varied — R9, R10 and R14 — so it is contention, not an arm-specific bug.** All three observed stalls happened to fall in `fuse-union-routing.real`; nothing in the measurements explains why that file rather than `fuse-union-mount.real`, which spawns a worker in 7 of 7 arms exactly as routing does, so do not read the victim as predicted. `tests/run.mjs` has no per-file exclusivity (one `run({files, concurrency})` call, one global `TEST_CONCURRENCY`), so the cap is part of how the gate is invoked rather than a property a file can declare. **Turning the gate on for a whole-suite run is the same hazard by another route** (`RUN_FUSE_LIFECYCLE=1 node tests/run.mjs` with no path arguments — what a mutation round does): measured 1 stall in 6 runs, the same 60 s cancellation, the file reaching 92 570 ms. `TEST_CONCURRENCY=4` measured green there against the real guard once — 377/377 reported, 0 killed, worst FUSE file 21 983 ms, suite wall 166 s — **and it is NOT established**: a separate gate-on full-suite baseline at that same `TEST_CONCURRENCY=4` red **R14L**, the same starvation shape, and the marking file then passed 4/4 alone at `TEST_CONCURRENCY=1`. So 4 is a starting point for a re-measurement, not a setting with a rate behind it; the only configuration with a rate is the family glob at `TEST_CONCURRENCY=1`, 0 kills in 10. Do not answer any of this by splitting further, by folding arms, by moving `FILE_KILL_MS`, or by changing the 60 s per-test timeout. **ONE FAILURE IN THAT 18 IS NOT EXPLAINED BY CONTENTION AND IS NOT CLAIMED TO BE:** R5 in `fuse-union-mount.real` failed FAST (1711 ms, i.e. it reached the end of the arm rather than timing out) rather than stalling, and its message was not captured. The obvious candidate is the one machine-global observation this section names as shared across the four concurrent processes — `snapshot()`'s `pid1Total`, a whole-machine `/proc/1/mounts` line count — and it is measured **not** supported: sampled every 100 ms across a whole uncapped family run, `/proc/1/mounts` held constant at 52 lines for 250 consecutive samples. **That non-support rests on ONE run's window**, so if R5 recurs, sample `/proc/1/mounts` at the arm's own before/after inside `snapshot()` rather than from a background sampler — a background sampler cannot see a disturbance that opens and closes between two of its ticks. R5 **has not recurred** in the 10 capped runs nor in 10 further logged uncapped ones; that is an absence of recurrence, not a mechanism — a concurrency cap cannot *explain* a 1711 ms assertion failure, and this one stays unnamed.

**The leak check has to be record-independent, and that is a scar.** The obvious one — a `/proc/1/mounts` delta plus starttime re-verification of the pids in `mount.json` — is blind by construction to a leaked process whose namespace is private *and* whose record has been deleted, and exactly one leaked. `tests/fuse-lifecycle.real.test.mjs` arm 7 therefore asserts, from `/proc/<pid>/environ` and without reading any record, that no process carrying its `CC_FUSE_RUNDIR` survives; it is ordered last in that file so it sees every earlier arm's residue. **Its scope is that file, not the run** — `runRoot` derives from each file's own store root, and the four files run concurrently — so the same check runs for the other three in the shared `after()` hook in `tests/fuseGateCase.mjs`, which names the file in its failure message because a hook failure reds no arm. **WHAT THAT HOOK IS WORTH OUTSIDE THE LIFECYCLE FILE HAS BEEN MEASURED FROM BOTH SIDES, and the two answers differ:** removing the anchor's SIGKILL from teardown **reds the marking file** through this hook — a leaked anchor that no arm in that file could see — so the assertion has real teeth where no arm covers it; but blinding the `/proc` scanner itself **survives** in the mount file, because nothing outside the lifecycle file proves the scan can still SEE a live attributable process. Only arm 4 does that, by holding one alive and asserting the scan finds it. **So for the other three the check catches a leak and cannot catch a blind scanner**, and a non-lifecycle file run alone has no positive control at all.

**THE SAME BLINDNESS REACHES THE MOUNT-RESIDUE OBSERVATION, and it is one limitation rather than two.** Every arm in the family that takes a residue delta measures it *under `runRoot`*, so a `runRoot` that is wrong-but-plausible makes both sides read empty and the arm passes vacuously. `setupFuseGate` verifies the `ctx` bag it publishes against `CTX_SHAPE` before handing it to a file, and that DOES fire — measured, in all four files, on a bag published with `runRoot: ''`, throwing in `before()` so no arm runs. But no predicate there can distinguish the true run root from any other absolute path, and `mountsUnder`'s refusal only rejects a non-absolute one: publish `/opt` and the mount, routing and marking files stay green 7/7, 7/7 and 4/4. **The only thing in the family that closes it is arm 1 and arm 4's POSITIVE mount-membership assertion** — that `record.root` is in the daemon's or anchor's table under `runRoot`, which a wrong root cannot satisfy — which is why the same substitution kills the lifecycle file and nothing else. So both record-independent observations, the process scan and the mount delta, rest for the other three files on this file's positive controls holding in a sibling process. A hook failure reds no arm — it surfaces on the suite's `describe` name — which is why the message names the file and says where it fired. (The scan result is NOT swallowed: `attributableProcesses` asserts the `/proc` walk ran, and a `.catch(() => [])` around it would turn "the scan could not run" into "no leak".)

**Two ways that gate can pass vacuously, both closed, in every file of the family.** `bootServer({realProcess:true})` **deletes** `FAKE_CLAUDE_SCENARIO` unless a scenario is passed, and `fake-claude` then exits 2 — so every teardown arm passed against a worker that had already crashed. And `status === 'idle'` is **not** a barrier that the CLI is up: `spawn()` reports idle as soon as the subprocess is alive and stdin is writable, while under the chroot the recorded pid is still `/bin/sh` running as root, mid-bootstrap. Each arm therefore drives a real turn, and the restart arm additionally asserts that the daemon, the anchor and the mount are **still alive** before the sweep runs — otherwise it silently degrades into a second copy of the crash arm.

**The gated real-dependency suites.** `RUN_CLI_CONTRACT=1` → `tests/systems-cli-*.real.test.mjs` (one subject per file over the shared harness `tests/cliContractCase.mjs` — hook mechanics, the settings levers, `CLAUDE_CODE_TMPDIR`, and what a redirected `Bash` does at its tool timeout — split because every case is a real `claude` run and the hang guard charges a file the SUM of its cases, so one file crossed its per-file deadline; see `tests/hangGuardConfig.mjs`, whose rule is that the answer is more files, not a later deadline): the undocumented CLI behaviours redirection rests on (`updatedInput` rewriting, `PostToolUse` carrying `tool_response`, `additionalContext` reaching the model, a `Bash(…)` deny still biting under bypass, neither `Glob` nor `Grep` being reachable, and — the whole of cc's cancellation channel — a tool timeout detaching the forwarder without aborting its request while an interrupt or a `TaskStop` on the detached task does abort it), asserted against the installed binary over the SAME `http` hook transport cc uses and through mechanical channels rather than the model's prose. `RUN_FUSE_LIFECYCLE=1` → `tests/fuse-*.real.test.mjs` (four files over the shared harness `tests/fuseGateCase.mjs`, split for the same reason and by the same rule — SIGKILLed at the deadline on some runs as one file, 82.2–90.2 s, → 29.3 s worst file, and run with `TEST_CONCURRENCY=1` because the four contend; see "The FUSE-union chroot's two suites" above). `RUN_DOCKER_SYSTEM=1` → `tests/systems-docker-boundary.real.test.mjs`: the reference provider INSIDE a container over `docker exec -i`. Every other Systems test reaches the provider on cc's own machine, which proves the protocol and **cannot** prove the redirection — a wrong-machine bug looks exactly like success when both machines are the same one. It also proves per-call cancellation (B1) and the output fence across the boundary, both witnessed from INSIDE the container, because cc's own return value cannot tell "was stopped" from "was abandoned and finished anyway": `ProviderShell#runOneShot` (`src/systems/providerShell.ts`) re-checks `signal?.aborted` after the exec has RETURNED and throws `cancelled()` ahead of reading `outputOverflowed`/`timedOut`, so the exec's own result is discarded. It is also the only place MUST 3 is exercised: `docker exec` children are reparented inside the container, so cc's kill of the local forwarder cannot reach them and the provider has to relay it — and the only place streaming is proved across a real machine boundary. None of the three runs in `npm test` or `gate:systems`; each needs something the repo does not ship.

**A remapped session can now run that family, and an unreachable API reds it fast**. `tests/cliContractCase.mjs` launches every CLI child through `cliEnv()`, which — and ONLY when `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` is set — drops `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`, `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` and `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` from the child, so it falls back to whatever first-party credentials the host has stored — and, on a host that has none, to a legible 401.
