Measured: no proxy-served model reachable here drives the assertions at all (**0 of 4** on each of two, one of them completing `is_error:false` having never called the tool), and an *unconditional* scrub takes a host authenticated solely by `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` from passing to **401** — so the gate on the base URL is what makes the helper a no-op on an ordinary host, by construction rather than by assumption. `ANTHROPIC_DEFAULT_HAIKU_MODEL` is deliberately left alone: at CLI **2.1.258**, the one version measured, an explicit `--model` is not subject to the alias remap, so it diverts only the session-title subquery's one cosmetic `unrecognized_model` line. That is a third-party invariant at a single version and **no test pins it**, so a release that started honouring the alias for an explicit `--model` would surface either as a misleading gated failure or not at all, while silently re-breaking a remapped reviewer — re-take it on a newer CLI. The accepted residual is a host whose ONLY route to a capable model is that proxy: it gets a legible 401 in place of a couple of hollow passes. Separately, ANY condition that makes the CLI return an `api_error` — a bad key and a model the endpoint does not serve were both staged — costs this family a **90 s hang-guard SIGKILL with the file reporting nothing and a process leaked** unless it is discriminated: the `--input-format=stream-json` child holds stdin open by design and never exits, so an exit watcher would fix nothing and the bounded waits simply sum past `FILE_KILL_MS`. `apiErrorReason()` is the discriminator, keyed on `api_error_status` and nothing else — `subtype` reads `"success"` on the error frame, and `is_error` reads true on the legitimate interrupt the detach suite's second case depends on. It rejects `runClaude`/`runClaudeEnv` and every pending and subsequent `claudeSession` waiter, and the detach suite's one poll over cc's own state (not over a frame) breaks on `cli.apiError`. `toolRegistry` is deliberately UNGUARDED: the one case it serves today resolves off the pre-API `system`/`init` frame and was measured passing truthfully under a 404 — guard a launch helper whose cases need the API to answer, leave unguarded one whose every case is pre-API. `tests/systems-cli-api-error.test.mjs` pins that discriminator against four `result` frames captured verbatim at CLI 2.1.258 (`tests/fixtures/cli-result-{healthy,404,401,interrupt}.json`), plus one fenced SYNTHETIC case: on all four captures `api_error_status != null` and `terminal_reason === 'api_error'` agree exactly, so only hand-built frames can pin WHICH key is read. It is the ONLY part of this family `npm test` or `gate:systems` executes.

**`gate:systems` leaves that family OFF, and that is a decision**. Enabling it needs no code change: `run()` in `tests/systems-gate.mjs` spreads `...process.env` into the child, so an exported `RUN_CLI_CONTRACT=1` reaches both rows and enables the family in each — measured, both rows green with the same ten passes. It stays off on what that costs. Measured on one 16-core host, **while the two rows still ran sequentially** (they are concurrent now, so re-take the pair rather than subtracting from a run taken today): `npm run gate:systems` 165 s → 216 s, and two full runs of the family — 273 s of billed CLI wall per gate, absorbed down to +51 s of gate wall because `tests/run.mjs` runs files concurrently. Every case launches a real `claude` child on a real prompt, so the spend is real: nine of the ten need the API to answer, and the tenth resolves off the pre-API `system`/`init` frame. On the arm measured here — `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` unset — it bills the host's own first-party account. The second of those two runs is not signal: the family loads no module under `src/`, so the provider variables the rows differ in reach nothing it loads — the positive control `tests/systems-provider-parity.test.mjs` loads `src/` modules under the same probe, among them `src/systems/registry.ts`, which owns both variable names. Nothing asserts that closure, so re-take the probe at the revisit. The two rows' per-case wall gapped 0.2–18 % of the smaller of each pair — widest the `Bash` deny case at 10.3 s against 8.8 s, with seven of the ten under 6 %. Two of the ten cases turn on the model ELECTING to act; one of those two has been observed red, and NEITHER rate is bounded — so enforcing today imports an off-diff red of unknown frequency into the gate this work is accepted against. `tests/systems-cli-contract.real.test.mjs` was also the row's longest file in that run, at 58 s against `FILE_KILL_MS` — on that host, at CLI 2.1.258 — so a slower release or a slow API can turn that red into a hang-guard kill rather than a named assertion. Run the family explicitly when a change touches it or the CLI behaviours it pins — `RUN_CLI_CONTRACT=1 node tests/run.mjs tests/systems-cli-*.real.test.mjs`, about a minute — and revisit this once those rates are bound; the shape to revisit is one row, not both.

**The path invariant** (`tests/systems-absolute-paths.test.mjs`). `requireAbsolute` (`src/systems/system.ts`) refuses a relative path on every path-taking operation and on `exec`'s `cwd` — the one way a `cwd` reaches the wire — hard, because a relative path resolves against wherever the far side runs — the provider's process cwd, or cc's own for the in-process system — so a read answers about a file nobody asked for and a write lands in a directory nobody chose while reporting success. **It lives on the CONTRACT and both implementations call it:** the invariant is a property of cc's callers, not of a transport, and guarding only the wire would leave production `local` — the system every ordinary project uses — covered by `gate:systems` alone and not by `npm test`. The suite runs the same operation table against a directly-constructed `LocalSystem` and a `ProviderSystem` for that reason. `LocalSystem.exec`/`readFile`/`realpath` are `async` purely so a guard violation REJECTS rather than throwing synchronously: the two implementations of one primitive cannot differ on whether a caller's `.catch()` sees it. The suite pins the guard per operation and pins the call site it was breached at: the conventions sweep, which resolved only the system and composed `path.join('', …)` from a listing row with no path. Its two sweep tests `process.chdir` into a scratch directory first — unguarded, the defect overwrites `CONVENTIONS.md` and prepends to `CLAUDE.md` in whatever directory the run started in, which for this suite is the repo itself.

**The remote-placement suites.** `tests/remoteSystem.mjs` binds the reference provider as a REGISTERED non-local system and hands back a temp dir on it to place trees under — deliberately OUTSIDE `PROJECTS_ROOT`, so any code that still composes a path from `projectsRoot()` lands where the tree is not and the assertion fails instead of passing by accident. Built on that: `tests/systems-remote-placement.test.mjs` (one test per third-branch site — the resolver hub, the listing union, create, delete, adopt, worktree placement), `tests/systems-remote-e2e.test.mjs` (the whole lifecycle over the real REST + MCP surfaces, asserting the instance registry stayed empty), `tests/systems-remote-refusals.test.mjs` (every named refusal, plus the plugin cases, which plant a decoy manifest at cc's own spelling of the remote path so a wrong-machine read is caught rather than assumed absent), `tests/systems-descendants-surfaced.test.mjs` (the `processGroupSignal` fallback reaching `project_bash` and the post-worktree hook), and `tests/systems-registration.test.mjs` (verify-by-handshake and the session-root placement check). Each disposes its live handles in `afterEach` (`disposeSystemHandles()`) — a provider process is a child, and the suite's leak guard is not optional. **`CC_CONFORMANCE_PROVIDER`** (a JSON argv array) points the conformance suite at a THIRD-PARTY provider with no test edits — the suite appends each `CAPABILITY_CONFIGS` entry's `flags` and, in the `remotes`/`remoteDescriptors` fixtures, `--remote`/`--mirror`/`--exclude`, and it builds its fixtures with node's own `fs`, so it verifies a provider reaching the same filesystem as the test process. The core fixtures are UNBOUND, so a provider serving only named targets must refuse them `ENOREMOTE` (`docs/systems-protocol.md` §8) and loses most of the battery until `CC_CONFORMANCE_REMOTE_ID` binds them — measured: an always-`remotes:true` provider went from a minority of rows to every row that is not skipped. Binding presupposes `--remote <that id>=<root>` on the provider argv, and only ONE half of that is guarded: cc refuses a `remoteId` client-side when the handshake says `remotes:false`, and `assertNegotiatedCapabilities` turns that into a handshake refusal naming the flag rather than a battery of bare value diffs. A DECLARED-BUT-DIFFERENT id is NOT guarded — it advertises `remotes:true`, clears the handshake, and reds the battery as value diffs whose actual side carries the provider's own `ENOREMOTE` naming the unknown target. A third-party run SKIPS every row gated on `IS_REFERENCE_PROVIDER` — the cc-side fixtures, which use a provider to assert what CC does rather than testing the provider, so the gate is provider IDENTITY, not provider shape. Each prints its own `skip` reason, and those reasons are the list. §10 of that document is the third-party contract.

Test **files run in parallel** (node:test `isolation:'process'` — one child process per file). The suite is isolation-safe: `bootServer` binds an ephemeral port (`listen(0)`) and `mkdtemp`s a unique home per server, so files never contend over ports or paths. Concurrency defaults to a cores-derived cap (see `resolveConcurrency` in `tests/run.mjs`). Override with `TEST_CONCURRENCY=<n>` (`1` = fully serial). Measured ~79 s serial → ~29 s at the default on an **8-core** device (that figure is unaffected by the cap moving from 4 to 8 — the expression yields 4 either way). **The ceiling is a tried and rejected setting, not an untried one:** on a 16-core box 16 slots measure *worse* than 8 on wall and much worse on CPU, and 8 is already near-saturated. The `resolveConcurrency` note in `tests/run.mjs` owns those measurements — do not restate them here. **The suite has no single-file floor, and keeping it that way is the point:** one file costing **48.9 s** of a **57.2 s** quiet 16-core run — `tests/idle-wake-ownership.test.mjs` before the split, measured 2026-08-26 — is a floor no number of concurrency slots can lower. It is split into the eight `tests/idle-wake-*.test.mjs` files over `tests/idleWakeCase.mjs` — its ~45 cases were mutually independent bounded wall-clock windows, so one file was charged their SUM and the family is now charged the MAX — taking the quiet suite to **42.5 s** with a largest member of **9.9 s**. `tests/hang-guard.test.mjs` had the same shape and was split the same way, 30.0 s → **8.5 s** (see "Regression suite" below). The gated `tests/fuse-lifecycle.real.test.mjs` was the third, over `tests/fuseGateCase.mjs`: its cost is real mount teardown per arm, so one file was charged the sum and four files are charged the max — 82.2–90.2 s as one file, SIGKILLed at the deadline on some runs, → **29.3 s** worst file at its documented `TEST_CONCURRENCY=1` (the one split whose members contend, so its total wall rose rather than fell) (it is opt-in and never in `npm test`'s ranking — see "The FUSE-union chroot's two suites" above). Past that point the suite is **aggregate-work-bound at concurrency 8, not single-file-bound**: the wall is several times the slowest file, so slots help again. Current quiet slowest-5 after the split: `idle-drain-settle` 16.6 s, `header-playbook-enforcement` 10.4 s, `plugins-supervisor` 9.9 s, `idle-wake-ownership` 9.9 s, `idle-wake-abort-qualifier` 9.8 s. **No entry on that line is condition-stable, the head included** — a deadline-bound file leads a quiet run and barely moves under starvation, while a CPU-bound one leads under load (at 72-way the same run's head is `playbook-enforce` 19.3 s, absent from the quiet five; `idle-drain-settle` drops to second at 17.6 s; and no `idle-wake-*` file reaches the top five at all, which is why that family's starved margin is quoted as a bound), so only compare verdict lines taken at the same load. `FILE_KILL_MS` in `tests/hangGuardConfig.mjs` owns the measurement and the trend. Running a whole **second** suite alongside does not move per-file walls either — that is what lets `gate:systems` run its two rows concurrently (below).

**Port ownership.** Picking a port by binding it and freeing it leaves a window in which another file's `bootServer()` `listen(0)` is handed the same port — after which an HTTP readiness probe is answered by that stranger and a destructive `POST /api/admin/restart` kills *another test file's process* mid-run. Two rules:

1. **Hold the socket when you can.** `tests/eaddrinuse-retry.test.mjs` and `tests/plugins-supervisor.test.mjs` bind `listen(0)` and never release until the test says so, which also makes the collision deterministic rather than lucky. **`tests/server-restart.test.mjs` deliberately does not** — it keeps bind-then-free `getFreePort()`. Converting it to hold-then-release was **measured and declined**, not deferred (16-core host, idle; 5 instrumented runs + forced-contention runs):
   - **Window A**, `getFreePort()` resolve → the child's banner: **241–278 ms** (median 268; spawn lag 0–2 ms, the rest is boot). Hold-then-release does **not** close it — the gate is the child's first `EADDRINUSE on port` stderr line, which `listenWithRetry` (`server.ts`) writes at the *start* of its 100 ms sleep, leaving a measured **111–114 ms** residual.
   - **Window B**, `scheduleRestart`'s `server.close()` (`src/restart.ts`, before the replacement spawns) → the replacement's banner: **254 ms**, and it is not test-side at all — a competing bind took the port on its **first attempt, 2 ms after** the parent's `restart: spawned replacement pid=` log. No test change narrows it. Tracked separately.
   - So the conversion narrows **window A — the larger of the two (median 268 ms vs B's 254) — by ~2.4×** (268 → 111–114 ms). **Two different ratios, do not conflate them:** the per-file aggregate falls only **~1.43×** (2 tests × each window: 536 + 508 = ~1044 ms → 224 + 508 = ~732 ms), because window B is untouched and carries 508 of the remaining 732 ms. That is **~0.022% → ~0.016% collision per suite run** (~261 ephemeral binds per run over ~30–60 s against 28232 free ports, `/proc/sys/net/ipv4/ip_local_port_range`) — that denominator is an **upper bound**: leaked fixture listeners held 12 of those ports on the measuring box. It does not move the rate. The kernel never hands out a port already in `LISTEN`, so an orphan holding *P* removes a candidate rather than causing an `EADDRINUSE`; 12 of 28232 shrinks the pool by 0.042%. The reason to decline is not that A is the smaller window — it is the larger — but that a production window **nearly as large** survives untouchable from a test while the machinery reads as "window closed". Declined on those numbers; do not re-open without new ones.
   - What makes ~0.022% acceptable is rule 2: forced contention on either window ends at `waitForBanner` rejecting (`child never printed listening banner #<n> for port <p>`), and in both forced runs the stranger received **zero** requests. The cost of a collision is this file timing out, never another file's process.
   - Dead alternative: spawning with `PORT=0` and reading the bound port back from the banner would close window A entirely, but `spawnReplacementAndExit` passes `env: process.env` (`src/restart.ts`), so the replacement inherits `PORT=0`, binds a *different* port, and destroys the same-port invariant.
2. **Never trust the port as identity.** Readiness on a spawned `server.ts` waits for that child's own stdout banner `code-conductor listening on http://<host>:<port>`, printed from the address it actually *bound* — never an HTTP probe a stranger answers just as happily. The rule lives once in **`tests/serverBanner.mjs`** (`waitForBanner(captured, port, {nth})`; the restart replacement inherits the same stdout, so its banner is occurrence #2) and is imported by every test that waits on a `server.ts` child. Additionally, never send a destructive request to a port whose owner you have not established: `server-restart.test.mjs` asserts the port is still `EADDRINUSE` immediately before each restart POST.

`tests/restart-port-identity.test.mjs` is the regression guard — a decoy `node:http` server answers the old probe while the real child cannot bind, and the test asserts that `waitForBanner` (the same import the restart tests use, which is what makes the guard cover them) rejects rather than reporting ready.

**Store isolation (`tests/safeStoreRoot.mjs`).** The sidecar stores resolve their on-disk root from `PROJECTS_ROOT`, falling back to a source-relative default (`src/../..`) that IS the real production `.code-conductor` when it's unset — so a test touching a store with `PROJECTS_ROOT` unset corrupts the live store. `run.mjs` pins the whole run to a throwaway temp root (`createSafeRoot()`) before any file forks — every child inherits it — and asserts via `assertStoreIsolated(orchStoreRoot())` that the store never resolves into the real workspace (aborting the run if it would). `bootServer` teardown restores `PROJECTS_ROOT`/`CLAUDE_PROJECTS_ROOT` to that safe root, never to unset, so out-of-window writes stay in the temp dir. `tests/store-isolation.test.mjs` is the regression guard.

**Git isolation (`pinGitConfig`, `tests/safeStoreRoot.mjs`).** The store root's sibling: `run.mjs` also writes a run-scoped `gitconfig` into the safe root and exports `GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL` at it before any file forks, so no repo the run creates reads the developer's `~/.gitconfig`. Its load-bearing key is `maintenance.auto=false`. Without it git spawns `git maintenance run --auto --quiet --detach` after ordinary write commands — **measured 765 spawns per `npm test`, 0 after** — and because that child is DETACHED it outlives its parent and repacks whenever it lands, including inside a `snapshotTree`/`assertTreeUnchanged` window, where it reds a systems row as a spurious tree diff (`.git/info/refs`, `objects/info/packs`, `multi-pack-index`, `pack-*.{idx,pack,rev}` appear; every loose object goes). `GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL`, **not** `GIT_CONFIG_COUNT/KEY/VALUE`: git clears the `GIT_CONFIG_*` family for commands run against another repository (`local_repo_env`), so the env form leaks on git's local transport — measured on one local push against a three-arm control: no config **1** spawn, `GIT_CONFIG_*` env form **1** (it does not bite), `GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL` **0**. `gc.auto=0` is a second key for the same outcome (each ALONE takes the spawn to 0 on git 2.55 here); it is kept for git versions that reach the repack through `git gc --auto` instead, which this host cannot check. Consequence: **a test needing a global git setting must add it to `GIT_CONFIG_BODY`**. `tests/git-maintenance-isolation.test.mjs` is the regression guard. **Every spawn figure here is a measurement from 2026-09-02 on git 2.55, not an invariant** — `pinGitConfig`'s comment carries the two-line command to re-take them.

**harness/playwright depth.** `harness/playwright/boot-orch.mjs`'s orchestrator cwd is derived from its own directory depth; the `debug/` → `harness/playwright/` move added a level and left it one short, breaking every `bootOrch()` caller with an opaque `child server exited before binding`. The constants now live in `harness/playwright/paths.mjs` — node builtins only, so it imports cleanly without the sibling `code-playwright` clone — and `tests/harness-playwright-paths.test.mjs` is the ungated regression guard. `boot-orch.mjs` consumes the same exports behind a fail-loud `existsSync` precondition.

**Forcing a fire-and-forget sidecar write to land late.** A store write fired without `await` (e.g. `markSessionBackend` at `Instance.spawn()`) can race a test's post-spawn read. Sample-then-assert is flaky by construction; the deterministic pattern (`tests/backend-spawn.test.mjs`, describe `'a sidecar write that lands after the spawn response'`) pre-creates `<dataFile>.lock` with a live PID (this test process's own) *before* triggering the write: `storeLock.ts` reclaims a held lock only when the owner PID is dead, so the write provably cannot have landed while the lock stands — a hard mutual-exclusion barrier, not a timing delay. Assert the miss first (proves the forcing engaged), release the lock, then read through a `waitFor`-based helper (`settledSessionBackend` in `tests/helpers.mjs`) rather than sampling again.

**Never assert across a machine-speed window.** `SPAWN_SETTLE_MS = 400` (`src/plugins/supervisor.ts`) bounds how fast a crash must be **observed**, not how fast a child boots — the latter is a property of the host. `tests/plugins-supervisor.test.mjs`'s settle-window test squatted a port and required a real `bash -lc 'node server.mjs'` to fail `listen()` and be reaped inside that window. Measured spawn→exit for that child: **p50 42 ms idle (max 46, 0/12 over 400 ms)** vs **p50 ≈ 420 ms at load1 ≈ 75 (min 219, max 813)** — so it went red **7/9 runs at load1 73.8–77.5** and never once idle. The rule: make each branch reachable **only** by construction, never by margin.
- *Inside the window* → the `_spawn` seam (`createSupervisor`). A fake child emitting its stderr + `exit` on `process.nextTick` is settled before any timer can run, so `raceSettle`'s first 20 ms tick always observes it — true on an arbitrarily starved box. Never a real process.
- *Outside the window* → `_settleMs: 0`, which makes "the window expired first" the only reachable branch, again with no competing clock. That test also cross-checks node's **actual** bind-failure line against the string the fakes emit, so the fixture cannot drift out of sync with reality.
- The **event-driven retry** (retry on the child's exit event instead of a window) was **measured and declined**, not deferred — same treatment as above. The retry heuristic is anti-correlated with its own risk: `allocatePort()`'s TOCTOU window widens under exactly the load that stops a 400 ms window from catching the resulting crash. But retrying after `start()` returned means rewriting a record `registry.ts` has already persisted via `store.recordStart(id, rec)`, i.e. a record-rewrite path for a ~0.02 %-class event (the collision rate computed under "Port ownership" above). Do not re-open without new numbers.
- The **readiness-cancellation** half is load-independent and pinned by a pair: a squatter that counts accepts and destroys each socket (so a probe cycle is countable and cannot leak), one test asserting accepts stop growing once the child is dead (`<= base + 1`, the one probe that can be in flight) and its **control** asserting they *do* grow over the same shared `PROBE_WINDOW` while the child lives (`> base + 1`). The two bounds are exact complements, so neither can pass vacuously.

**Second worked example of the same rule — a RATIO between two wall figures.** `tests/summary-attribution.test.mjs` compared per-file durations as `fast * 3 < medium` and `medium * 1.25 < slow`. A difference cancels the per-file spawn cost `C` to first order; a **ratio multiplies it**. `fast * 3 < medium` reduces to an absolute cap of `C < 600 ms` — not a spread tolerance — and `C` reaches ~770 ms at 72-way (above), so it went red **10/24 (42 %) at load1 72.8–74.4** on a completely correct reporter, while passing **30/30 at 24-way**. The construction that replaced it: **chain the fixtures' completions through a rendezvous** (`tests/fixtures/attribution/chain.mjs` — each waits for its predecessor's **process to exit**, spends its own designed increment, then publishes its own pid) and assert **differences against those increments** (`medium - fast >= 1200`, `slow - medium >= 900`, `sub - fast >= 550`). Because `reported_a - reported_b = (done_a - done_b) + (dequeue_b - dequeue_a)` and both terms are non-negative when `a` is listed before `b` in argv, every bound is a lower bound **no child-side cost** can violate. **State that claim precisely** — `done` is stamped in the runner's `test:complete` *handler*, so the identity also carries a parent-side delivery term, and one contiguous stall of the parent's event loop spanning both completions compresses the difference toward zero. The stall must be roughly the size of the increment it attacks (~600 / 850 / 1150 ms for the 550 / 900 / 1200 bounds); measured buffers over the floors are **+12…+53 ms quiet, +46…+539 ms at 72-way**. That branch is still margin-reachable and the test header says so — **the smallest increment, 550 ms, is what sets the breaking stall size, so do not shrink it without re-measuring.** It fails loud (red), never silently green, and the exposure is far smaller than the multiplied-`C` term it replaced. Two things are load-bearing and commented at their call sites: `TEST_CONCURRENCY=4` (fewer slots than chained fixtures deadlocks the chain, loudly) and the **descending** argv order `slow, medium, subsecond, fast`. Resolution claims get the same treatment — a `(0, 1000)` band on a difference was measured *outside* its own range (gap 1053 ms) on a correct reporter and is now a load-free lattice residue, `some(ms => ms % 100 !== 0)`. **Gate a rendezvous on the predecessor's process EXIT, not on its marker appearing** — a marker written at the end of a test body leaves that child's whole teardown+exit cost inside the difference, subtracting: measured at 72-way, marker-gating broke all three bounds (minima 1200/900/550 → **1050 / 811 / 460**, standalone red **13/24**, i.e. worse than the ratio it replaced), and the deficits tracked the *predecessor's* own reported figure monotonically. Exit-gating removes that term and makes the successor's exit cost a positive buffer: **24/24 green at load1 70.9–75.8**, minima **1312 / 953 / 596**.

**Third worked example — a fixed PRE-SIGNAL DELAY.** `tests/hang-guard-sweep.test.mjs`'s two interrupt legs (`SIGTERM …exits 143`, `SIGINT …exits 130`) signalled the nested runner a fixed **1200 ms** after spawn (`runGuard(…, { signalAfterMs: 1200, signal })`) and then read the fixture's `silent-orphan: holder pid=<n>` line back out of the captured stream. That is a margin claim about a **host property** — the case's own header said the delay was "well clear of process start-up on a starved box" — because the whole nested boot chain (runner boot + `node:test` dispatch + fixture boot + two `--import` preloads + `spawn`) has to fit inside it. Measured spawn→marker: **759 / 952 / 1585 ms** (min/med/max, n=8) **at 72-way** vs **87–115 ms idle**. Three ratios, none of them interchangeable: loaded boot vs idle boot is **13.8×** (1585 / 115), the window vs idle boot is **10.4×** (1200 / 115) — the headroom the test was written against — and the window vs *loaded* boot is only **0.76×** (1200 / 1585), which is the one that decides the case. Load moves the boot cost by an order of magnitude while the window stays fixed, so the margin inverts. Standalone `node tests/run.mjs tests/hang-guard-sweep.test.mjs` ×14, measured twice independently at 72-way: **6/14 runs (43 %) / 8/28 legs** at load1 75.1–76.6 / load5 53.0–63.5, and **5/14 runs (36 %) / 6/28 legs** at load1 73.9–75.5 / load5 70.9–72.6 — always on `the fixture never printed its holder pid` and never on a product assertion: on every red the runner still printed its interrupt banner, swept under the correct trigger label, and exited 130/143 with `signal === null`. **Test defect against a correct product — no `src/` and no `tests/run.mjs` change.**

The construction that replaced it: **`signalWhen`, a rendezvous on the fixture's own marker** (`runGuard`, `tests/hangGuardCase.mjs`) — one signal, sent synchronously in the same tick the captured stream first matches the regex, rather than after a duration. Five preconditions are *eliminated*, not widened. **E1** *the marker precedes the signal* (the assertion that failed): was a 1200 ms margin, now causal — the signal exists only because the line arrived. **E2** *the holder is alive when the sweep runs*: structural — the fixture writes the pid only after `spawn()` returned. **E3** *run.mjs's SIGINT/SIGTERM handlers are armed*: structural — registered by the `for (const [sig, code] of [['SIGINT', 130], ['SIGTERM', 143]])` loop in `tests/run.mjs`, which precedes that file's `const stream = run({` call, and only an already-dispatched file can emit the marker. **E4** *the holder-is-actually-dead poll executes*: it was silently skipped on every flake (`holderPidFrom` aborted first), so **coverage strictly increases**. **E5** *no armed timer outlives the case*: the interrupt path now arms no timer at all, leaving `bail` as the only one `runGuard` has to clear (the `stdoutPauseMs` pause-release is armed in that configuration only, and is `.unref()`d). `CC_TEST_DWELL_MS` stays at **4000**; nothing was widened. The firing pattern and the read-back are one `HOLDER_LINE` const in the test file (no `g` flag — a global regex carries `lastIndex` across `.test()` calls), so the signal cannot fire on a line the assertion then refuses. `runGuard` also **throws** on `signalWhen` together with `discardStdout` — the rendezvous reads the accumulator `discardStdout` throws away, so that pair would proceed to a state where the signal could never be sent; pinned by `runGuard refuses signalWhen together with discardStdout` in the sweep file, because demoting the throw to a warning otherwise leaves the suite green.

**Residual (R1), stated rather than hidden:** the interrupt must still land mid-run, so one contiguous **>~4000 ms** stall of the *outer* test process's event loop — between the pipe becoming readable and the `data` handler running — would miss it. Issuing the kill synchronously inside that handler is what keeps the window that tight, but it is a **description of the code, not an invariant**: a mutant deferring the kill via `setImmediate` survived with both legs green, because a late signal either still lands inside the fixture's dwell or the leg goes loud red down the healthy-end path. Do not add an assertion pinning same-tick delivery. Measured fixture-write→parent-observation latency at 72-way: **1–52 ms (n≈88, median ~12)**, a ~330× buffer on a term that was never the failing one, and the failure direction is a loud red (`code=1`, `trigger: run end`), never a silent green. **No fallback timer or absolute cap was added, deliberately:** "wait N ms then signal anyway" *is* `signalAfterMs` renamed; the two bounds are different objects (1200 ms was a **lower** bound the green path had to clear, while a cap is an **upper** bound whose exceedance reds at *any* value, since the assertions fail either way); and the run is already bounded three times over — the fixture's 4000 ms dwell, then `CC_TEST_RUN_CAP_MS` 12 000, then `hardTimeoutMs` 20 000. **Negative control** (point `HOLDER_LINE` at a pattern the fixture never prints): **3/3 runs red at 72-way**, each leg failing in **5.0–5.8 s** on the `no-holder-pid` assertion — exactly **one** AssertionError per leg, because `holderPidFrom` throws before any later assert runs, so the exit-code contract is violated (code 1, observed) as an *unreached* assertion rather than a second firing guard — with **no hang** — the construct holds the authority rather than relocating it. Because `HOLDER_LINE` is single-sourced, that same neutering also reds the run-end sweep case that only *reads* the pid (3 failures per run, not 2), which is the drift it exists to prevent. Post-fix, the real file went **0/20 red** at load1 73.2–74.5 / load5 65.8–70.7, with each interrupt leg completing in **0.99–1.44 s** — below the 1200 ms a fixed delay waits, because the signal lands when the marker does. Dead alternative: an **open-ended dwell** (`await new Promise(() => {})`, so only the interrupt can end the run) removes R1 and measured **0/8 red**, declined because it slows the *broken*-path failure from ~5 s to `FILE_KILL` 8000 ms and adds an env knob for no measured gain.

**This does not contradict the rule "gate a rendezvous on the predecessor's process EXIT, not on its marker appearing"** — that rule is scoped to **duration differences**, where marker-gating leaves the predecessor's teardown+exit cost inside the quantity under assertion. These legs assert no duration at all: only ordering, an exit status, and a liveness fact. And the rendezvous target here must be **alive** when the signal lands, so exit-gating would guarantee the interrupt arrived after the run was already over. Marker-gating is not the weaker choice here, it is the only admissible one.

**Negative assertions ("X did not happen") — barrier + `settle()`, never a fixed sleep.** `await new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, 200))` before asserting an absence is both flaky (too short on a starved box) and weak (vacuous on a fast one, because nothing forced the thing you are asserting the absence of to have had its chance). Instead: `waitFor` on an observable that is **causally downstream of the decision under test**, then `settle(turns = 3)` from `tests/helpers.mjs` (drains queued micro/macrotasks via `setImmediate`; load-independent). `settle()` is **not** a barrier on its own — it cannot wait for work that has not been scheduled yet, so a `settle()` with no barrier in front of it is exactly as vacuous as the sleep it replaced. Worked examples across `tests/idle-wake-defer.test.mjs` (which owns the fire-or-defer barriers): the barrier is the target's turn_end handler having run (`waitFor(() => target.status === 'idle')`), because that same handler both sets the status **and** makes the fire-or-defer decision — so a wrong delivery is already queued and `settle()` puts it in the ring where the assertion sees it.

**The overage usage seams default to a non-network tripwire, not the live fetcher.** `OverageResumeController.fetchUsage` and `UsageOverageMonitor.fetchUsage` default in production to the real `getAccountUsage`, so a fire-path test that forgets to inject makes a live `api.anthropic.com` request bounded by a 10 000 ms `AbortSignal` (`src/accountUsage.ts`) — racing the overage tests' own 10 000 ms `waitFor` budget. So `tests/overage-action.test.mjs`, `tests/overage-turn-guard.test.mjs` and `tests/overage-backend-exempt.test.mjs` install `installUsageSeamTripwire(instances)` in `beforeEach` (both seams → one counting stub returning `null`, the existing "can't confirm" branch) and call `assertUsageSeamInjected(seam)` in `afterEach`, after `shutdown()`/`rmrf()`. Both live in **`tests/overageUsageSeam.mjs`**. A **throw** inside the stub would not work as the guard — both call sites swallow it into `usage = null` — hence count-then-assert. `null` rather than an under-the-bar payload is deliberate: an un-injected test must not silently *work*. When the assertion fires, inject what that test actually means (`setResumeUsage(UNDER)`/`(OVER)`, or `async () => null`); never relax the assertion. **Each of the three files also carries its own usage-seam tripwire test calling `assertUsageSeamsInstalled(instances, seam)`** (`tests/overage-action.test.mjs` and its two siblings) — the wiring pin has to live in the same file as the wiring, because each `beforeEach` is independently editable and a sibling's assertion cannot see this one reverted (mutation-proven: without it, reverting either of the other two files' `beforeEach` to the live default survives the whole suite green). That helper compares each seam against `state.fn` by **identity before invoking anything** — if the tripwire is absent the seam may be the real `getAccountUsage`, and calling it to find out would make the request the guard exists to prevent. How an un-injected test then fails depends on its `resetsAt`, because `_reschedule` parks at `max(resetsAt, now + recheck)`: a **future** `resetsAt` (these files' usual `nowSec() + 3600`) parks ~1 h out, so the fail-open counter stalls at 1 and the body burns its full 10 s `waitFor`; a **past** `resetsAt` (the fail-open tests' `nowSec() - 100`, chosen so the counter *can* climb) fails open and lets the body pass, leaving the call count as the only detector. Both regimes are offline and deterministic.

**A negative assertion needs a positive control, or it decays silently.** "No stub arrived" proves a timer was cleared only if a stub *would otherwise* have arrived. Pair the negative with an identical test that omits the cancelling step and asserts the event **does** land in the same window, and single-source the window constants so the pair cannot drift (`HEARTBEAT_SECONDS` / the derived `OBSERVE_MS` in `tests/idle-wake-interval.test.mjs` — module-local to that file precisely because it is the one file holding both halves of the pair). Verified by mutation: with the production heartbeat patched never to fire, the negative goes **vacuously green** and only the control goes red. Where an assertion genuinely requires a real timer to fire, a wall-clock window is unavoidable without a fake clock (an `src/` change) — widen it for margin, keep the control, and if it still flakes change the assertion rather than the number.

**DOM-vs-null assertions (`tests/dom-assert.mjs`).** Never compare a DOM node against `null`/`undefined` with `assert.equal`/`strictEqual`/`deepEqual` — use `assertNull(node, label)` / `assertUndefined(node, label)`. On failure node serializes `err.actual` across the per-file child-process boundary, walking a live happy-dom element's circular `ownerDocument`/`Window` graph: the file stalls **33–120s** and then aborts, so **every later test in it silently never runs**. Supplying a message makes it *worse*, not better (3-arg measured slower than 2-arg at the same site — `err.actual` crosses the boundary regardless), so the rule is unconditional on arity. `deepStrictEqual` never terminates at all: it grows unboundedly until the OS kills it (`exit 137` on a smaller device; **27 GB RSS** on a 30 GB host, killed by hand at ~2 min before the kernel acted), and `--max-old-space-size` does **not** bound it — do not raise that flag to chase the symptom. Reproduce **only** under a hard address-space cap, which does bound it because it cuts below V8's own accounting: `( ulimit -v 2000000; node repro.mjs )` aborts in **0.9s** with `exit 134` (SIGABRT) and never passes 2 GB. Without it the process consumes the whole machine, not just its own test run. The helpers compare without handing the node to `assert` and throw an `AssertionError({message, stackStartFn})` carrying only a short `<tag#id.class>` summary, leaving `actual`/`expected` `undefined` — that is what keeps the cycle off the wire. Always pass a `label` naming the element and its source (`'copy button must not be inside <pre>'`): nothing enforces it (a missing one just yields `undefined — expected null, found …`), but it is the whole failure message.

**Enforcing the DOM-vs-null rule — two guards, non-overlapping ownership.** A sweep fixed ~50 occurrences but nothing stopped re-accumulation, and only *failing* assertions stall, so a green suite hides the pattern. Both guards are needed; each module's header says why the other cannot be deleted as redundant.

- **`tests/dom-assert-scan.mjs` (+ `tests/dom-assert-guard.test.mjs`) OWNS the invariant** *no equal-family assertion in `tests/` is written against a DOM-valued expression.* It is the only guard that fails at authoring time on a site that currently **passes** — a latent site is invisible at runtime, which is exactly how the sweep's occurrences accumulated.
- **`tests/dom-assert-tripwire.mjs` GUARDS THE PATH, for one shape only:** *a DOM node compared against `null`/`undefined` by a positive equal-family assertion never reaches assert's serializer.* Preloaded into every per-file child via the `execArgv: ['--import', …]` option on `run({…})` in `tests/run.mjs`; it wraps only the positive family (`equal`/`strictEqual`/`deepEqual`/`deepStrictEqual` — `not*` can only fail when the operand is already nullish) and throws an `AssertionError` carrying just the `<tag#id.class>` summary. It covers DOM-ness the scan is structurally blind to. **Not covered, and still stalling 33–120 s:** a node compared against a non-nullish value (`assert.equal(node, 5)`, `assert.equal(el.parentElement, otherNode)`) and a node nested inside a compared structure. Do not widen the tripwire, because intercepting any node operand breaks legitimate node-vs-node assertions that currently pass.

The shared predicate `isDomNode` and the `describeFound` formatter live in **`tests/domNode.mjs`, which must have ZERO imports**: `dom-assert.mjs` imports `node:assert/strict`, and instantiating assert's ESM namespace during the preload is what creates the hole below.

**The scan is comment-stripped and paren-balanced, not a line regex.** A line regex both over-matches prose (`tests/header-playbook-enforcement.test.mjs` names `assert.equal(node, null)` in English) and misses multi-line-formatted calls (`tests/notifications.test.mjs`). `stripNonCode` blanks comments, string bodies, template *text* (`${…}` stays live) and regex bodies; **line numbers stay valid because newlines are preserved**, not because the output is the same length. The argument splitter then walks a `()[]{}` stack. **No JS parser is available** — `typescript@7.0.2` is the Go-native `tsgo` shim (exports only `version`/`versionMajorMinor`), there is no acorn/babel/espree, and `CONVENTIONS.md` forbids adding one.

**Scan direction, and why there is no allow-list.** Enumerating every legitimate primitive-vs-null comparison in `tests/` is intractable — they take too many arbitrary shapes and share no primitive signal. The scanner instead keys on DOM-*producing* signals in **tail position** — so `root.querySelector('a').length` correctly does not match. Receiver scoping on the `DOC_PROPS` and `PICKS` signal sets is load-bearing, not decoration: an unscoped `.body` produced the only false positives measured (5, all HTTP response bodies), and an unscoped `.find` conflates `[...root.querySelectorAll('.x')].find(…)` with `list.body.find(…)`. There is deliberately **no escape hatch**: a genuine future false positive is fixed by narrowing a signal constant in one place, never by an inline ignore comment.

**Measured recall: 46 of the 50 pre-sweep sites, at zero false positives across every legitimate primitive-vs-null comparison in `tests/`** (unchanged after widening `isDocumentReceiver` to accept a `.document` tail and adding the destructuring arm). Exact counts drift as unrelated changes add nullish assertions, so they are not restated here — `tests/dom-assert-scan.mjs`'s header carries a MEASUREMENT RECORD with the numbers, the sha and the date; re-derive with `scanTestsDir('tests')`. The 4 residual occurrences are 3 distinct expressions — `conv.emptyNode`, `main.leadingAssistantWrap` (×2), `batch.leadingWrap` — whose DOM-ness lives in `public/` return values and is absent from the test syntax entirely. Two further shapes are **waived** known misses, named in the scanner's false-negative note: redundant parens around a whole call, and declare-empty-then-assign. **None of this is an oversight and must not be "fixed" with a name list**; the tripwire bounds them at runtime.

**The tripwire must use `createRequire(import.meta.url)('node:assert')`, never a static ESM import.** Measured: with `import assert from 'node:assert'` in the preload, a test file's `import { strictEqual } from 'node:assert'` **bypasses the patch** — the ESM namespace snapshot of the CJS named exports is taken when the preload instantiates it, before the patch lands. `createRequire` leaves the namespace uninstantiated, and all four styles (both modules × named/default) end up patched. Patch `assertObj.strict[k]` as well (`assert.strict` is `node:assert/strict`'s default export). Forward `arguments` (`orig.apply(this, arguments)`) — named parameters would turn `assert.equal(1)` into `assert.equal(1, undefined)` and lose `ERR_MISSING_ARGS`. If the preload itself throws, the child exits 1 before the body runs and `run.mjs`'s `test:fail` counter increments; it cannot silently no-op.

`tests/dom-assert-guard.test.mjs` holds T1–T11, each titled with the invariant it pins: tree-is-clean (T1), induced-violation recall across seven shapes (T2), comment/string immunity incl. the real prose comment (T3), `not*` never flagged (T4), historical-recall floor (T5), tripwire behaviour parity via two child processes over a 12-case table (T6), tripwire installed-and-fires via a node-*ish* plain object — never a live node, so an absent tripwire fails instantly instead of stalling (T7), `dom-assert.mjs` reads the shared predicate (T8), the predicate accepts a real happy-dom node (T9), all four import styles patched (T10), throwing-preload fails loudly (T11). **Cost:** scan ≈300 ms including its extra `node:test` child (~180 ms of blanking + matching over all of `tests/`); the tripwire's per-child cost is below the ±7 ms run-to-run noise floor; T6/T10/T11 add 4 child spawns ≈200 ms.

**ClaudeLauncher seam (in-process fake by default).** `Instance` never calls `child_process.spawn` directly — it spawns through an injected `ClaudeLauncher.launch({command,args,cwd,env})` (`src/claudeLauncher.ts`), threaded `createServer({claudeLauncher})` → `new InstanceManager({claudeLauncher})` → `Instance`. Production default is `RealClaudeLauncher` (a raw `child_process.spawn` — behavior byte-for-byte unchanged). `src/claudeLauncher.ts` also owns the two pure launch-resolution primitives — `resolveClaudeBin()` (splits `CLAUDE_BIN` into `{command,prefixArgs}`; **unset, empty and whitespace-only all default to `claude`** — the empty case is what stock docker ships, and a default firing only on `undefined` made every spawn and the boot probe `spawn('')`; test injection via `CLAUDE_BIN="node fake.mjs"`) and `resolveBackendLaunch(backendRecord, model, claudeBin)` — so one-shot spawners that need no `Instance` (`health.ts`'s boot probe, `summarize.ts`'s summary generation, `claudeShellEnv.ts`'s bundle-gen) can import just these without pulling in the 2000+-line `instances.ts`. **Backend-agnostic spawn:** `resolveBackendLaunch` computes only command + prefix (plus the backend's `env`) from the **backend record**; every caller then appends the same claude args (including `--model <model>`) uniformly. A blank `template` returns the resolved claude bin's `{command,prefixArgs}` unchanged; otherwise the template is whitespace-split with `{model}` substituted **inside each token** (so `--model={model}` works too), token 0 becoming the command and the rest the prefix — e.g. the built-in `ollama` row yields `['launch','claude','--model',<model>,'--yes','--']` (the model also rides in the forwarded `--model` — a harmless no-op duplicate). Because templates are DATA, there is no per-command env shim: a test pointing a backend at a fake binary just registers a row whose template names it. The helper owns the **null-model invariant** — it throws whenever the template is non-blank and no model resolved (unconditional on the template shape; see the create-time guard below), rather than emit `--model undefined` — so `Instance.spawn()` wraps the call in a try/catch only to add its own `_setStatus('crashed')` side effect before rethrowing. A backend needing a non-default host sets it as an `env` pair on its registry row (e.g. `OLLAMA_HOST`) rather than through any dedicated plumbing. Only `model` + `backend` thread `create()→_doCreate→Instance ctor→spawn()`; both appear in `summary()` (so the resume manifest carries them) and survive an in-place `/clear` renewal. On resume, `backend` is recovered from the `session-backends.js` marker; `model` is recovered from that same marker's tagged value for substitution-backend sessions (the jsonl `message.model` is bare — tag dropped by the inner CLI — so a jsonl-only recovery would relaunch the unpullable tagless name), falling back to the jsonl (`readLastSessionModel`) for Claude sessions and legacy model-unknown entries. There is **no** spawn-time reachability preflight — a bad backend or model fails at launch, and a subprocess on a substitution backend that terminates on its own emits a `system/launch_failed` event (`_handleExit`, gated by a `_killing` flag so a commanded kill isn't mislabeled). `stderr` on that event carries what the wrapper wrote **if it ran**; for a wrapper that never started there is none (`stderr: null`, `code: -2`) and the reason rides on the preceding `spawn_error` — that sub-case reaches `_handleExit` only via the terminal latch (see Instance lifecycle above). That event is therefore the only failure signal for a misconfigured backend. Four create-time guards still fire before any spawn: `422 BACKEND_MODEL_MISSING` when a substitution backend has no resolvable model (unconditional on the template shape — the model also rides in the forwarded claude args and drives the context-window env, so a template that omits `{model}` is no exception), `422 BACKEND_GONE` when the named backend isn't in the registry, whether it came in explicitly or from the session sidecar, `501 FUSE_LAUNCHER_UNRESOLVED` when the session is on a system and `resolveOnPath(resolveClaudeBin().command)` is empty — that path is the union's marking event and the CLI's only host pin, so it is a refusal rather than a degraded spawn; exempt on an `inProcess` launcher, which has neither — and `501 FUSE_BACKEND_UNRESOLVED` when the session is on a system and `resolveOnPath(resolveBackendLaunch(…).command)` is empty. The last is **diagnosis, not pin correctness**: that command is exec'd by `setpriv` before the CLI and runs unmarked throughout, so it needs no host pin (gate arm `R14L`) — but an unresolvable one dies inside the chroot naming nothing, and the spawn seam knows the token. It sits after the model is settled, because `resolveBackendLaunch` refuses a model-less substitution launch; same `inProcess` exemption, and for the `claude` backend the token is the one `FUSE_LAUNCHER_UNRESOLVED` already checked. `Instance.spawn()` re-checks the record and refuses a null one, so a row removed under a live instance crashes it visibly instead of falling into resolveBackendLaunch's identity branch and launching the real `claude` with a foreign model id. A live "Change model" (`Instance.setModel(model, backend)`) is **blocked** (`409 BACKEND_LOCKED`) whenever either side is a non-`claude` backend, including substitution↔substitution (the running endpoint is fixed at launch), with a "kill and respawn" message; only Claude↔Claude live switches proceed. **`bootServer()` defaults to `InProcessClaudeLauncher`** (`tests/inProcessLauncher.mjs`): the fake-claude engine runs on the event loop against in-memory `PassThrough` streams — **zero OS subprocesses per instance**. This is the forget-proof default (an Android phantom-process-killer defense: the app UID is killed above ~32 processes), so a normally-written test forks nothing. The in-process handle is a `FakeChildProcess` (EventEmitter + 3 PassThrough + `pid=null`); it defers engine start to `setImmediate`, gates `'exit'` on the readable-side `'end'` of stdout/stderr (so a trailing `turn_end` is never delivered after `'exit'`), and maps `kill('SIGKILL')` → `exit(null,'SIGKILL')`, `kill('SIGTERM')`/`stdin.end()` → graceful `exit(0,null)`. `pid=null` makes the manager's OS-`process.kill` sync-shutdown paths (`shutdownTempSync`/`shutdownForResumeSync`) a safe skip.

The fake-claude **scenario engine** lives once in `tests/fake-claude-engine.mjs` (`runFakeClaude({argv,env,cwd,stdin,stdout,stderr,onReaderReady})`): silent until first stdin message, auto-acks `control_request`s, emits canned events from `env.FAKE_CLAUDE_SCENARIO`, matches `control_response`s so scenarios can branch on allow/deny. `env.FAKE_CLAUDE_ARGV_DUMP` / `FAKE_CLAUDE_ENV_DUMP` capture the launch argv / computed child env; `FAKE_CLAUDE_TRANSCRIPT` records stdin. It reads everything from its params (not `process.*`), so the same engine drives both the in-process launcher and the thin subprocess entrypoint `tests/fake-claude.mjs`. Config flows through `env` (bootServer sets `FAKE_CLAUDE_SCENARIO`), and for the in-process path `CLAUDE_BIN` is a **bare sentinel** (`claude`, no script path) so `resolveClaudeBin`'s `prefixArgs` stay empty and the argv the engine sees equals the real CLI flag set.

**`bootServer({claudeLauncher})`** injects a custom launcher (e.g. `tests/backend-spawn.test.mjs`'s `ControllableLauncher`, whose child stays alive until the test triggers a spontaneous `crash()` or a `kill()`) to exercise post-launch crash paths. A test needing a **substitution backend** that reaches a real fake binary registers a registry row whose `template` names it (`addBackend`) — templates are data, so that is the injection seam; there is no per-command env shim.

**`bootServer({realProcess:true})`** opts a test back into a real `node fake-claude.mjs` subprocess (`RealClaudeLauncher`) — needed only where a test uses a real `.pid` / OS `process.kill(pid)` / the SIGKILL+reap sync-shutdown path: the `instances.test.mjs` crash+respawn test, and the two `shutdownTempSync` tests (`archive-sessions.test.mjs`, `restart-temp-cleanup.test.mjs`). Each opts in per-test (booting its own dedicated realProcess server) so the rest of its file stays in-process. `shutdownForResumeSync` is graceful (`stdin.end()`), so `resume-after-restart.test.mjs` stays fully in-process.

**Hand-editing `settings.json` in a test — do it BEFORE the first `appSettings` call under that root.** `appSettings` caches the parsed document keyed by `settingsPath()`, so the cache goes cold exactly once per `freshProjectsRoot()` and a direct `fs.writeFile` **after** any read or write through the module is never seen. A test that plants a malformed shape and then asserts on it therefore asserts against the pre-edit state — i.e. against nothing, silently green even with the code that handles the shape deleted. Measured: two `tests/systems-registry.test.mjs` cases written that way (a stored row shadowing a managed id; duplicate ids) passed against the unfixed code both times, and only started failing once the seed moved ahead of the first API call (`seedSettings` in that file). This is the same "it passes for the wrong reason" trap as the mutation checks elsewhere in this section — a test over a cached store must control when the cache is seeded.

**System-seam interception** (`tests/systems-seam.test.mjs`). Two escape hatches, two techniques, and the difference matters if you write another test like this. `fs.promises` is ONE shared object and src modules call `fs.readFile(…)` by property lookup, so wrapping its methods is visible to already-imported code (the same trick `tests/external-projects.test.mjs` uses to inject a stat failure). `node:child_process` is NOT: an ESM `import { spawn }` binds the function at instantiation, so patching `require('node:child_process').spawn` afterwards is invisible to a module that was already loaded — **measured, both directions**. The test therefore patches first and imports every src module DYNAMICALLY, and must not statically import any of them. Attribution is per call via `new Error().stack` (with `Error.stackTraceLimit` raised — the default 10 frames can cut the frame being looked for): a call is a leak unless a `systems/localSystem.ts` frame sits beneath it. The fixture is an ADOPTED project, so the project tree and cc's own paths occupy disjoint path spaces and "reached the project tree" is a pure prefix test.

**Compile cache** (`tests/compileCache.mjs`, wired once in `tests/run.mjs`'s pre-fork block). `NODE_COMPILE_CACHE` is pointed at `<repoRoot>/.compile-cache` (gitignored) before any child forks, so every test child — `npm test`, both `gate:systems` rows, every mutation iteration — reuses the V8 compile + type-stripping of the `helpers.mjs → server.ts → src/*.ts` graph instead of redoing it. Measured: importing `tests/helpers.mjs` in a bare child ~220 ms uncached against ~120 ms cached; a whole gate 166.9 s / 973.6 s CPU with the cache off against 143.4 s / 717.3 s with it on, back to back. **Shared and persistent** on purpose — a per-run directory pays the cold compile every time — and **per worktree**, because a shared `os.tmpdir()` location would let one checkout's size-bound wipe delete entries under a sibling's live run. Bounded by `COMPILE_CACHE_MAX_BYTES`, which wipes the directory (Node recreates it lazily) and prints a `compile-cache: reset` line; that is the only line it ever prints. Node's cache is **path-keyed and overwrites** (verified on v24.18.0), so an in-place mutation campaign replaces one entry per mutant rather than adding one. `CC_TEST_COMPILE_CACHE=0` is the opt-out; a **pre-set `NODE_COMPILE_CACHE` wins verbatim and is never walked or wiped** — we do not delete a directory we did not choose.

**Process-count guardrail** (`tests/run.mjs`): samples the peak concurrent `fake-claude.mjs` subprocess count (~100 ms) and fails the run if it exceeds a process-count budget `FAKE_CLAUDE_BUDGET` (`tests/run.mjs`) — catching a regression that flips the default back to subprocess or a new test that spawns real processes without `realProcess:true`. A clean default run peaks at ~2–3 (only the realProcess opt-in tests). **The count is scoped to this run's descendants** (`censusMatching`, `tests/procTree.mjs`): a `/proc/*/cmdline` match is narrowed by `CC_TEST_RUN_ID` in `/proc/<pid>/environ`, so concurrent suite runs on one box — a sibling worktree, or the two `gate:systems` rows — do not count each other's children. Measured before the fix: two concurrent runs, both `fail 0`, both exit 1 at peaks of 14 and 15 against a budget of 12. Only the cmdline matches get an environ read (the sampler ticks over every visible pid, so a read per pid would regress it), and the box-wide peak is printed as a second `guardrail:` line whenever it exceeds the counted one — `hasMarker` fails closed, so a counted figure collapsing against a high box-wide one is the guard going blind, not a clean run. Enforcement is skipped under `RUN_REAL_CLAUDE=1` and when `/proc` is unavailable. It reads `/proc` through `tests/procTree.mjs`, the suite's single `/proc` reader (one `snapshot()` per sampler tick answers every question the tick asks, so the answers cannot disagree mid-tick). (Use `/proc` reads only here — `pkill`/`lsof` misbehave on this host.)

### Suite hang guard

**The invariant: no test file may outlive its own report.** node:test emits a file's terminal `test:summary` **only when its child process exits**, so a file that finishes its tests but leaves the event loop open never reports and wedges `reporter.on('end')` in `tests/run.mjs` *forever*. That is not hypothetical: it has cost code-mutant's entire 300 s external ceiling for one leaked handle.

**The `timeout` option is not a defence.** `run({timeout})` reaches each child as `--test-timeout`, a **per-test** deadline on the test body's promise. It cancels the *test*, never the *process*. Measured shapes that it does not touch at all: a test that **passes** while leaking a handle; a test that **fails** before its cleanup line; a CPU busy-loop (the timer cannot run); an orphan grandchild holding inherited stdio after the child exits cleanly.

**`--test-force-exit` is a trap — do not add it.** It makes leaking children exit, but `process.exit()` in the child **truncates its pending report to the parent pipe**. Two whole-suite scans with it on disagreed by 31 tests; one silently emitted *nothing* for `tests/mcp-conductor-view.test.mjs` (~38 tests) while still printing `fail 0` and **exit 0**. A silent green is worse than a hang.

Three layers, all reading their deadlines from `tests/hangGuardConfig.mjs` so a guard can never protect a private copy of a number. Every deadline is env-overridable **only** so the `tests/hang-guard-*.test.mjs` suite can drive the real code path at millisecond scale:

| constant | env override | role |
|---|---|---|
| `FILE_KILL_MS` | `CC_TEST_FILE_KILL_MS` | parent: SIGKILL a per-file child that outlives it. Must stay **above** the per-test `timeout` in `run.mjs` so it cannot pre-empt a test node would still cancel and report. |
| `LEAK_GRACE_MS` | `CC_TEST_LEAK_GRACE_MS` | child: time from root-`after()` entry to declaring the loop leaked. |
| `ORPHAN_SWEEP_MS` | `CC_TEST_ORPHAN_SWEEP_MS` | parent: grace between every discovered file being *settled* and declaring the report stream stalled. `end` normally follows the last summary within ~4 ms, so this is nearly all margin; applied once, from the last file to settle. |
| `RUN_CAP_MS` | `CC_TEST_RUN_CAP_MS` | parent: absolute run ceiling. **Last-resort backstop only** — it makes an unbounded hang finite; the per-file deadlines above are what produce a timely verdict and what must fit inside an external harness's ceiling. Measured: a full run under 24-way CPU starvation (load ~30) takes **~157–170 s**, so a 240 s cap would leave only ~1.4× margin and go red on a merely-loaded box; the default is sized ~3.5× the starved figure. **The plan's stated property "below code-mutant's 300 s so we, not it, produce the verdict" is therefore NOT true of this constant and must not be re-asserted** — `harness/mutation/config.json`'s `timeoutMs` is 300 s. It is benign because the per-file deadlines (90 / 15 / 5 s) and the stream-stall check are what actually terminate a wedged run, all well inside 300 s. |

- **Layer B — `tests/handleLeakGuard.mjs`** (child side), preloaded into every per-file child via `execArgv: ['--import', …]` alongside the DOM tripwire. A `--import` preload evaluates before the test body, so its root `after()` hook is registered — and therefore runs — **first**, ahead of the file's own `after(() => ctx.close())`. It does not check anything there; it arms an **unref'd** `LEAK_GRACE_MS` timer. Clean file → the loop drains, the process exits, the timer never fires, zero happy-path cost (which is exactly why arming it ahead of the file's own teardown does not race that teardown). Leaking file → the timer gets a turn, which **is** the criterion; it writes the diagnostic with `fs.writeSync(2, …)` (synchronous, so it cannot be truncated the way `process.exit` truncates an async pipe write) and exits 1, which the parent surfaces as a file-level `test:fail`. Cost of a leak becomes `LEAK_GRACE_MS` instead of infinity.
- **Layer A — `tests/run.mjs`**: (1) a **completeness ledger** — every discovered file must emit its own `test:summary` (the per-file event carries `data.file`; the single run-level summary has `file === undefined`, which is how they are told apart). Anything discovered but never reported is **named and counted as a failure**, which closes the truncation class above whatever its cause. (2) A **per-file kill watchdog** mapping live direct children to the test file named as the **last** element of their argv, SIGKILLing the whole tree past `FILE_KILL_MS`. (3) A **stream-stall check** plus a **leaked-process sweep** (see below) on **four** triggers — stream stall, run cap, **normal run end**, and **SIGINT/SIGTERM**. The last two are: a leaked process only wedges the stream if it holds stdio inherited from the *runner*, and a plugin child holds pipes to the *test file's* child, so it wedged nothing and no trigger ever fired — measured, 21 orphans across 9 runs that all exited 0. (4) An **absolute run cap**. (5) An unconditional `hang-guard:` verdict line on green *and* red runs, carrying files-reported / killed / swept / stall / cap state and the slowest 5 files — the slowest figure is the standing evidence that `FILE_KILL_MS`'s margin is still real as the suite grows. Expect a **steep ranking with one culprit**, not a plateau; a flat top five means the metric regressed (see the next bullet). `FILE_KILL_MS` in `tests/hangGuardConfig.mjs` owns the measured figures, the margin trend across review rounds, and — the number that actually binds — the **broken-guard** cost; that comment is the reference, not this line, and the line also **says when `/proc` was unavailable**, because a blind watchdog reporting `0 killed` must not read as `clean`.
- **The verdict line's two events are different events, and the split is deliberate.** The **completeness ledger** is defined against `test:summary` (per-file carries `data.file`; the run-level one has `file === undefined`). The **per-file duration** comes from `test:complete`, filtered to the file-level test via `d.name === d.file`. They differ because per-file `test:summary` events are emitted in `files` order, so a file finishing ahead of an earlier-listed one has its summary **held** until that one reports — and a duration measured at summary time then charges it the earlier file's wall. Measured, same file, argv order swapped: `parser.test.mjs` reported 1172 ms vs 108 ms, and in the inflated case **ranked above** the file that actually spent the time. `test:complete` fires at real completion and is order-independent. It is preferred over `test:summary`'s own `duration_ms`, which is measured inside the child and so excludes spawn + import: dispatch→child-done keeps the figure comparable to `FILE_KILL_MS`, a process-lifetime deadline. Measured on a 16-core box at the default concurrency, `summary.duration_ms` runs **31–297 ms lower**, never higher, across 90 file observations — sampling **every 6th name of the sorted `tests/*.test.mjs` list (45 of 266 at the time), skipping the files that spawn nested runners of their own** — then two (`hang-guard`, `summary-attribution`), now six (the five `hang-guard-*` plus `summary-attribution`), so repeating this sample today skips six — in two runs (one idle, one under a concurrent mutation campaign) that agreed closely. **The range travels; the centre does not.** Median 219–234 ms / mean 179–181 ms describe *that* sample only: the excluded work *is* spawn + import, so the centre tracks the import weight of whichever files are picked — cheapest here ~31–35 ms, express+ws importers ~297 ms, and a lighter-weight subset of the suite medians near 87 ms. The earlier "consistently 25–50 ms" figure was ~5× low because it described only the cheapest files. `tests/summary-attribution.test.mjs` is the regression guard. It runs the real `tests/run.mjs` against fixtures under `tests/fixtures/attribution/` and asserts on the `hang-guard:` lines, covering three seams that fail against **different** wrong implementations — none is redundant. Test 1 overrides to **`TEST_CONCURRENCY=4`** (one slot per chained fixture — fewer deadlocks the chain, loudly); tests 2 and 3 keep the helper's default of 2:

| test | fixtures | fails against `test:summary` source | fails against last-**inner**-`test:complete` source | fails against a **whole-second** figure |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| charges each file its own wall | the four **chained** fixtures — `fast` 0 → `subsecond` +550 → `medium` +650 → `slow` +900 — argv **descending** (`slow, medium, subsecond, fast`), `slow` still **first** | yes — (1), (2) and (2b) all fire | no | yes — (5), unconditionally |
| a **killed** file keeps its figure and rank | `killed` (outlives `CC_TEST_FILE_KILL_MS=2000`), `fast` | yes — it emits no summary at all | yes — its one test never completes | no |
| post-test **teardown** is inside the figure | `teardown` (~1 ms test, ~900 ms root `after()`) | no | yes — reports ~29 ms instead of ~954 ms | no |

`subsecond` exists because its **+550 ms link is the smallest designed increment**, and therefore the resolution floor: assertion (2b) `sub - fast >= 550` is the only one that dies against a reporter which rounds away sub-second separations while preserving larger ones. Assertion (5) is `[slow, medium, sub, fast].some(ms => ms % 100 !== 0)` — a **lattice residue, carrying no load term at all**. It fires on any lattice whose quantum is a multiple of 100 ms (every figure becomes `≡ 0 (mod 100)`), which covers the whole-second, 500 ms and 100 ms cases — the last two being holes the superseded two-sided `0 < medium - subsecond < 1000` band provably let through. That band is **gone**: it was measured *outside* its own range (gap 1053 ms) on a **correct** reporter at load1 ~73, i.e. it was itself a machine-speed window. (4) `slow >= 2100` is a pure control — an absolute **lower** bound that load can only inflate. **Envelope, not totality:** a uniform rescale of every figure by a constant factor (e.g. ×1.5) passes every assertion here, violating no ordering, no designed separation and no resolution claim. A lattice whose quantum is **not** a multiple of 100 ms largely **escapes**: (5) fires only when *all four* figures happen to land on the 100 ms lattice, so a 50 ms quantum is caught with probability ~(1/2)^4 = **1 run in 16** — measured **0 kills in 16 runs**. State that as the escape it is; the residue's guarantee covers 100 ms-multiple quanta only.

Argv order and the concurrency are load-bearing: the ordering bug only fires for a file listed *after* one still running, and the **descending** argv order is what keeps the dequeue term non-negative. **NEVER A RATIO**, and that is the defect to avoid: a ratio between two wall figures *multiplies* the per-file spawn+import cost instead of cancelling it (`fast * 3 < medium` reduces to an absolute cap of `C < 600 ms`), and it went red 10/24 at 72-way on a correct reporter. Every bound is now a **difference against a designed chain increment** (1200 / 900 / 550), plus the one absolute **lower**-bound control; the fixture sleeps are no longer solved as a pair against a shared `C` budget, because no bound carries a `C` term. The threshold algebra that lived in the test's header is **deleted** — do not restore it, and do not reintroduce a ratio. See the ratio worked example above for the construction and both campaigns' figures. It follows the same two rules as the hang-guard regression suite below — `*.fixture.mjs` naming and a **deleted `NODE_TEST_CONTEXT`** in the child env. Do not move the duration back onto the `test:summary` handler, and do not narrow it to the last inner `test:complete`.
- **Layer C is deliberately empty.** There is no periodic sweep of "files that leak"; the guard is structural, fires on any future leak with no per-file opt-in, and needs no rule for a test author to remember.

Three non-obvious mechanics:

- **`killTree(process.pid)` kills the runner.** `tests/procTree.mjs` therefore exposes `killDescendants()` (never touches the root) and `killPids()` refuses `process.pid` and pid ≤ 1. At the run cap, use `killDescendants` — an earlier revision turned a reportable timeout into a bare `exit 137` with no verdict at all.
- **A leaked process keeps the report stream open. It is identified EXACTLY, by a run marker — do not re-introduce a heuristic.** A grandchild is reparented to init the instant its parent dies, so no `/proc` walk from the runner reaches it; and if its file completed inside one 100 ms sampler tick it was never recorded either (measured: a 33 ms file's orphan survived an entire run and leaked onto the box). FD-INODE MATCHING was tried and refuted — node spawns child stdio over *socketpairs*, whose two ends have **different inodes**, so our `/proc/<pid>/fd` link never equals the orphan's. That refutes inode **equality** only; peer resolution does exist via netlink `UNIX_DIAG_PEER` and is merely out of scope. The full note, with the measurement, lives at `sweepOrphans` in `tests/run.mjs` — read it there rather than trusting a summary. So the design splits the two concerns:
  - **Termination is guaranteed without identifying anything.** A file is *settled* once it has reported, been killed, or lost its child without reporting. Once every discovered file is settled and the stream *still* has not ended, it can teach us nothing further, so the runner **abandons the wait**, prints `STREAM STALLED`, and fails the run. This needs no `/proc` at all. The trigger is deliberately **not** "this file failed to report": the measured orphan shape is a file whose child exits *cleanly with every test passing*, so its summary arrives — a `reported`-gated check switches itself off in exactly the case it exists for, and the run then falls through to the absolute cap.
  - **Killing uses an exact identity, not a heuristic.** `tests/run.mjs` exports a run-unique **`CC_TEST_RUN_ID`** (an `mkdtemp` basename) before any child forks, so every descendant inherits it and `processesWithMarker()` finds it by reading `/proc/<pid>/environ`. That reflects the environment as of *exec*, so it survives `setsid`/`detached: true` (which escapes the process group), survives reparenting to init, needs nothing to have been sampled beforehand, and cannot match a stranger, a system daemon, a shell-pipeline sibling, or a *different concurrent run* of this suite. It replaced a process-group + `ppid == 1` + start-time heuristic, which was **unsafe**: a process group is a job boundary only under interactive job control, and under `sh -c`, CI, or an agent harness — how this runner is actually executed — the runner inherits its parent's group, so the group spans unrelated work. **`PROJECTS_ROOT` is not usable as the marker**: `bootServer` reassigns it per server to a path outside the run root, so a grandchild spawned mid-test would not carry it (measured). Two best-effort sources remain alongside it (live descendants; pids recorded per file), but the marker covers everything they miss. A **standalone** `node tests/foo.test.mjs` gets its marker from `markRun()` in `tests/safeStoreRoot.mjs` (`??=`, so an inherited one is never replaced — overwriting it would drop that child out of its own run's sweep); without it a single-file run sets none, which is the one mode in which a leaked process has no identity at all. **The run-end sweep runs before `removeSafeRoot`**, and that order is pinned rather than commented: `run.mjs` re-reads its own marker between the two, so moving the sweep past teardown (which is what leaves a live process with a `(deleted)` cwd) prints `RESIDUAL` and fails the run. The check **holds** the invariant rather than only reporting it — anything it finds already matched this run's marker, so it is reaped through the same `killPids` path before the root goes, and the run still goes red. **A `/proc` snapshot is not evidence of liveness**: each candidate is re-verified **live** via `hasMarker`, because `processesWithMarker`'s `readdirSync('/proc')` samples the pid list microseconds after the sweep's kills, so a pid early in a ~2700-pid walk is read inside its own death window — observed once at load 25 as a false `RESIDUAL` with `SWEPT` naming the same pid. `RESIDUAL_SETTLE_MS` (`tests/hangGuardConfig.mjs`) then caps how long a genuinely-signalled process is given to die. It owns the measured figures, the reason it is *not* the fix, and the reason it is a **measurement-informed choice rather than a guarantee** — no fixed bound covers a process held in D-state sleep or under swap/cgroup throttling. What makes that acceptable is the failure *direction*: exceeding it produces a visible red on an otherwise-clean run, never a silent leak, with `CC_TEST_RESIDUAL_SETTLE_MS` as the escape hatch. **SIGKILL of the runner is uncoverable by construction** — no in-process handler runs — which is the standing reason `tests/reapOrphans.mjs` exists.
  - **The stall check is gated on node having finished** (`nodeFinished`, set by node's single run-level `test:summary`, the one with `file === undefined`). Without that gate it false-positives on a healthy run with a **slow stdout consumer**: the ledger settles from source-stream events at push time, while the wait is on the composed reporter, whose `end` is deferred by backpressure from `reporter.pipe(process.stdout)`. Measured with ~4 MB of output and a paused reader — gate removed: `STREAM STALLED`, a SIGKILL sweep and **exit 1 at 1.8 s on a green run**; gate present: exit 0. `node tests/run.mjs | less` is enough to trigger it and raising `ORPHAN_SWEEP_MS` cannot help, since a pager pause is unbounded. The run-level summary is the exact discriminator: emitted at push time on a healthy run (~44 ms), **never** emitted in a genuine wedge (absent through 5 s). Note the volume threshold is real — at ~200 KB the runner still finishes in ~120 ms behind a paused reader, because the OS pipe plus libuv's queue absorb it and the closing `process.exit()` discards the rest, so a smaller fixture would pass against the broken code.
  - Measured outcomes, against what the sweep exists to prevent — a leaked process holding the report stream open until the absolute cap (`RUN_CAP_MS`, `tests/hangGuardConfig.mjs`) fires: a sub-tick leak swept, run ends ~1.7 s; a **detached** leak swept (the marker reaches it), ~1.7 s; a live grandchild swept, ~3.2 s.
- **The run cap timer is deliberately REF'D** (and cleared in a `finally`, on both paths). An unref'd cap cannot fire once the loop has otherwise drained — the "all children gone but the stream never emitted `end`" case, where the runner would exit *naturally* with code 0 and skip the verdict. **Disclosed gap:** no test discriminates ref'd from unref'd. Making it unref'd leaves the whole suite green, because inducing that state requires the loop to drain while the stream is still pending, and every way of holding the stream open also holds a handle. `tests/hang-guard-run-cap.test.mjs` pins the cap's *observable* behaviour instead (it fires, fails the run, prints the verdict, bounds the wall time) with a child still alive — a state an unref'd timer would also fire in. Ref'd is kept because it costs nothing and the failure it guards is a silent green.
- **`LEAK_GRACE_MS` is the real per-file teardown deadline, not `FILE_KILL_MS`.** It is 6× tighter (15 s vs 90 s) and is armed at the *first* root `after()` hook — which, because a `--import` preload registers before the file's own hooks, is before the file's own teardown starts. A file whose teardown legitimately exceeds it is failed by Layer B as a leak and the 90 s figure never gets a say; under the ~2.7× starved slowdown a file with ~6 s of quiet-box teardown would cross it. If a file starts awaiting many child exits in teardown, raise `LEAK_GRACE_MS`, not `FILE_KILL_MS`.

**`process._getActiveHandles()` is a private Node API** (present through **v24.18.0**, the version all of this was measured against). Layer B uses it for the **diagnostic only** — never as the pass/fail criterion — and degrades to a clear "culprits UNAVAILABLE" note if it is ever absent, because a guard whose own failure mode is a crash would just be a new hang class. The culprit filter is `_getActiveHandles()` minus stdio, kept only where `hasRef()` is true; note `hasRef` lives on the internal `_handle`, not the JS wrapper. Ref-awareness is what makes it usable: a naive `process.getActiveResourcesInfo()` check flags **207 of 267** files (unref'd timers, in-flight `FSReqCallback`s).

**Two different probes, two different numbers — keep both, because collapsing them deletes the fact that would re-open the Layer C decision.** The plan measured **12 of 267** files holding a ref'd handle *at the moment of child exit*, and that number was the entire justification for planning a 12-file sweep. Layer B asks a different question: *is the loop still open `LEAK_GRACE_MS` after teardown began?* By that predicate **0 of 273** files leak, measured 2026-08-24. **The census lineage, because 268 + 4 ≠ 273 and the gap is real, not an error:** 267 at the at-exit probe → **268** when `hang-guard.test.mjs` was added → **269** after an unrelated change added a file → **273** once that one file was split into five.
