Anchor any re-count to a dated re-run and the named changes above, not to the arithmetic. Layer B is preloaded into every per-file child, so a green whole-suite run *is* this probe. A re-run of the plan's own at-exit probe now flags **1** (`static.test.mjs`, a `Socket`). Both results are true: a handle that is closing *during* teardown is real at the exit snapshot and never wedged anything. Layer B's is the operationally meaningful one, which is why the sweep was dropped — see "Layer C is deliberately empty" above.

**Two refuted mechanisms, recorded because the obvious intuition is wrong both times.** (1) **`_getActiveHandles()` does not report timers at all** in v24 — a ref'd `setTimeout` keeps the process alive while being completely invisible to it. Layer B still catches a leaked timer, but via *the loop still being open* (the grace timer firing), not via the handle list; so an empty culprit list plus a live loop is reported as **either** a leaked ref'd timer **or** teardown work still in flight — pending fs ops and awaited child exits are *requests*, not handles, so they are equally invisible, and "await the child's actual exit in teardown" is a pattern this very document recommends. The guard does not assert which; only the loop-still-open criterion is certain. (2) Consequently **Layer B cannot pin "an idle heartbeat was cleared"** — and `src/idleSubscriptions.ts` `.unref()`s that interval anyway, so an uncleared one holds nothing open. Do not "restore" a coupling between `tests/idle-wake-interval.test.mjs`'s `timeoutMs` tests and the leak guard; see the honest-pinning comments at those sites.

**Writing a test so it cannot leak.** Release every fixture on the **fail** path too — `try/finally` or `afterEach`, never a cleanup line trailing the assertions (that is the exact leaked-timer shape). For a happy-dom `Window`, `window.happyDOM.abort()`; for a spawned child, await its actual exit.

**Register the teardown AT SPAWN TIME, not after the helper returns**. A `try/finally` in the caller cannot cover a helper that spawns and *then* awaits readiness: the handle does not reach the caller until after the wait, so a timeout there leaks a child the `finally` never learns about. `startAndSettle` in `tests/plugins-supervisor.test.mjs` is the pattern — `t.after(...)` on the line after `sup.start(...)`, before anything that can throw.

**A teardown that signals needs a LICENCE, and identity is the only safe one.** Once teardown is unconditional it also runs for tests using a fake/injected `spawn`, whose stand-in children carry synthetic pids (`900001 + n`) — and `sup.stop` signals a process *group*, so an unguarded hook calls `process.kill(-900001, …)`. The hazard is the **wrap**, not any one census: `pid_max` here is 4194304 and allocation has long since passed the synthetic band, so a literal `900001` is a *real* pid — an unrelated live process sat at **1089935** while this was written (2026-08-25; the live ceiling moved 3.99M → 4.10M over that interval, which is why the figure is dated and the property is what you should rely on). So that group belongs to a stranger. Guard with **`hasMarker(pid, marker)`** (`tests/procTree.mjs`) — `processesWithMarker`'s identity asked about one pid, failing closed on a vanished or unreadable `environ` — never a caller-supplied "this one is fake" flag, which relocates kill authority to the caller instead of closing the class. Match by identity only: **never** by name, process group, ppid, or start time.

**Regression suite:** five files — `tests/hang-guard-file-kill.test.mjs`, `-run-cap`, `-layer-b`, `-sweep`, `-stall` — over a shared harness in `tests/hangGuardCase.mjs` (`RUNNER`, `fixture()`, the squeezed deadlines, `FAST`, `redactTotals()`, `runGuard()`; not named `*.test.mjs`, so `discover()` ignores it). Each runs the **real** `tests/run.mjs` as a subprocess against `tests/fixtures/hang/*.fixture.mjs`, with the deadlines squeezed via the `CC_TEST_*` overrides.

**A fixture's diagnostics reach `runGuard` on the runner's STDOUT — never on its stderr — including a raw `writeSync(2, …)`.** `node:test` captures the per-file child's stderr, relays it as a `test:stderr` event, and `tests/run.mjs`'s `spec` reporter writes it out through `reporter.pipe(process.stdout)` in `tests/run.mjs`. Choosing fd 2 over `console.log` in a fixture buys freedom from in-child reporter reformatting, **not** a different outer stream (measured 5/5 with `silent-orphan.fixture.mjs`). `runGuard` drains both pipes into one `out`, so a case that only *reads* the text is unaffected; anything that *hooks* a stream is not — which is why the interrupt legs' `signalWhen` rendezvous is called from both `data` handlers, a stderr-only hook having silently never fired. The **stderr** call is consequently unexercised today — deleting it alone leaves the suite green — and is deliberately kept rather than pruned as dead code, because `run.mjs`'s *own* `hang-guard:` output is split across both handlers and **the writer call is what decides which**. Its `console.error` diagnostics land on the stderr handler (the SIGINT/SIGTERM banners, `SWEPT` under every trigger, `STREAM STALLED`, `NO REPORT`), so a case rendezvousing on one of those needs that call and nothing else; its `console.log` diagnostics land on the stdout handler instead — the verdict line (grep `tests/run.mjs` for `files reported,`), the `/proc` WARNING (`WARNING — /proc was unavailable`), `slowest files` (`slowest files (limit`). **The rule now has a standing test:** `the discarded-stdout shape carries stderr diagnostics and drops stdout ones` in `tests/hang-guard-run-cap.test.mjs` runs that file's `discardStdout` shape against `detached-orphan` and asserts `STREAM STALLED` and `SWEPT` *arrive* while the `console.log` verdict line does not — the non-vacuity pin for the slow-consumer case below it, which asserts the ABSENCE of the same two patterns (shared `STALL`/`SWEPT` consts) against a stderr-only accumulator. What moving a writer to `console.log` destroys is diagnostic honesty plus the narrow "printed but not punished" discrimination, **not** detection: that case still reds on its `code === 0` assertion, since `run.mjs` does `failed++` on both `streamStalled` and `sweptOrphans > 0`. "Capture stdout instead of discarding it" was measured and refused — with the 4000 ms pause in place and no discard the accumulator is *still* empty, because a failing run's `close` fires at ~1.8 s before the consumer ever resumes. Check the writer for the pattern you are matching rather than assuming a stream: `writeSync` appears nowhere in `run.mjs`, and assuming stderr is the original silent-no-signal failure.

**Why five files and not one**: each case's cost is its squeezed **deadline**, not work, so a single serial file was charged the *sum* of every case's deadline and five files are charged the *max*. Measured before → after: **30 183 ms → 8 493 ms quiet**, **35 299 ms → 9 369 ms starved**; with a guard deliberately regressed, **47 408 ms → 24 720 ms** worst file (1.90x → 3.64x against `FILE_KILL_MS`). Files rather than in-file concurrency, because `FILE_KILL_MS` is a *per-file process* deadline: a truncating SIGKILL now costs one file's diagnostics instead of all sixteen cases', which is the property the suite exists for. Concurrent nested runners cannot cross-kill because each `tests/run.mjs` overwrites `CC_TEST_RUN_ID` with its own `mkdtemp` basename before any fork and `processesWithMarker` anchors on a **NUL-terminated** needle — verified live: six distinct markers across simultaneously-running nested runners, and serial-vs-concurrent per-case outcomes identical over three rounds. Fixtures are named `*.fixture.mjs` so `discover()` (which globs `*.test.mjs`) never picks them up in a normal run — several hang by design — while an explicit argv path still runs them. **`NODE_TEST_CONTEXT` must be deleted from the child env**: a nested `run()` that sees it prints `run() is being called recursively within a test file` and silently runs *nothing*. Verified against the **pre-fix tree — before the five-file split landed** (2026-08-20): `leak-on-pass`, `leak-on-fail`, `busyloop` and `orphan-grandchild` all hang **forever** pre-fix, and `exit-before-report` exits **0** silently; only the `clean` control passes both ways.

**Reaping what the sweeps cannot (`tests/reapOrphans.mjs`).** A run SIGKILLed outright runs no
handler, so its children survive; and orphans predating the run-id marker are a fixed historical
residue. This is the one-off tool for both. **Not wired into `npm test`** — reaping is implicitly
destructive and nothing new accumulates once the sweeps are in place.

```
node tests/reapOrphans.mjs --dry-run                # print pid / marker / cmdline, kill nothing
node tests/reapOrphans.mjs                          # reap
node tests/reapOrphans.mjs --id cc-testrun-XXXXXX   # a SIGKILLed run, whose root still exists
```

`staleRunTargets(snap, rootExists)` is pure over its snapshot and its oracle. A pid is licensed iff
**all** hold: `snap.available` (a partial `/proc` read yields *nothing*, never everything); `pid > 1`
and not `process.pid`; its `environ` carries a both-ends-anchored `CC_TEST_RUN_ID` entry (`MARKER_RE`
in `tests/procTree.mjs` — one home for the anchoring); the captured id matches `RUN_ROOT_SHAPE`
(exported from `tests/safeStoreRoot.mjs`, not re-declared); and **`!rootExists(id)`** — the owning run
reached `removeSafeRoot`, so it is *provably* over. That last conjunct is what makes the tool safe to
run while a suite is in progress. `--id` swaps the oracle for `marker !== id`, so naming a run
narrows the list and a typo selects nothing. Kills go through `killPids`, which re-verifies each
pid's starttime identity immediately before signalling.

**`pkill -f 'node server.mjs'` is NOT safe as routine cleanup**, though it works as a one-off. A
*name* match hits the live children a running suite spawned — manufacturing failures that read as
real reds — and unrelated apps on a shared box. It is also what destroyed the `server.mjs` family's
evidence.

Stale `/tmp/cc-testrun-*` **directories** are closed by teardown, not by a reaper. `createSafeRoot()`
records every root it mints in a private set in `tests/safeStoreRoot.mjs`; `removeSafeRoot()` removes
one on `tests/run.mjs`'s normal completion path, and a `process.on('exit')` backstop removes whatever
is still registered on every other exit (`process.exit(128+signo)` from the signal handler, the
store-isolation `exit(1)`, an uncaught throw, or a standalone file run that never had a removal at
all). The ownership rule: **mint ⇒ own ⇒ remove at exit; inherit ⇒ never remove** — registration
lives in `createSafeRoot()`, not `ensureSafeStoreEnv()`, so a root taken from the inherited branch
can never enter the set, and a test file under `run.mjs` can never delete the whole run's store from
under its siblings. The property — a standalone run of any test file, and a full suite run, each leave
no `/tmp/cc-testrun-*` root behind — is pinned by tests in `tests/safeStoreRoot.test.mjs`, which also
pin the inherited-root case, the deletion gate, and the backstop edges below. The gate
(`_forTesting.validateRootForDeletion`) is a second check on a path already drawn from the set, never
a selector: it refuses a symlink *pointing at* a valid run root, which `assertSafeTestRunRoot` alone
would realpath into acceptance; it refuses a temp dir of the wrong shape; and because `lstat` runs
before the shape gate it reports an already-removed root as `ENOENT` — the one error class the
backstop can read as "already gone" — which the backstop then consumes silently rather than reporting
a cleanup failure for work the normal path already did. The ordering inside `removeSafeRoot` is
likewise pinned: the registry entry is dropped only after `rmrf` resolves, so a removal that FAILS
leaves the root registered for the backstop to retry. Reversed, the registry empties while the root is
still on disk and the backstop's emptiness guard returns early — a silent leak. The test induces that
failure with a `chmod` (a deterministic `EACCES`, not a class `fs.rm` retries) and so is uid-dependent:
it skips as root, where a `chmod` would not constrain removal, rather than passing vacuously.

**No directory reaper exists, and one cannot be made safe.** The oracle it would need does not exist:
`/proc/<pid>/environ` is the environment *as of exec*, and `tests/run.mjs` sets `CC_TEST_RUN_ID` after
its own exec — so the process that **owns** a run root is invisible to `processesWithMarker`, and only
its children carry the marker. A reaper keyed on "no live process carries this marker ⇒ orphaned" —
the dual of `staleRunTargets`' licence — would therefore delete a **live** run's root during any
moment no marked child happens to be running. The only other available oracle is mtime age — the
name-plus-age heuristic this suite deliberately does not carry. So `reapOrphans.mjs` stays
process-only. SIGKILL remains uncovered (no in-process handler runs), which leaks a throwaway
directory only.

The subagent-aware idle wake deferral (`IdleSubscriptionHub._onTurnEnd` gating on `activeAgentTaskCount` **and** `taskNotificationPending`) is covered by `tests/idle-subagent-defer.test.mjs` (two layers: hub-gate tests with injected fake instances driving `manager.emit('event', turn_end)`, and Instance-lifecycle tests feeding captured-shape raw stdout lines to a real unspawned `Instance._handleStdoutLine` — sync-delivered result clears, attach-batch clears, nested tool_result does NOT clear, a queued notification survives turn_end until the re-invocation turn start, an idle notification never sets) and end-to-end in `tests/idle-wake-defer.test.mjs` via four fixtures — `scenario-bg-task-hang.json` (turn_end while a subagent is live, never completes → wake deferred), `scenario-bg-task-complete.json` (subagent completes *between* turns + follow-up turn_end → single delivery), `scenario-bg-task-midturn-complete.json` (mid-turn `task_notification` with no tool_result after it → wake deferred to the re-invocation turn's turn_end, single delivery), and `scenario-bg-task-midturn-consumed.json` (mid-turn `task_notification` followed by a top-level tool_result → consumed in-turn, wake fires at that same turn_end — regression for the indefinite-defer hang).

The idle task-drain settle path is covered by `tests/idle-drain-settle.test.mjs` (sets `ORCH_IDLE_DRAIN_SETTLE_MS=400` before a dynamic import — the settle length is a module-load-time constant). Three layers: hub-gate tests (fakes extended with `ring.nextSeq` + `idleWindowDirty` — orphan drain wakes once and stops the heartbeat; post-arm seq advance and pre-arm dirty window both refuse the early wake; fire-time drops on status flip / removal / proc replacement; re-arm resets the freeze baseline; turn_end/purge cancel a pending settle, and `disarmSilently` does so only when the disarmed caller was the target's LAST watcher — with two owners the settle survives and still delivers to the other — while a HEARTBEAT deliberately cancels nothing; `wasConsumed` stays false on a settle-fire), Instance-lifecycle tests for the `_idleWindowDirty` edges, and two end-to-end fixtures — `scenario-bg-task-idle-drain.json` (turn_end deferred on a live bg task, then `task_updated`+`task_notification` at idle and silence → the settle delivers the folded wake, exactly once, with no second turn_end ever) and `scenario-bg-task-idle-reinvoke.json` (same drain followed by the re-invocation's `init`/`status`/`message_start`/`turn_end` at 150 ms spacing, so the 400 ms settle deadline falls inside the pre-`message_start` silence → no early wake, single wake at the re-invocation `turn_end`).

`tests/instance-liveness.test.mjs` covers `InstanceManager.isSessionLive`. Mostly unit-level, no server: bare `InstanceManager` plus prototype-only `Instance` stand-ins pin the THREE completeness windows bare `liveForSession` would miss — a resume in flight via `_resumingPublicIds` (live with no `byId` entry at all), a prune's kill→relaunch window via `rotationPending` (live with `proc` null), and rewindToUserMessage's/`InstanceManager.respawn`'s identical window via `_relaunching` — alongside the ordinary proc-attached and genuinely-dead cases. The per-instance half of that predicate is `isLiveOrComingUp(inst)` (`src/instances.ts`), shared with the resume reclaim so the two cannot drift; `isSessionLive` applies it with **`.some()` over every instance answering to the session**, never `anyForSession`'s first match — an exited instance the registry still holds must not SHADOW a live one, which is both a real defect (the gate read `live:false` while a process ran) and what makes the reclaim safe to run behind the guard. `tests/mcp-resume-reclaim.test.mjs` pins that half. A `describe` block boots a real server (fake-claude launcher, no real subprocess) to prove `rewindToUserMessage`/`respawn` actually SET `_relaunching` at the right moments (per-path, via a `launch()` monkey-patch that captures `isSessionLive` at the instant it is invoked — a synchronous call-then-check trick does not work here, since neither path has a genuine multi-tick async gap for a plain worker) and CLEAR it once the relaunch lands, so a worker killed afterward with no further relaunch pending reads not-live — the dangerous direction: a flag that outlives its window would read a dead worker live forever, wedging a `workers:"one"` slot and satisfying `needs …@live` permanently. `tests/playbook-liveness.test.mjs` covers the gate level: a hand-seeded ledger file plus a stub `InstanceManagerLike` (`isSessionLive` is the only liveness input it exposes) drives `gate.check()`/`gate.readProjection()` directly — a `needs:@live` entry is satisfied or refused purely from the stub's `isSessionLive`, independent of what the ledger's own `retire`/`spawn` history says, and a `workers:"one"` slot seeded with no `retire` event anywhere reads free the moment the stub reports the occupant not-live, with **no boot-time write**: every allowed-decision case asserts the ledger file is byte-identical afterwards, which is what kills a reintroduced repair writer even if it computes the right answer. `tests/playbook-enforce.test.mjs`'s `setup({seedLedger})` option writes a hand-authored ledger (a complete relay run with no `retire` event, under sessionIds that never existed in this test process) **between** `bootServer()` and the conductor spawn — the conductor's first status tick reaches `ensureLoaded()` before `setup()` returns, so seeding any later would fold against a bare projection — and asserts a fresh `workers:"one"` spawn into that stage succeeds with no `retire` event written for the orphaned row at all, by construction rather than by a boot repair.

Opt-in real-claude smoke (`smoke.real.test.mjs`, `RUN_REAL_CLAUDE=1`): spawns the actual CLI, asserts `system/init` + ≥1 `text_delta` + non-error `turn_end`. Cleans the session jsonl on exit.

`prune.real.test.mjs` (same `RUN_REAL_CLAUDE=1` gate) is the regression test for Prune's harness-state invariants — it cannot be replaced by a fake, because what it checks is how the real CLI reconstructs state from a transcript. It reads a file, prunes, resumes, asserts the Edit is refused with the CLI's own "File has not been read yet", then re-Reads and asserts the Edit applies. **Pinned to `claude-haiku-4-5`**: the read-before-edit guard is only enforced for a fixed model set, so on a newer model the test would pass vacuously. It also has to point `CLAUDE_PROJECTS_ROOT` at the real `~/.claude/projects` after `bootServer` — HOME is not overridden for the child, so the real binary writes its transcript there while the temp store would otherwise be read server-side. (The other real-claude tests only inspect the event stream, so they never hit this.)

The plugin system tests against **`tests/fixtures/fake-plugin/`** — a dependency-free `node:http` child (health, relative-asset HTML, streaming `/echo`, hand-rolled WS echo, MCP endpoint with `echo`/`sleep`/`fail`/`transport-bug` tools pinning both halves of the child contract) plus `crash.mjs`/`slow-ready.mjs`/`forker.mjs` for the supervisor edge cases; `tests/plugin-helpers.mjs` clones it into temp projects roots. `bootServer` exposes `pluginHost` (and `pluginLibrary`) and stops plugin children on close. `tests/plugins-library.test.mjs` unit-tests `createPluginLibrary()` directly (mirrors `plugins-registry.test.mjs`'s direct-construction style) with `_cloneImpl`/`_runHookImpl` test-only overrides standing in for the real `git clone` child process and postClone/postPull commands — no network needed; its `update()` pull-success test uses a small real local git fixture (a bare "remote" + a seed clone that pushes a second commit, mirroring `plugins-registry.test.mjs`'s own local-git-fixture `git()` helper) so the real `runGit`-based `--ff-only` pull is exercised end-to-end. `tests/plugins-api.test.mjs` covers the `/api/plugins/library*` HTTP surface for listing + validation-error paths (invalid scheme, already-installed, not-installed) without ever invoking a real clone or pull.

### Reproducing CPU starvation

Several deadlines and flake rates in this repo are quoted "under 24-way CPU starvation"
(`FILE_KILL_MS` in `tests/hangGuardConfig.mjs`; `tests/summary-attribution.test.mjs`,
`tests/renew-session.test.mjs`, the `tests/idle-wake-*.test.mjs` family). This is how that condition is
produced. Use the **self-limiting** form:

```bash
N=24; SECS=600          # 24 = the DEFAULT width; the attribution figures are at 72 — see below
for i in $(seq $N); do timeout --signal=KILL "$SECS" sh -c 'while :; do :; done' & done
trap 'kill $(jobs -p) 2>/dev/null' EXIT INT TERM
wait
```

- **`timeout --signal=KILL` is the load-bearing half.** The `trap` dies with the shell; `timeout`
  does not. A worker killed mid-campaign cannot leave 100%-CPU spinners behind, which a bare
  `trap`-only version does — and a leaked spinner is far more expensive than a leaked idle timer.
- **The agent shell here is zsh, where bare `kill $pids` silently leaks spinners.** When killing by
  hand rather than letting `timeout` do it, use `xargs` with an anchored `pgrep`.
- **`N` is a starvation WIDTH, not a core count.** `FILE_KILL_MS` (`tests/hangGuardConfig.mjs`)
  and the test comments above are quoted at **24-way**, which is why the example is 24;
  `tests/summary-attribution.test.mjs`'s figures are quoted at **72-way**. A run at a different `N` is a
  different measurement and must say which. (Do not confuse this with the *8-core* / *16-core* device figures elsewhere in this
  section: those are hardware, not induced load.)
- `SECS` bounds the whole campaign, and is what makes the recipe self-limiting.
- **A 24-way pass does not clear a load-correlated flake.** 24-way is the default width, not the
  top of the range a defect can live in.
  `tests/summary-attribution.test.mjs` was measured **2026-08-26** on a 16-core host (dated to the
  day, not pinned to a build): **0/30 red at 24-way (load1 19.5–25.0)** and **10/24 red at 72-way
  (load1 72.8–74.4)**, with **0 orphans either side of every campaign**. Quote the width with the
  result, and when a reported reproduction was at a wider N, verify at that N — not at 24.
- **The quantity that scales is per-file spawn+import cost `C`** (fork+exec+node boot+import, which
  `run.mjs`'s `done - dequeue` figure includes). Measured maxima: **`C` ≈ 320 ms at 24-way** vs
  **`C` ≈ 770 ms at 72-way** — ~2.4–3.2x. Any test bound that tolerates `C` must be re-checked
  against the 72-way envelope, not the 24-way one.
